4-manifolds with a given boundary
Anthony Conway, Daniel Kasprowski

Abstract
. This paper studies the homotopy and homeomorphism classifications of 4 4 -manifolds with boundary. Given 4 4 -manifolds X 0 X_{0} and X 1 X_{1} with fundamental group π \pi , we consider the problem of extending a homotopy equivalence h : ∂ X 0 → ∂ X 1 h\colon\partial X_{0}\to\partial X_{1} to a homotopy equivalence X 0 → X 1 X_{0}\to\penalty\ X_{1} . We solve this problem in broad settings for a class of groups that includes free groups, finite cyclic groups, finite dihedral groups, solvable Baumslag-Solitar groups, and many 3 3 -manifold groups. When the fundamental group is additionally assumed to be good, we use surgery theory to list situations when a homeomorphism h : ∂ X 0 → ∂ X 1 h\colon\partial X_{0}\to\partial X_{1} extends to a homeomorphism X 0 → X 1 X_{0}\to X_{1} . The outcome recovers results of Boyer in the simply-connected case and work of the first author and Powell when π ≅ ℤ \pi\cong\mathbb{Z} and the ∂ X i \partial X_{i} have torsion Alexander module.



1.
Introduction

Known homeomorphism classifications of closed orientable topological
4
-manifolds include the case where the fundamental group is trivial
[
Fre82
]
, finite cyclic
[
HK88
]
, infinite cyclic
[
FQ90
]
, and a solvable Baumslag-Solitar group
BS(1,n)

[
HKT09
]
.
When the boundary is nonempty, a complete classification is known for simply-connected 4-manifolds
[
Boy86
,
Boy93
]
and for
4
-manifolds with
\pi_{1}\cong\mathbb{Z}
and certain restrictions on the boundary
[
CP23
,
CPP25
]
.

A little more is known concerning the problem of deciding when two closed
4
-manifolds are homotopy equivalent since one need not assume the fundamental group be good.
The earliest results go back to Whitehead
[
Whi49b
]
and Wall
[
Wal67
]
and now include several classes of finite groups
[
HK88
,
Bau88
,
KPR24
,
KNR22
]
as well as
\operatorname{PD}_{3}
-groups
[
HKPR25
]
.
For manifolds with boundary, there are no results besides those for which the homeomorphism classification is known.

This article achieves the homotopy classification of
4
-manifolds with a given boundary for large classes of groups under the assumption that the inclusion induced map
\pi_{1}(\partial X)\to\pi_{1}(X)
is surjective (Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
).
Surgery theory is then used to derive the homeomorphism classification in several cases (Theorems
1.6
and
1.8
).
Our results hold more generally for
4
-dimensional Poincaré pairs, which are pairs
(X,Y)
that are homotopy equivalent to finite CW pairs and for which there are classes
[X]\in H_{4}(X,Y)
and
[Y]\in H_{3}(Y)
such that the cap products with
[X]
and
[Y]
induce Poincaré duality isomorphisms (see Definition
2.1
).
During this introduction however, we restrict ourselves to manifolds for simplicity.
We work in the topological category.
Manifolds are assumed to be compact, path-connected and oriented.

1.1.
The homotopy classification of
4
-manifolds with boundary.

Given
4
-manifolds
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
with isomorphic fundamental groups, connected boundaries and
\iota_{j}\colon\pi_{1}(\partial X_{j})\to\pi_{1}(X_{j})
surjective for
j=0,1
, we aim to find necessary and sufficient conditions for a degree one homotopy equivalence
h\colon\partial X_{0}\to\partial X_{1}
to extend to a homotopy equivalence
X_{0}\to X_{1}
.

For such an extension to exist, it is necessary that there be an isomorphism
u\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\pi_{1}(X_{1})
with
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
and, if such an isomorphism exists, then it is unique.
Henceforth when such an isomorphism exists, we use it to identify
\pi_{1}(X_{0})
with
\pi:=\pi_{1}(X_{1})
.
Another necessary condition can be formulated in terms of the
relative equivariant intersection form

\lambda_{X_{i}}^{\partial}\colon H_{2}(X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H_{2}(X_{i},\partial X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi].

Namely we say that
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-isomorphisms

\displaystyle F\colon H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle G\colon H_{2}(X_{0},\partial X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(X_{1},\partial X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

are
compatible
with the homotopy equivalence
h
if they fit into the commutative diagram

\textstyle{H_{2}(\partial X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0},\partial X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G}
\textstyle{H_{1}(\partial X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(\partial X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{1},\partial X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{1}(\partial X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}

and intertwine
\lambda_{X_{0}}^{\partial}
and
\lambda_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
, meaning that for all
x\in H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and all
y\in H_{2}(X_{0},\partial X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
,

\lambda_{X_{1}}^{\partial}(F(x),G(y))=\lambda_{X_{0}}^{\partial}(x,y).

We call
(F,G,h)
a
compatible triple.

A rapid verification shows that the existence of a compatible triple is a necessary condition for
h
to extend to a homotopy equivalence.

We describe our solution to the problem of extending
h\colon\partial X_{0}\to\partial X_{1}
in increasing order of complexity, starting with the case where
H_{1}(\partial X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}:=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(H_{1}(\partial X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
vanishes.
When
\pi
is free abelian, this is equivalent to

H_{1}(\partial X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
being
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-torsion.

Our first result holds for some classes of groups
\pi
that are either finite or of dimension at most
3
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\pi
be a group that is either finite abelian with at most
2
generators, or finite dihedral, or a surface group, or a solvable Baumslag–Solitar group, or a torsion-free
3
-manifold group,
let
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
be
4
-manifolds with
\pi_{1}(X_{j})\cong\pi
and
\iota_{j}\colon\pi_{1}(\partial X_{j})\to\pi_{1}(X_{j})
surjective for
j=0,1
.
Fix a degree one homotopy equivalence
h\colon\partial X_{0}\to\partial X_{1}
.

When
H_{1}(\partial X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}=0
, the following assertions are equivalent:

•

The homotopy equivalence
h
extends to a homotopy equivalence
X_{0}\to X_{1}
.

•

There exists an isomorphism
u\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\pi_{1}(X_{1})
with
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
and a compatible triple
(F,G,h)
with

F_{*}(k_{X_{0},\partial X_{0}})=h^{*}(k_{X_{1},\partial X_{1}})\in H^{3}(B\pi,\partial X_{0};\pi_{2}(X_{1})).

When the group is infinite,
the condition involving the relative
k
-invariants can be omitted.

Additionally, given a
k
-invariant preserving compatible triple
(F,G,h)
as above, the homotopy equivalence
X_{0}\to X_{1}
extending
h
can be chosen to induce
F
and
G
on
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-homology.

Here, a
3
-manifold group
refers to the fundamental group of a closed
3
-manifold, and
k_{X_{i},\partial X_{i}}
denotes the
relative
k
-invariant
of
(X_{i},\partial X_{i})
for
i=0,1
.
We refer to
[
CK25
]
for further details on the definition of
k_{X_{i},\partial X_{i}}
: in this article, only its properties are needed, and these are collected in Section
6
.
We nevertheless mention that, essentially,
k_{X_{i},\partial X_{i}}
is the
obstruction to extending the map
\partial X_{i}\to X_{i}\to P_{2}(X_{i})
to a section
B\pi\to P_{2}(X_{i})
of the fibration
P_{2}(X_{i})\to B\pi
.
Here,
P_{2}(X_{i})
denotes the Postnikov
2
-type of
X_{i}
.

Remark 1.2
.

If
h
is a homeomorphism, the case
\pi=1
of Theorem
1.1
can be recovered from work of Boyer
[
Boy86
, Theorem 0.7 and Proposition 0.8]
, whereas the case
\pi=\mathbb{Z}
can be deduced from
[
CP23
, Theorem 1.10]
.
Theorem
1.1
is new in all other cases.
For
\pi\in\{1,\mathbb{Z}\}
,
the results of
[
Boy86
,
CP23
]
are formulated in terms of compatible pairs
(F,h)
, i.e. pairs
(F,h)

with

\textstyle{H_{2}(\partial X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{{\overbrace{H_{2}(X_{0},\partial X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}^{\cong H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(F^{*})^{-1}}
\textstyle{H_{1}(\partial X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(\partial X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\underbrace{H_{2}(X_{1},\partial X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}_{\cong H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{1}(\partial X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}

instead of compatible triples
(F,G,h)
, but for
\pi\in\{1,\mathbb{Z}\}
the former determine the latter; see Proposition
2.11
.
Note that for a compatible pair
(F,h)
, the condition
\lambda_{X_{1}}^{\partial}(F(x),(F^{*})^{-1}(y))=\lambda_{X_{0}}^{\partial}(x,y)
for all
x\in H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),y\in H_{2}(X_{0},\partial X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is automatically satisfied.
We refer to Section
7.2
for further details on compatible pairs and triples.
We also wish to emphasise that even under the assumptions of this remark, the proof of Theorem
1.1
is entirely different from the arguments in
[
Boy86
]
and
[
CP23
]
.

The next theorem states our results when the hypothesis on the
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-homology of the boundary is relaxed.
These results hold whenever
\pi
is a torsion-free
3
-manifold group.
This includes the cases where
\pi
is trivial or free.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
\pi
be a torsion-free
3
-manifold group, and let
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
be
4
-manifolds with
\pi_{1}(X_{j})\cong\pi
and
\iota_{j}\colon\pi_{1}(\partial X_{j})\to\pi_{1}(X_{j})
surjective for
j=0,1
.
Fix a degree one homotopy equivalence
h\colon\partial X_{0}\to\partial X_{1}
.

Assume
H_{1}(\partial X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong L\oplus T
with
L
a
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-free module and
T^{*}=0
.

•

When the universal covers of
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
are not spin,
h
extends to a homotopy equivalence
X_{0}\to X_{1}
if and only if there is a group isomorphism
u\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\pi_{1}(X_{1})
with
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
and a
k
-invariant preserving compatible triple
(F,G,h)
.

•

When the universal covers of
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
are spin,
h
extends to a homotopy equivalence
X_{0}\to X_{1}
if and only if there is a group isomorphism
u\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\pi_{1}(X_{1})
with
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
, a
k
-invariant preserving compatible triple
(F,G,h)
, and the universal cover of
X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}
is spin.

When the universal covers are spin, given a
k
-invariant preserving compatible triple
(F,G,h)
, the homotopy equivalence
X_{0}\to X_{1}
extending
h
can be chosen to induce
F
on
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-homology.

In the second item,
X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}
refers to the pushout of
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
along the homotopy equivalence
h
.
Propositions
3.8
and
3.9
show that
X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}
is a
4
-dimensional Poincaré pair with fundamental group
\pi
, allowing us to make sense of its equivariant intersection form and whether or not its universal cover is spin.

We note that even when
\pi=1
, Theorem
1.3
does not seem to have appeared in the literature (when
h
is a homeomorphism and
\pi=1
, the result follows from
[
Boy86
, Theorem 0.7 and Proposition 0.8]
).
In this case,
H_{1}(\partial X_{i})
necessarily decomposes as the direct sum of a free module and torsion module (because
\mathbb{Z}
is a PID), the relative
k
-invariants vanish and compatible triples can be replaced by compatible pairs.

Remark 1.4
.

We reframe the conditions on the universal cover in terms of the equivariant intersection form of the
X_{i}
.
Recall that a hermitian form
\lambda\colon H\times H\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
is
weakly even
if for every
x\in H
we have
\lambda(x,x)=a+\overline{a}
for some
a\in\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
,
odd
if it is not weakly even, and
even
if there exists a sesquilinear form
q\colon H\times H\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
with
\lambda(x,y)=q(x,y)+\overline{q(y,x)}
for every
x,y\in H
.
Even forms are weakly even, and if
H\cong L\oplus T
with
L
free and
T^{*}=0
then weakly even forms are even (Lemma
2.19
).

Given a
4
-manifold
X
, Lemma
2.21
shows that
\widetilde{X}
is spin if and only if
\lambda_{X}
is weakly even.
The first and second scenarios of Theorem
1.3
can therefore be thought of as the “weakly even” and “not weakly even” cases respectively.

1.2.
Homeomorphism classifications

When the fundamental group is good, in favourable circumstances, surgery theoretic arguments make it possible to upgrade homotopy classifications to homeomorphism classifications.
We list several sample results focusing on situations where the
2
-type need not be mentioned.

We begin by stating the surgery theoretic input.

Proposition 1.5
.

Assume that
\pi:=\pi_{1}(X_{1})
is trivial, finite cyclic, or a solvable Baumslag–Solitar group.
Let
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
be
4
-manifolds with fundamental group
\pi
and
\pi_{1}(\partial X_{i})\to\pi_{1}(X_{i})=:\pi
surjective for
i=0,1
.
If
\operatorname{ks}(X_{0})=\operatorname{ks}(X_{1})
and a homeomorphism
\partial X_{0}\to\partial X_{1}
extends to a homotopy equivalence
\phi\colon X_{0}\to X_{1}
, then it extends to a homeomorphism
\psi\colon X_{0}\to X_{1}
that satisfies
\psi_{*}=\phi_{*}
on
\pi_{1}(X_{0}),H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{0})
and
H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{0},\partial\widetilde{X}_{0})
.

Proof.

In the case where
\pi
is trivial or a Baumslag–Solitar group,
the argument for closed manifolds is by now well known (see e.g.
[
KT01
,
KL22
]
and
[
HKPR25
]
).
The tools involve the surgery exact sequence coupled with a Novikov pinching argument both of which go through without change for manifolds with boundary.
For the finite cyclic case, we refer to
[
LW93
, Proposition 4.6]
.
The equality
\psi_{*}=\phi_{*}
on
\pi_{1}(X_{0})
and
\pi_{2}(X_{0})\cong H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{0})
is explained in
[
LW93
, Proof of Proposition 4.6]
and the proof also yields the equality on
H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{0},\partial\widetilde{X}_{0})
and extends to the other fundamental groups we have listed.
∎

When
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
are simply-connected, Theorem
1.3
together with Proposition
1.5
recover (with a different proof) the result due to Boyer
[
Boy86
, Theorem 0.7 and Proposition 0.8]
that an orientation-preserving homeomorphism
h\colon\partial X_{0}\to\partial X_{1}
extends to a homeomorphism
X_{0}\to X_{1}
if

•

either
H_{1}(\partial X_{i})
is torsion and there is a compatible pair
(F,h)
(when the
X_{i}
are not spin, add the requirement that
\operatorname{ks}(X_{0})=\operatorname{ks}(X_{1})
),

•

or the
X_{i}
are not spin, there is a compatible pair
(F,h)
, and
\operatorname{ks}(X_{0})=\operatorname{ks}(X_{1})
,

•

or the
X_{i}
are spin, there is a compatible pair
(F,h)
, and the union
X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}
is spin.

We therefore focus on case in which
\pi
is nontrivial, starting with the infinite cyclic case.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
be
4
-manifolds with
\pi_{1}(X_{j})\cong\mathbb{Z}
and
\iota_{j}\colon\pi_{1}(\partial X_{j})\to\pi_{1}(X_{j})
surjective for
j=0,1
.
Fix an orientation-preserving homeomorphism
h\colon\partial X_{0}\to\partial X_{1}
.

•

When
H_{1}(\partial X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}])
is torsion,
h
extends to a homeomorphism
X_{0}\to X_{1}
if and only if there is an isomorphism
u\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\cong\pi_{1}(X_{1})
with
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
, and there is a compatible pair
(F,h)
; when the
X_{i}
are not spin, add the condition that
\operatorname{ks}(X_{0})=\operatorname{ks}(X_{1})
.

•

When the
X_{i}
are not spin and
H_{1}(\partial X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}])\cong L\oplus T
with
L
free and
T
torsion,
h
extends to a homeomorphism
X_{0}\to X_{1}
if and only if there is an isomorphism
u\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\cong\pi_{1}(X_{1})
with
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
, there is a compatible pair
(F,h)
, and
\operatorname{ks}(X_{0})=\operatorname{ks}(X_{1})
.

•

When the
X_{i}
are spin and
H_{1}(\partial X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}])\cong L\oplus T
with
L
free and
T
torsion,
h
extends to a homeomorphism
X_{0}\to X_{1}
if and only if there is an isomorphism
u\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\cong\pi_{1}(X_{1})
with
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
, there is a compatible pair
(F,h)
, and the union
X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}
is spin.

Additionally, when
H_{1}(\partial X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}])
is torsion or when the
X_{i}
are spin, given a
k
-invariant preserving compatible pair
(F,h)
as above, the homeomorphism
X_{0}\to X_{1}
extending
h
can be chosen to induce
F
on
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}]
-homology.

Proof.

Since
\pi_{1}(X_{i})\cong\mathbb{Z}
for
i=0,1
, Proposition
2.11
and the existence of a compatible pair
(F,h)
ensure the existence of a compatible triple
(F,G,h)
.
Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
imply that the homeomorphism
h\colon\partial X_{0}\to\partial X_{1}
extends to a homotopy equivalence
X_{0}\to X_{1}
.
If
X:=X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}
is spin, then the addivity of the Kirby-Siebenmann invariant (see e.g.
[
FNOP19
, Theorem 9.2]
) and of the signature give

\operatorname{ks}(X_{0})+\operatorname{ks}(X_{1})=\operatorname{ks}(X)=\frac{\sigma(X)}{8}=\frac{\sigma(X_{0})-\sigma(X_{1})}{8}=0\mod 2.

Here, we used that the signature of
X_{i}
is determined by the equivariant intersection form of
X_{i}
.
When
H_{1}(X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}])
is torsion and the
X_{i}
are spin,
X=X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}
is automatically spin
[
CP23
, Theorem 3.12]
; this also follows from the combination of Theorem
2.18
and Lemma
2.21
.
Proposition
1.5
then implies that
h
in fact extends to a homeomorphism.
The fact that a given isometry
F
can be realised follows from the corresponding realisation statement in Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
and from the equality
\psi_{*}=\phi_{*}
in Proposition
1.5
.
∎

The first item of Theorem
1.6
is
[
CP23
, Theorem 1.10]
(but the proof is entirely different), whereas a result similar in nature to the third item was announced in 2020 during an online talk by Kreck and Teichner.

Remark 1.7
.

The third item of
Theorem

1.6
implies the following related statement.
Let
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
be 4-manifolds with
\pi_{1}(X_{i})\cong\mathbb{Z}
, a preferred spin structure
\sigma_{i}
, and
\iota_{i}\colon\pi_{1}(\partial X_{i})\to\pi_{1}(X_{i})
surjective for
i=0,1
.
Fix a homeomorphism
h\colon\partial X_{0}\to\partial X_{1}
that preserves the induced spin structures
\partial\sigma_{i}
.
When
H_{1}(\partial X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}])\cong L\oplus T
with
L
free and
T
torsion, the homeomorphism
h
extends to a spin structure preserving homeomorphism
X_{0}\to X_{1}
if and only if there is an isomorphism
u\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\cong\pi_{1}(X_{1})
with
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
, and there is a compatible pair
(F,h)
.

To see this, note that since
h
preserves the induced spin structures, the union
X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}
is spin, so
Theorem

1.6
ensures that
h
extends to a homeomorphism
H\colon X_{0}\to X_{1}
.
Let
\sigma_{0}^{\prime}
be the pullback of
\sigma_{1}
along
H
.
Then
h
pulls back
\partial\sigma_{1}
to
\partial\sigma_{0}^{\prime}
and thus
\partial\sigma_{0}=\partial\sigma_{0}^{\prime}
.
This implies that
\sigma_{0}^{\prime}=\sigma_{0}
: indeed, since

H_{1}(\partial X_{0};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to H_{1}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is surjective,
\operatorname{Spin}(X_{0})\to\operatorname{Spin}(\partial X_{0})
is injective.
It follows that
H
preserves the spin structures
\sigma_{i}
as claimed.

For finite cyclic groups and solvable Baumslag–Solitar groups, we obtain the following result.

Theorem 1.8
.

Let
\pi
be either a solvable Baumslag–Solitar group or a finite cyclic group.
Let
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
be
4
-manifolds with
\pi_{1}(X_{j})\cong\pi
and
\iota_{j}\colon\pi_{1}(\partial X_{j})\to\pi_{1}(X_{j})
surjective.
Fix an orientation-preserving homeomorphism
h\colon\partial X_{0}\to\partial X_{1}
.

When
H_{1}(\partial X_{j};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}=0
for
j=0,1
, the following assertions are equivalent.

•

The homeomorphism
h
extends to a homeomorphism
X_{0}\to X_{1}
.

•

There is an isomorphism
u\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\cong\pi_{1}(X_{1})
with
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
, there is a compatible triple
(F,G,h)
, and
\operatorname{ks}(X_{0})=\operatorname{ks}(X_{1})
.

Additionally, given a
k
-invariant preserving compatible triple
(F,G,h)
as above, the homeomorphism
X_{0}\to X_{1}
extending
h
can be chosen to induce
F
and
G
on
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-homology.

Proof.

The argument is the same as in the proof of Theorem
1.6
: Theorem
1.1
ensures that
h
extends to a homotopy equivalence
X_{0}\to X_{1}
and
Proposition
1.5
then implies that
h
extends to a homeomorphism.
∎

Remark 1.9
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1.8
, it is conceivable that, in some cases, the existence of a compatible triple implies that
\operatorname{ks}(X_{0})=\operatorname{ks}(X_{1})
as is the case for
BS(1,0)\cong\penalty\ \mathbb{Z}
(recall Theorem
1.6
).
For example, when
\pi\cong BS(1,n)
and the universal covers of
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
are spin, the existence of a compatible triple ensures that the universal cover of
X=X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}
is
spin (combine Theorem
2.18
with Lemma
2.21
) which in turn, when
n
is even, implies that
X
is spin
[
HKT09
, Theorem B]
and thus
\operatorname{ks}(X_{0})=\operatorname{ks}(X_{1})
(by the same reasonning as in the proof of Theorem
1.6
together with
[
HKT09
, Remark 4.2]
).

Organisation

Section
2
describes our results in greater detail and proves Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
.
The remainder of the paper decomposes into four parts.
Sections
3
-
5
set up our primary and secondary obstructions to the extension problem.
Sections
6
and
7
are concerned with relative
k
-invariants and with reformulating the primary obstruction in terms of compatible triples.
Sections
8
-
11
are concerned with the analysis of the secondary obstruction.
Finally, Section
12
focuses on describing situations in which our theorems apply.
The appendix collects some facts about twisted homology and obstruction theory.

Conventions

Spaces are assumed to be compact and connected unless mentioned otherwise.
CW complexes are assumed to be finite.

Given a ring
R
with involution, and a left
R
-module
H
, we write
\overline{H}
for the right
R
-module whose underlying group agrees with that of
H
but with the
R
-module structure
x\cdot r:=\overline{r}x
for
x\in H
and
r\in R
.
Similarly, we write
H^{*}:=\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{left-}R}(H,R)}
to indicate that we are using the involution on
R
to turn the right
R
-module
\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{left-}R}(H,R)
into a left
R
module.

Finally, given a hermitian form
b\colon H\times H\to R
and a map
f\colon V\to H
we write
f^{*}b\colon V\times V\to R
for the hermitian form
(x,y)\mapsto b(f(x),f(y)).

If
f=g^{*}
is an induced map on cohomology, then we write
g^{*}b
instead of
(f^{*})^{*}(b).

2.
The homotopy classification in terms of the Postnikov
2
-type.

We describe our results in greater detail and then explain how they recover the statements from the introduction.
The organisation of this section matches the organisation of the paper but the proofs of the results stated here are deferred to later sections.

Since our results hold more generally than for manifolds, we begin with some terminology.

Definition 2.1
.

An
n
-dimensional
Poincaré complex

Y
consists of a space that is homotopy equivalent to
a CW complex, together with a class
[Y]\in H_{n}(Y)
such that for every
k\geq 0
, the cap product with
[Y]
induces an isomorphism

-\cap[Y]\colon H^{k}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(Y)])\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n-k}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(Y)]).

An
n
-dimensional
Poincaré pair

consists of a pair
(X,Y)
together with a class
[X]\in H_{n}(X,Y)
such that

•

the space
Y
is an
(n-1)
-dimensional Poincaré complex,

•

the inclusion
Y\subset X
is a cofibration;

•

there is a homotopy equivalence of pairs
(X,Y)\simeq(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
to a CW pair;

•

the connecting homomorphism satisfies
\partial([X])=[Y]
and, for every
k\geq 0
, the cap product with
[X]
induces an isomorphism

-\cap[X]\colon H^{k}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)])\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n-k}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)]).

Remark 2.2
.

We briefly comment on Definition
2.1
.

•

Definition
2.1
differs slightly from Wall’s definition
[
Wal67
]
.
Indeed, in addition to the condition on cap products, Wall requires Poincaré complexes to be finitely dominated CW complexes and, similarly, Poincaré pairs are assumed to be CW pairs (thus ensuring that the inclusion
Y\subset X
is automatically a cofibration).
We relax this condition so that, as proved in Lemma
3.1
below, if
M
is an
n
-manifold, then
(M,\partial M)
is an
n
-dimensionsal Poincaré pair, even if
M
or
\partial M
happen not to admit a CW structure.
In particular, all the results obtained in this section, which are stated in terms of
4
-dimensional Poincaré pairs, also apply to
4
-manifolds.

•

Using his definition, Wall proves that if
(X,Y)
is a Poincaré pair, then the cap product with
[X]
in fact induces isomorphisms on (co)homology with all coefficients
[
Wal67
, Lemma 1.2]
.
His proof also applies with our definition.

2.1.
Primary and secondary obstructions

A
Postnikov
2
-type
of a space
X
consists of a pair
(P_{2}(X),c)
, where
P_{2}(X)
is a space with
\pi_{i}(P_{2}(X))=0
for
i\geq 3
and
c\colon X\to P_{2}(X)
is a
3
-connected map.
The pair
(P_{2}(X),c)
is unique up to (an essentially unique) homotopy equivalence and thus, for brevity, we will often refer to the space
P_{2}(X)
as the Postnikov
2
-type of
X
.

Notation 2.3
.

Fix
4
-dimensional Poincaré pairs
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
with
\pi_{1}(X_{i})\cong\pi
, the same
2
-type, and
\iota_{j}\colon\pi_{1}(Y_{j})\to\pi_{1}(X_{j})
surjective for
j=0,1
.
Fix a degree one homotopy equivalence
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
.
Write
B:=P_{2}(X_{1})
and assume without loss of generality that
Y_{1}\subset B
: if
c\colon X_{1}\to B^{\prime}
is a
3
-connected map into a model for the
2
-type, then the mapping cylinder
B
of
c
is homotopy equivalent to
B^{\prime}
and contains
Y_{1}
as a subspace.
This leads to a
3
-connected map
c_{1}\colon(X_{1},Y_{1})\to(B,Y_{1})
.
Finally fix a
3
-connected map
c_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B
with
c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}=c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h
so that it induces a map of pairs
c_{0}\colon(X_{0},Y_{0})\to(B,Y_{1}).

This also implies that the group isomorphism
u:=(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}\circ(c_{0})_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\pi_{1}(X_{1})
satisfies
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
.

The maps
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
lead to relative pairings on
(B,Y_{1})
which are more convenient to describe in cohomology.
For this reason, we work with the cohomological version of the relative intersection form
\lambda_{X_{i}}^{\partial}
, i.e. with the pairing

\displaystyle b_{X_{i}}^{\partial}\colon H^{2}(X_{i},Y_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H^{2}(X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle\beta,\operatorname{PD}_{X_{i}}(\alpha)\rangle.

If
Y_{i}
is empty, we write
b_{X_{i}}
instead of
b_{X_{i}}^{\partial}
.
Poincaré duality induces an isometry
b_{X_{i}}^{\partial}\cong\lambda_{X_{i}}^{\partial}
; see Remark
A.8
for further details.
For
i=0,1
we then consider the pulled back pairing

\displaystyle c_{i}^{*}b_{X_{i}}^{\partial}\colon H^{2}(B,Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto b_{X_{i}}^{\partial}(c_{i}^{*}(x),c_{i}^{*}(y)).

The first main definition of this section is the following.

Definition 2.4
.

The
primary obstruction
to
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
being homotopy equivalent rel.
h
is the pairing

c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}-c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}\colon H^{2}(B,Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi].

Remark 2.5
.

In this definition, the homotopy equivalence
h
is permitted to have degree
\pm 1
.

In order to describe the secondary obstruction, we write
U:=X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}
for the pushout of
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
along
h
and set
c:=c_{0}\cup c_{1}\colon U\to B
.
We refer to Propositions
3.8
and
3.9
for a proof of the fact that
U
is a
4
-dimensional Poincaré complex with fundamental group
\pi
(so that one can make sense of its equivariant intersection form).

The next proposition follows from the combination of Propositions
4.5
and
4.8
.
In essence, it shows that when the primary obstruction vanishes, a secondary obstruction can be defined.

Proposition 2.6
.

Assume the maps
c_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B
and
c_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B
satisfy
c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
.
For
a,b\in H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
,
and
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
with
c_{1}|_{\partial}^{*}(a^{\prime})=a,c_{1}|_{\partial}^{*}(b^{\prime})=b
, consider

\displaystyle b(c_{0},c_{1})\colon H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]

\displaystyle(a,b)

\displaystyle\mapsto b_{U}(c^{*}(a^{\prime}),c^{*}(b^{\prime})).

This assignment does not depend on the choice of
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}
, and the pairing
b(c_{0},c_{1})
is hermitian.

In order to make precise the idea that
b(c_{0},c_{1})
serves as a secondary obstruction, we need to arrange for the vanishing of
b(c_{0},c_{1})
not to depend on the choice of
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
within their homotopy classes over
B
.
Write
\operatorname{hAut}_{Y_{1}}(B)
for the group of rel.
Y_{1}
homotopy classes of homotopy equivalences
B\to B
that restrict to the identity on
Y_{1}
and, given a
3
-connected map
c_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B
, consider the group

\mathcal{G}=\{\varphi\in\operatorname{hAut}_{Y_{1}}(B)\mid(\varphi\circ c_{1})^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}\}.

Observe that
\mathcal{G}
does not depend on
c_{1}
within its homotopy class over
B
.
Proposition
5.8
proves that
\mathcal{G}
acts on the set
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
of hermitian forms on
H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, and Proposition
5.9
shows that, once considered in
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}
, the vanishing of the pairing
b(c_{0},c_{1})
does not depend on the choice of
c_{0}
(within its homotopy class over
B
) satisfying
c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h=c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}.

Definition 2.7
.

Assume the maps
c_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B
and
c_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B
satisfy
c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
,
The
secondary obstruction
to
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
being homotopy equivalent rel.
h
refers to the hermitian form

b(c_{0},c_{1})\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}.

We describe informally how we think of the hermitian form
b(c_{0},c_{1})
.
Given
a\in H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, since the universal covers
\widetilde{X}_{0}
and
\widetilde{X}_{1}
, a loop
\gamma
representing
\operatorname{PD}_{Y_{1}}(a)\in H_{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
bounds immersed discs in
\widetilde{X}_{0}
and
\widetilde{X}_{1}
whose union forms an immersed sphere
S(a)
representing the Poincaré dual
\operatorname{PD}_{X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}}(c^{*}(a^{\prime}))\in H_{2}(X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.
We then think of
b(c_{0},c_{1})(x,y)
as
\lambda_{X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}}(S(x),S(y))
.

2.2.
If the obstructions vanish, then the homotopy equivalence extends

One of our main technical results shows that for large classes of fundamental groups, the primary and secondary obstructions are the only obstructions to extending
h
to a homotopy equivalence.
When
\pi
is finite, the statement involves the Whitehead
\Gamma
-group
\Gamma(\pi_{2}(X_{1}))
; see Section
12.2
for a brief recollection of this construction.

Recall that a group
\pi
has
cohomological dimension at most
n
, denoted
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq n
, if the
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module
\mathbb{Z}
, endowed with the trivial
\pi
-action, has a projective resolution of length
n
.
The proof of the following theorem can be found in Section
5
.

Theorem 2.8
.

Let
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
be
4
-dimensional Poincaré pairs with fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X_{i})\cong\pi
, Postnikov
2
-type
B:=P_{2}(X_{1})
and
\pi_{1}(Y_{i})\to\pi_{1}(X_{i})
surjective for
i=0,1
, and let
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
be a degree one homotopy equivalence.
Assume that

•

either
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
and the map
\operatorname{ev}^{*}\colon H^{2}(X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{**}\to H_{2}(X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}
is an isomorphism,

•

or
\pi_{1}(X_{i})
is finite and
\Gamma(\pi_{2}(X_{1}))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\mathbb{Z}
is torsion free.

The homotopy equivalence
h
extends to a homotopy equivalence
X_{0}\to X_{1}
if and only if
there exist
3
-connected maps
c_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B
and
c_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B
such that
c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h=c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}
and

(1)

the pulled back pairings
c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}
and
c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
on
H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
are equal,

(2)

the hermitian pairing
b(c_{0},c_{1})
vanishes in
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}
.

Additionally, when
b(c_{0},c_{1})=0\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
, the homotopy equivalence
f\colon X_{0}\to X_{1}
extending
h
can be chosen to satisfy
c_{1}\circ f\simeq c_{0}
.

As we describe in Section
12
, the conditions in Theorem
2.8
hold for the trivial group, the infinite cyclic group, surface groups, Baumslag–Solitar groups, for torsion-free
3
-manifold groups, as well as for classes of finite groups, including finite cyclic groups.

For large classes of groups,
Theorem
2.8
provides a solution to the problem of deciding whether a homotopy equivalence
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
extends to a homotopy equivalence
X_{0}\to X_{1}
.
Since practical applications typically require statements that do not involve the
2
-type, the next sections describe settings where Theorem
2.8
can be formulated without appealing to
c_{0},c_{1}
and
B
.

2.3.
The primary obstruction and compatible triples

Theorem
6.2
shows that if there is an isomorphism
u\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\cong\pi_{1}(X_{1})
with
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
and an isomorphism
F\colon\pi_{2}(X_{0})\cong\pi_{2}(X_{1})
with
h^{*}(k_{X_{1},Y_{1}})=F_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}})
, then there are
3
-connected maps
c_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B
and
c_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B
that satisfy
c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}=c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h
.
The next result, in which the homotopy equivalence
h
is permitted to have degree
\pm 1
,
shows that the vanishing of the primary obstruction is equivalent to the existence of a compatible triple.

Theorem 2.9
.

Assume that
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is injective and
H_{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong L\oplus T
, with
L
a free
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module and
T^{*}=0
.
The following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

there is an isomorphism
u\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\cong\pi_{1}(X_{1})
with
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
and a compatible triple
(F,G,h)
with
h^{*}(k_{X_{1},Y_{1}})=F_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}})
;

(2)

there are
3
-connected maps
c_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B,c_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B
such that
c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}=c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h
with

c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}\quad\text{ and }\quad(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}(c_{0})_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}})=h^{*}(k_{X_{1},Y_{1}}).

Given
(F,G,h)
as in
(1)
, the maps
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
can be chosen to satisfy
(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}(c_{0})_{*}=F
as well as
(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}(c_{0})_{*}=G
.
Conversely, given
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
as in
(2)
, the maps
F
and
G
are defined as
F:=(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}(c_{0})_{*}
and
G:=(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}(c_{0})_{*}
.

Proof.

The
(1)\Rightarrow(2)
direction is Proposition
7.7
.
The
(2)\Rightarrow(1)
direction is Proposition
7.12
.
∎

Remark 2.10
.

Proposition
12.2
shows
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is injective if
H_{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}=\penalty\ 0.

We record settings in which compatible triples can be replaced by compatible pairs.
The proof of the following result, in which one need not assume that
h
has degree one, can be found in Section
7.2
.

Proposition 2.11
.

If
H^{k}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
for
k=2,3
, then the existence of a compatible pair ensures the existence of a compatible triple.

We also record settings in which the condition on the relative
k
-invariants can be omitted.

Proposition 2.12
.

Let
F\colon\pi_{2}(X_{0})\cong\pi_{2}(X_{1})
be an isomorphism.
Assume that

•

either
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 2
and there is a compatible triple
(F,G,h)
,

•

or

H_{4}(\pi)=0
and
H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}=0
,

then
F_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}})=h^{*}(k_{X_{1},Y_{1}})
.

Proof.

The first item is Proposition
7.13
, the second is Proposition
7.15
.
∎

2.4.
The vanishing of the secondary obstruction I: odd forms.

We now turn to the vanishing of the secondary obstruction.
We briefly recall the notation from Notation
2.3
and additionally fix
3
-connected maps for which the primary obstruction vanishes.

Notation 2.13
.

Let
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
be
4
-dimensional Poincaré pairs with
\pi_{1}(X_{j})\cong\pi
,

2
-type
B:=P_{2}(X_{1})
and
\iota_{j}\colon\pi_{1}(Y_{j})\to\pi_{1}(X_{j})
surjective for
j=0,1
.
Fix a degree one homotopy equivalence
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}

and assume there are
3
-connected maps
c_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B,c_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B
such that
c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}=c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h
with
c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
.
Note that this implies
\lambda_{X_{0}}\cong\lambda_{X_{1}}
.

We also fix terminology concerning hypotheses that will often be assumed.

Definition 2.14
.

We say that
\operatorname{ev}^{*}
is an isomorphism
if the following is an isomorphism for
i=0,1
:

\operatorname{ev}^{*}\colon H^{2}(X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{**}\to H_{2}(X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}.

Proposition
12.5
shows that this condition holds if
\pi
is a free product of a free group and
\operatorname{PD}_{3}
groups (e.g. if
\pi
is trivial, free, or, more generally, a torsion-free
3
-manifold group), or if
\pi=\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
is the fundamental group of an orientable surface and
\pi_{1}(\Sigma\times S^{1})\to\pi
is the projection onto the first coordinate, or if
\pi
is a solvable Baumslag–Solitar group.

When
\lambda_{X_{0}}\cong\lambda_{X_{1}}
is odd, the following result (which is proved in Section
11
) shows that the secondary obstruction vanishes under some additional hypotheses.

Theorem 2.15
.

Assume that
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
.
If
\operatorname{ev}^{*}
is an isomorphism,
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is injective,

\lambda_{X_{1}}
is odd, and
H_{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong L\oplus T
with
L
free and
T^{*}=0
, then

\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}=0.

In particular, the secondary obstruction vanishes:

b(c_{0},c_{1})=0\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}.

Remark 2.16
.

The prevalence of the condition
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
in our results is explained by Proposition
3.4
:
\operatorname{cd}(\pi_{1}(X_{i}))\leq 3
is equivalent to requiring that
\pi_{2}(X_{i})
be
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{i})]
-projective.

2.5.
The vanishing of the secondary obstruction II: (weakly) even forms.

Continuing with Notation
2.13
, the next result (which is proved in Section
10
) shows that contrarily to the odd case, when
\lambda_{X_{0}}\cong\lambda_{X_{1}}
is even, an additional sufficient condition is needed for the secondary obstruction to vanish.

Theorem 2.17
.

Assume that
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
.
If
\operatorname{ev}^{*}
is an isomorphism,
\lambda_{X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}}
is weakly even, and
H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong L\oplus T
with
L
free and
T^{*}=0
,
then

b(c_{0},c_{1})=0\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}.

In fact, there is a
c_{0}^{\prime}\simeq c_{0}
with
c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h=c_{0}^{\prime}|_{Y_{0}}
such that
b(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1})=0\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
.

Note that if
\lambda_{X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}}
is (weakly) even, then so is
\lambda_{X_{i}}
for
i=0,1
.
Our next result (also proved in Section
10
) shows that modulo the difference between even and weakly even forms, the condition on the union from Theorem
2.17
is also necessary.

Theorem 2.18
.

Assume that the hermitian form
\lambda_{X_{0}}\cong\lambda_{X_{1}}
is weakly even.
If the secondary obstruction
b(c_{0},c_{1})
vanishes in
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}
, then
\lambda_{X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}}\cong b_{X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}}
is weakly even.

At this stage, it is useful to record three lemmas that we will constantly refer to when working with even hermitian forms.

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
H\cong L\oplus T
be a
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module where
L
is free and
T^{*}=0
.
If a hermitian form
\lambda\colon H\times H\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
is weakly even, then it is even.

Proof.

A weakly even hermitian form
b
on a free module
L
is even: choose a basis
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
for
L
with
b(e_{i},e_{j})=x_{i}+x_{i}^{*}
, define
q(e_{i})=x_{i}
and note that
\lambda=q+q^{*}
.
It follows that the pairing
\lambda
is even on
L\subset H
.
But since
T^{*}=0
, it follows that
\lambda|_{T\times T}=0

and so
\lambda
itself is even.
∎

Lemma 2.20
.

Let
\pi
be a group with no elements of order
2
and let
H
be a
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module.
A hermitian form
(H,b)
over is weakly even if and only if
b(x,x)_{1}
is even for every
x\in H
.

Proof.

In general, without any condition
\pi
, a hermitian form
b
over
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
is weakly even if and only if its coefficient
b(x,x)_{g}
at
g\in\pi
is even for every
g\in\pi
of order
2
.
∎

Lemma 2.21
.

Let
X
be either a
4
-dimensional Poincaré complex or be part of a Poincaré pair
(X,Y)
.
The form
\lambda_{X}
is weakly even if and only if
\widetilde{X}
is spin.

Proof.

Set
\pi:=\pi_{1}(X)
.
If
\lambda_{X}
is weakly even, then for every
x\in\pi_{2}(X)
, the coefficient
(\lambda_{X}(x,x))_{g}
at
g\in\pi
is even for every
g\in\pi
of order
2
.
In particular
Q_{\widetilde{X}}(x,x)=(\lambda_{X}(x,x))_{e}
is even for every
x\in X
.
Thus the intersection form
Q_{\widetilde{X}}
is even and therefore
\widetilde{X}
is spin.

We prove the converse, first when
X
is a manifold, then when
X
is a Poincaré complex and finally when
(X,Y)
is a Poincaré pair.
We first assume
X
is a manifold.
If
\widetilde{X}
is spin, then
\lambda_{X}
admits a quadratic refinement
\mu_{X}
, say
\lambda_{X}=\mu_{X}+\mu_{X}^{*}
; this is explained in detail in
[
KPT25
, Remark 1.7]
.
Thus
(\lambda_{X}(x,x))_{g}
is even for every
g\in\pi
of order
2
and we conclude that
\lambda_{X}
is weakly even.
We now assume that
X
is a Poincaré complex.
Since
X
is reducible
[
Lan22
]
, there is a degree one map
f\colon M\to X
and using surgery we can assume it is an isomorphism on
\pi_{1}
.
By naturality, since
\widetilde{X}
is spin, so is
\widetilde{M}
, implying that
\lambda_{M}
is weakly even.
Since
\lambda_{M}=\lambda_{X}\oplus\lambda_{K}
, where
\lambda_{K}
is the form restricted to the surgery kernel, it follows that
\lambda_{X}
is weakly even as well.

We conclude with the general case where
(X,Y)
is a Poincaré pair.
In what follows, we write
\widetilde{Y}\subset\widetilde{X}
for the preimage of
Y
in the universal cover
\widetilde{X}
.
Since
\widetilde{X}
is spin,
(\lambda_{X}(x,x))_{e}
is even for every
x\in H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),
and it remains to prove that
(\lambda_{X}(x,x))_{g}
is even for every
x\in H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and every
g\in\pi
of order
2
, i.e. that
a\cup ga=0\in H^{4}_{cs}(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{Y};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\cong H^{4}_{cs}(\widetilde{X}\cup_{\widetilde{Y}}\widetilde{X},\widetilde{X};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
for every element
a\in H^{2}_{cs}(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{Y};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\cong H^{2}_{cs}(\widetilde{X}\cup_{\widetilde{Y}}\widetilde{X},\widetilde{X};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.
This follows from the naturality of the cup product under the map
j\colon(\widetilde{X}\cup_{\widetilde{Y}}\widetilde{X},\emptyset)\to(\widetilde{X}\cup_{\widetilde{Y}}\widetilde{X},\widetilde{X})
because
j^{*}
induces an isomorphism on
H^{4}(-;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
and
j^{*}(ga\cup a)=gj^{*}(a)\cup j^{*}(a)=0
.
In the last equality, we used the statement for Poincaré complexes: the double
\widetilde{X}\cup_{\widetilde{Y}}\widetilde{X}
is a Poincaré complex by Proposition
3.8
.
∎

2.6.
The vanishing of the secondary obstruction III : the main technical result.

Both Theorem
2.15
and Theorem
2.17
rely on being able to change
b(c_{0},c_{1})
by an even form.
This is the content of the next theorem which is one of the more technical results of this paper.
More precisely we show that for certain groups with
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
, for any sesquilinear form
q
on
H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, it is possible to homotope
c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}
back to itself so that the secondary invariant changes from
b(c_{0},c_{1})
to
b(c_{0},c_{1})+q+q^{*}
, where
q^{*}(x,y):=\overline{q(y,x)}
.
Continuing with the set-up from Notation
2.13
, the resulting statement reads as follows.

Theorem 2.22
.

Assume that
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
.
If
\operatorname{ev}^{*}
is an isomorphism
and
b(c_{0},c_{1})
is even, then

b(c_{0},c_{1})=0\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}.

In fact, there is a
c_{0}^{\prime}\simeq c_{0}
with
c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h=c_{0}^{\prime}|_{Y_{0}}
such that
b(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1})=0\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
.

The proof of Theorem
2.22
is fairly arduous and will be spread out between Sections
8
and
9
.
The intuition behind (the technical result underlying) Theorem
2.22
is explained in Remark
9.5
.

Remark 2.23
.

Given simply-connected
4
-manifolds
X_{0},X_{1}
an orientation-preserving homeomorphism
h\colon\partial X_{0}\to\partial X_{1}
and an isometry
F\colon\lambda_{X_{0}}\cong\lambda_{X_{1}}
that is compatible with
h
, Boyer defines an obstruction

\theta(F,h)\in\operatorname{im}(H^{1}(Y_{1})\to H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}_{2}))

which is shown to vanish if and only if
h
extends to a homeomorphism inducing
F

[
Boy86
, Theorem 0.7]
.
The definition involves the spin structures of
Y_{1}
and satisfies the following properties
[
Boy86
, Proposition 0.8]
:

(1)

if
H_{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Q})=0
, then
\theta(F,h)=0
,

(2)

when
\lambda_{X_{i}}
is even,
\theta(F,h)=0
if and only if
X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}
is spin,

(3)

when
\lambda_{X_{i}}
is odd, there is an isometry
F^{\prime}
that is compatible with
h
such that
\theta(F^{\prime},h)=0
.

Informally, we think of our secondary invariant
b(c_{0},c_{1})
as an analogue of Boyer’s
\theta(F,h)
.
Indeed despite their definitions being very different (our invariant does not involve spin structures and Boyer’s does not reference
2
-types), they share similar properties as illustrated by Theorems
2.15
and
2.22
as well as the fact that
b(c_{0},c_{1})
vanishes if
H_{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}=0
.

We further compare the target
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}
of our secondary obstruction with Boyer’s target, namely
I^{1}(Y):=\operatorname{im}(H^{1}(Y_{1})\to H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}_{2}))
.
For this, given a
3
-dimensional Poincaré complex
Y
and
an epimorphism
\pi_{1}(Y)\twoheadrightarrow\pi
, we generalise Boyer’s
I^{1}(Y)
by setting

I^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]):=\operatorname{im}(H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\xrightarrow{\operatorname{ev}}\operatorname{Hom}(H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to\operatorname{Hom}(H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),\mathbb{Z}_{2})),

where the second map is induced by augmentation modulo
2
.
When
\pi=1
, evaluation induces an isomorphism
I^{1}(Y)\to I^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
, whence our notation (which is chosen to mimick Boyer’s).

Given
x\in\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
, write
(x)_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
for the coefficient of the neutral element and consider the group homomorphism

\displaystyle\widehat{}\ \colon\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))

\displaystyle\to I^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\mapsto(x\mapsto b(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}^{-1}(x),\operatorname{PD}_{Y}^{-1}(x))_{1}\mod 2).

The following proposition relates
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
to
I^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and leads to an upper bound on the cardinality of
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}
; here we emphasise that the definition of
\mathcal{G}
involves the Postnikov
2
-type
B
and the relative hermitian form
b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
; recall Notation
2.13
.

Proposition 2.24
.

The map
\ \widehat{}\
satisfies the following properties.

(1)

The kernel of
\ \widehat{}\
consists of hermitian forms
b
such that
b(x,x)_{1}
is even for every
x
.
In particular, if
\pi
has no nontrivial elements of order
2
,
the kernel consists of weakly even forms.

(2)

If
H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong L\oplus T
with
L
free and
T^{*}=0
, then the map
\ \widehat{}\
is surjective and therefore, if
\pi
has no nontrivial elements of order
2
, induces an isomorphism

\left(\mathbb{Z}[\pi]/\langle x+\overline{x}\mid x\in\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\rangle\right)^{\oplus b_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}\cong\frac{\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))}{\{\text{(weakly) even forms}\}}\xrightarrow{\widehat{}\ ,\ \cong}I^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),

where a (weakly) even form acts on a hermitian form by
b\cdot b^{\prime}:=b+b^{\prime}
.

(3)

Use
\ \widehat{}\
to transport the action
\mathcal{G}\curvearrowright\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
(which we denote
\varphi\cdot b:=b(\varphi)+b
) to an action
\mathcal{G}\curvearrowright I^{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
(given by
\varphi\cdot x:=\widehat{b}(\varphi)+x
) so that
\ \widehat{}\
induces a morphism

\widehat{}\ \colon\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}\to I^{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])/\mathcal{G}.

that is an isomorphism if
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3,\operatorname{ev}^{*}
is an isomorphism, and
H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong L\oplus T
with
L
free and
T^{*}=0
.

Proof.

The first item follows from the definition of
\ \widehat{}\
and Lemma
2.20
.
The surjectivity follows from the definitions
and, since
H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong L\oplus T
with
L
free and
T^{*}=0
, Lemma
2.19
ensures that weakly even forms are even.
We prove the third item.
The fact that
\ \widehat{}\
descends to a homomorphism on the orbit sets follows from the equation
b(\psi\circ\varphi)=b(\psi)+b(\varphi)
(this equality is proved in Proposition
5.8
), whereas the surjectivity follows from the first two items of this proposition.
It remains to prove injectivity.
If
\widehat{b}=\widehat{b(\varphi)}
for some
\varphi\in\mathcal{G}
, then
b
and
b(\varphi)
differ by a weakly even form and thus by an even form because
H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong L\oplus T
.
Apply Theorem
9.4
to realise this form as
b(\varphi^{\prime})
for some
\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}
so that
b=b(\varphi)+b(\varphi^{\prime})=b(\varphi\circ\varphi^{\prime})
, as required.
∎

We emphasize the take-away from the second and third items of this proposition: since
\mathcal{G}

can act by odd forms, we are not able to entirely determine
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}
, even when
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
and
\operatorname{ev}^{*}
is an isomorphism.
Under these assumptions, the third item of Proposition
2.24
nevertheless yields

|\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}|\leq|I^{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])/\mathcal{G}|\leq|I^{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])|\leq|\operatorname{Hom}(H_{1}(Y),\mathbb{Z}_{2}))|=|H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}_{2})|

We conclude with instances in which
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}
can be be determined.

Proposition 2.25
.

Assume
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
.
Assume additionally that
H_{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong L\oplus T
with
L
free and
T^{*}=0
, and that
\operatorname{ev}^{*}
is an isomorphism.

•

If
\lambda_{X_{0}}\cong\lambda_{X_{1}}
is odd and
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is injective, then

\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}=0.

•

If
\lambda_{X_{i}}
is weakly even, then
\ \widehat{}\
induces an isomorphism

\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}\cong I^{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).

Proof.

The first item is Theorem
2.15
.
The second follows from the third item of Proposition
2.24
and from the assertion that, when
\lambda_{X_{i}}
is weakly even,
I^{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])/\mathcal{G}=I^{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.
This assertion amounts to showing that the action
\varphi\cdot x:=\widehat{b}(\varphi)+x
is trivial, i.e. that
\widehat{b}(\varphi)
is weakly even.
But this is the first claim in the proof of Theorem
2.18
at the end of Section
10
.
∎

In particular, when
\pi
is trivial, our target is isomorphic to Boyer’s in the even case.
In the odd case, our target is trivial whereas Boyer’s need not be so.

2.7.
Proof of the statements from the introduction

This section shows how the previous results lead to the statements from the introduction.
We recall the relevant statements both for the reader’s convenience and to record that they hold more generally than stated above: namely they hold for
4
-dimensional Poincaré pairs.
In order to avoid repeating the set-up, we fix the following notation for the remainder of this section.

Notation 2.26
.

Fix
4
-dimensional Poincaré pairs
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
with
\pi_{1}(X_{i})\cong\pi
, the same
2
-type, and
\iota_{j}\colon\pi_{1}(Y_{j})\to\pi_{1}(X_{j})
surjective for
j=0,1
.
Additionally, fix a degree one homotopy equivalence
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
.

The next result generalises Theorem
1.1
from the introduction.

Theorem 2.27
.

Let
\pi
be a group and assume

•

either that
\pi
is finite and
\Gamma(\pi_{2}(X_{1}))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\mathbb{Z}
is torsion free,

•

or that
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
and that
\operatorname{ev}^{*}
is an isomorphism.

When
H_{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}=0
, the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

the homotopy equivalence
h
extends to a homotopy equivalence
X_{0}\to X_{1}
;

(2)

there exists an isomorphism
u\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\cong\pi_{1}(X_{1})
with
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
and a compatible triple
(F,G,h)
with

F_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}})=h^{*}(k_{X_{1},Y_{1}}).

When
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
, the condition involving the relative
k
-invariants can be omitted.

Additionally, given a
k
-invariant preserving compatible triple
(F,G,h)
as above, the homotopy equivalence
X_{0}\to X_{1}
extending
h
can be chosen to induce
F
and
G
on
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-homology.

Proof.

The
(1)\Rightarrow(2)
direction is not overly challenging and so we focus on the
(2)\Rightarrow(1)
direction.
By Theorem
2.9
, the existence of a
k
-invariant preserving compatible triple ensures the existence of
c_{0},c_{1}
with vanishing primary obstruction.
Since
H_{1}(Y_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}=0
, there is no secondary obstruction.
Theorem
2.8
now ensures that the homotopy equivalence extends.
When
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
, Proposition
2.12
ensures that the
k
-invariant condition can be ignored.
The fact that a given compatible triple can be realised follows from the corresponding clauses in Theorems
2.9
and
5
.
∎

Remark 2.28
.

We explain why Theorem
1.1
follows from Theorem
2.27
.
If
\pi
is finite abelian with two generators or is finite dihedral, then
\Gamma(\pi_{2}(X_{1}))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\mathbb{Z}
is torsion free
[
HK88
,
KPR24
,
KNR22
]
, whereas if
\pi
is either a surface group, or a solvable Baumslag–Solitar group, or the free product of a free group with
\operatorname{PD}_{3}
groups, then
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
, and Proposition
12.5
then ensures that
\operatorname{ev}^{*}
is an isomorphism.
Recall that a group
\pi
is a
\operatorname{PD}_{n}
group
if
B\pi
is a
n
-dimensional Poincaré complex.

The next result generalises Theorem
1.3
from the introduction.

Theorem 2.29
.

Let
\pi
be a free product of a free group and
\operatorname{PD}_{3}
groups
and assume
H_{1}(Y_{j};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong L\oplus T
with
L
a
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-free module and
T^{*}=0
.

•

When the universal covers of
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
are not spin,
h
extends to a homotopy equivalence
X_{0}\to X_{1}
if and only if there is a group isomorphism
u\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\cong\pi_{1}(X_{1})
with
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
, and there is a
k
-invariant preserving compatible triple
(F,G,h)
.

•

When the universal covers of
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
are spin,
h
extends to a homotopy equivalence
X_{0}\to X_{1}
if and only if there is a group isomorphism
u\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\cong\pi_{1}(X_{1})
with
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
, there is a
k
-invariant preserving compatible triple
(F,G,h)
, and the universal cover of
X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}
is spin.

When the universal covers are spin, given a
k
-invariant preserving compatible triple
(F,G,h)
, the homotopy equivalence
X_{0}\to X_{1}
extending
h
can be chosen to induce
F
on
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-homology.

Proof.

Lemma
2.21
ensures that the universal covers of
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
are spin if and only if
\lambda_{X_{0}}\cong\lambda_{X_{1}}
is weakly even.
Also, the condition
H_{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong L\oplus T
ensures that weakly even hermitian forms on
H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
are even hermitian (recall Lemma
2.19
).

The existence of a homotopy equivalence extending
h
is readily seen to imply that there is a
k
-invariant preserving compatible triple and that there are
3
-connected maps
c_{0},c_{1}
into the
2
-type of
X_{1}
with
b(c_{0},c_{1})=0
(see Theorem
5
for this latter point).
In the even case, Theorem
2.18
then ensures that
X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}
has weakly even equivariant intersection form.
Lemma
2.21
ensures that the universal cover of
X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}
is spin.

We focus on the converse.
First, we note that Propositions
12.3
and
12.5
show that our assumption on the group
\pi
ensures that the hypotheses of Theorems
2.8
,
2.9
and
2.17
are satisfied.
Next,
by Theorem
2.9
, the existence of a compatible triple ensures the existence of
3
-connected maps
c_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B,c_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B
such that
c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}=c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h

and with vanishing primary obstruction.
In the nonspin case, Theorem
2.15
guarantees that
b(c_{0},c_{1})=0\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}.

In the spin case,
Lemma
2.21
ensures that
\lambda_{X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}}
is weakly even and Theorem
2.17
then implies that
b(c_{0},c_{1})=0\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}.

Theorem
2.8
now ensures that the homotopy equivalence extends.
For the final sentence, Theorem
2.17
ensures that there is a
c_{0}^{\prime}\simeq c_{0}
with
c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h=c_{0}^{\prime}|_{Y_{0}}
and
b(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1})=0\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
, so the fact that a given compatible triple can be realised follows from the corresponding clauses in Theorems
2.9
and
5
.
∎

3.
Preparatory results

This section collects some preliminary results needed to define the primary and secondary obstructions.
Section
3.1
records some facts about Poincaré pairs.
Given a
4
-dimensional Poincaré pair
(X,Y)
with
\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(X)=:\pi
surjective, Section
3.2
focuses on the
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module
\pi_{2}(X)
.
Next, given a space
B
and a
\sigma\in H_{4}(B)
, Section
3.3
considers the pairing

b_{\sigma}\colon H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi],(\alpha,\beta)\mapsto\langle\beta,\alpha\cap\sigma\rangle.

Section
3.4
is concerned with the
pushout of two Poincaré pairs along a homotopy equivalence.
Finally the short Section
3.5
states and proves an extension lemma.

3.1.
Poincaré pairs

This short section collects two lemmas concerning Poincaré pairs.

We begin by proving a fact that was mentioned in Remark
2.2
.

Lemma 3.1
.

If
M
is an
n
-manifold, then
(M,\partial M)
is a
n
-dimensional Poincaré pair.

Proof.

Since
\partial M
is a manifold, it satisfies Poincaré duality and is homotopy equivalent to a CW complex, see e.g.
[
FNOP19
, Theorem 3.16]
.
Hence
\partial M
is a Poincaré complex.
Since
M
satisfies Poincaré duality and
\partial M\hookrightarrow M
is a cofibration (thanks to the existence of a collar neighborhood of
\partial M
), it only remains to show that
(M,\partial M)
is homotopy equivalent to a CW pair.

The manifolds
M
and
\partial M
are homotopy equivalent to CW complexes
X
and
Y
, respectively, see e.g.
[
FNOP19
, Theorem 3.16]
.
Choose a homotopy equivalence
h\colon\partial M\to Y
, a homotopy inverse
\overline{h}\colon Y\to\partial M
, and consider the map

f\colon Y\xrightarrow{\overline{h}}\partial M\hookrightarrow M\simeq X.

By cellular approximation, after possibly changing
f
by a homotopy, we can assume that it is cellular.
Since
Y
is a CW complex and
f
is cellular, it follows that the mapping cylinder
M(f)
is a CW complex containing
Y
as a subcomplex.
We will show that
(M,\partial M)\simeq(M(f),Y)
.

The homotopy equivalence
g\colon M\simeq X\hookrightarrow M(f)
restricts to a map
g|_{\partial M}\colon\partial M\to M(f)
such that the composition
Y\xrightarrow{\overline{h}}\partial M\xrightarrow{g|_{\partial M}}M(f)
is homotopic to the composition
Y\xrightarrow{f}X\hookrightarrow M(f)
.
The latter is homotopic to the inclusion
Y\hookrightarrow M(f)
.
It follows that
g|_{\partial M}
is homotopic to the composition
\partial M\xrightarrow{h}Y\hookrightarrow M(f)
.
Applying homotopy extension,
g
is homotopic to a homotopy equivalence
g^{\prime}\colon M\to M(f)
that restricts to the homotopy equivalence
h\colon\partial M\to Y
.
Since the maps
\partial M\to M
and
Y\to M(f)
are cofibrations, the map
(g^{\prime},h)\colon(M,\partial M)\to(M(f),Y)
is a homotopy equivalence of pairs by e.g.
[
Bro06
, 7.4.2]
.
∎

Lemma 3.2
.

For
n\geq 4
, every
n
-dimensional Poincaré pair
(X,Y)
is homotopy equivalent to a CW pair
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
such that
Y^{\prime}
is
(n-1)
-dimensional and
X^{\prime}
is
n
-dimensional.

Proof.

By definition of a Poincaré pair,
Y
is an
(n-1)
-dimensional Poincaré complex.
Next, since
H^{i}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(Y)])\cong H_{n-i-1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(Y)])=0
for
i>(n-1)
, a result of Wall
[
Wal66
, Corollary 5.1]

ensures that
Y
is homotopy equivalent to an
(n-1)
-dimensional CW complex
Y^{\prime}
.

If
Y
is empty, then the previous paragraph can be applied to
X
, establishing the lemma in this case.
Thus, from now on, we assume that
Y
is nonempty.
Set
\pi:=\pi_{1}(X)
and note that since
H^{i}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong H_{n-i}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
for
i>n
, a second application of
[
Wal66
, Corollary 5.1]
, ensures that
X
is homotopy equivalent to an
n
-complex
X^{\prime\prime}
.
Choose a homotopy equivalence
h\colon Y\to Y^{\prime}
, a homotopy inverse
\overline{h}\colon Y^{\prime}\to\partial Y
, and consider the map

f\colon Y^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\overline{h}}Y\hookrightarrow X\simeq X^{\prime\prime}.

By cellular approximation, after possibly changing
f
by a homotopy, we can assume that it is cellular.
Since
Y^{\prime}
is an
(n-1)
-dimensional CW complex, the mapping cylinder
M(f)
is an
n
-dimensional CW complex.
The exact same argument as the one in the last paragraph of the proof of Lemma
3.1
then produces a homotopy equivalences of pairs
(X,Y)\to(M(f),Y^{\prime})
.
∎

3.2.
The second homotopy group

We study
\pi_{2}(X)
as a
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)]
-module.
The main result, stated in Proposition
3.4
, describes a necessary and sufficient condition for it to be projective.

The next result serves as a first step towards deciding when
\pi_{2}(X)
is projective.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
(X,Y)
be a 4-dimensional Poincaré pair such that
\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(X)=:\pi
is surjective.
There exist finitely generated free
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-modules
C_{0}
,
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
and an exact sequence of
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-modules

0\to\pi_{2}(X)\to C_{2}\to C_{1}\to C_{0}\to\mathbb{Z}\to 0.

In particular,
\pi_{2}(X)
is free as an abelian group.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.2
, we can assume that
X
is obtained from
Y
by attaching cells of dimension at most
4
.
Since
\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(X)=:\pi
is surjective, we can furthermore assume that
X
is obtained from
Y
by attaching cells of dimension
2,3
and
4
(to see this, adapt the proof that
1
-handles can be traded for
3
-handles, as e.g. in
[
BKK+21
, page 258]
, with the birth and death of cancelling
1,2
-handles and
2,3
-handles replaced by elementary expanses and collapses of
1,2
-cells and
2,3
-cells).
It follows that
C^{1}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
and therefore

\pi_{2}(X)\cong H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong H^{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=\ker\left(d^{3}\colon C^{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to C^{3}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\right).

Since
H^{3}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong H_{1}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
and
H^{4}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong H_{0}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong\mathbb{Z}
, considering the free
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-modules
C_{i}:=C^{4-i}(X,Y,\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
provides the required exact sequence.
∎

The next proposition characterises when
\pi_{2}(X)
is projective.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
(X,Y)
be a 4-dimensional Poincaré pair such that
\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(X)=:\pi
is surjective.
The following assertions are equivalent:

•

the fundamental group
\pi
of
X
satisfies
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
,

•

the
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module
\pi_{2}(X)
is projective.

Proof.

If
\pi_{2}(X)
is projective, then the exact sequence from
Lemma

3.3
gives a
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-projective resolution of
\mathbb{Z}
of length
3
.
Hence
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
as needed.
Conversely, if
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
, then there is a projective resolution

0\to P_{3}\to P_{2}\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\to\mathbb{Z}\to 0
, and Schanuel’s lemma applied to the exact sequence from
Lemma

3.3

implies that
\pi_{2}(X)\oplus P_{2}\oplus C_{1}\oplus P_{0}\cong P_{3}\oplus C_{2}\oplus P_{1}\oplus C_{0}.

It follows that
\pi_{2}(X)
is projective, as required.
∎

3.3.
The cap product pairing

Let
(X,Y)
be a
4
-dimensional Poincaré pair such that the inclusion induced map
\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(X)=:\pi
is surjective, and let
c\colon X\to B
be a
3
-equivalence to its Postnikov
2
-type.
Given
\sigma\in H_{4}(B)
, we consider the pairing (which is hermitian by Lemma
A.9
)

\displaystyle b_{\sigma}\colon H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle\beta,\alpha\cap\sigma\rangle.

The goal of this section is to study the assignment
\sigma\mapsto b_{\sigma}
and to find conditions ensuring it is injective.
The first step is to relate
H_{4}(B)
to
\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}H_{4}(\widetilde{B})
.
Indeed, Whitehead’s exact sequence (from
[
Whi50
]
) implies that the latter group is isomorphic
\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\Gamma(\pi_{2}(B))
and is therefore related to hermitian forms on
H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
(X,Y)
be a
4
-dimensional Poincaré pair such that
\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(X)=:\pi
is surjective, and let
c\colon X\to B
be a
3
-equivalence to its Postnikov
2
-type.
If
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
or
\pi
is finite, then the projection induces an isomorphism

\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}H_{4}(\widetilde{B})\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{4}(B).

Proof.

Consider the Leray–Serre spectral sequence associated to the fibration
\widetilde{B}\to B\to B\pi
:

H_{p}(\pi;H_{q}(\widetilde{B}))\Rightarrow H_{p+q}(B).

Since
\widetilde{B}
is simply-connected,
H_{1}(\widetilde{B})=0
and the Hurewicz theorem gives a surjection

0=\pi_{3}(B)=\pi_{3}(\widetilde{B})\twoheadrightarrow H_{3}(\widetilde{B})

[
Hat02
, page 390]
so
H_{3}(\widetilde{B})=0
.

We first assume that
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
.
In this case,
H_{p}(\pi;H_{0}(\widetilde{B}))=0
for
p\geq 4
.
We also have
H_{p}(\pi;H_{2}(\widetilde{B}))=0
for
p\geq 1
because
Proposition

3.4
ensures that
H_{2}(\widetilde{B})\cong\pi_{2}(B)\cong\pi_{2}(X)
is projective.
This shows that
\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}H_{4}(\widetilde{B})
is the only nonzero term on the
p+q=4
-line of the spectral sequence.
This term survives to the infinity page since all potential differentials into it have trivial source.
The isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}H_{4}(\widetilde{B})\cong H_{4}(B)
follows.

Next, we assume that
\pi
is finite.
We first consider the Leray–Serre spectral sequence for the fibration
\widetilde{X}\to X\to B\pi
.
Since
\pi
is finite,
H^{1}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong H^{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
.
As
Y
is connected and
\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(X)
is surjective, we deduce that
H_{3}(\widetilde{X})\cong H_{3}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong H^{1}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0.

Also, note that
H_{i}(\widetilde{X})\cong H_{i}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong H^{4-i}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
for every
i\geq 4
, where for
i=4
we used that
Y\to X
is
\pi_{1}
-surjective.
Hence the only two non-trivial terms on the
p+q=n
-line are
H_{n-2}(\pi;H_{2}(\widetilde{X}))
and
H_{n}(\pi;H_{0}(\widetilde{X}))\cong H_{n}(\pi;\mathbb{Z})
.

Claim
.

For
n\geq 3
, the following third page differentials are isomorphisms:

d_{3}\colon H_{n}(\pi;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{n-3}(\pi;H_{2}(\widetilde{X})).

Proof.

We first note that the
d_{3}
differentials
H_{n}(\pi;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{n-3}(\pi;H_{2}(\widetilde{X}))
are injective for
n\geq 3
: otherwise
\ker(d_{3})
would contribute to
H_{n}(X)

via the infinity page. This gives a contradiction since
H_{i}(X)\cong H^{4-i}(X,Y)=0
for
i\geq 3
because
H_{1}(Y)\to H_{1}(X)
is surjective.
By
Lemma

3.3
, there is an exact sequence

0\to H_{2}(\widetilde{X})\to C_{2}\xrightarrow{d_{2}}C_{1}\xrightarrow{d_{1}}C_{0}\to\mathbb{Z}\to 0.

It follows by dimension shifting (i.e. by using
H_{i}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
for
i>0
and the long exact sequence associated to a short exact sequence of coefficients, see for example
[
Bro94
, III.(7.1)]
) that

H_{n}(\pi;H_{2}(\widetilde{X}))\cong H_{n+1}(\pi;\operatorname{im}d_{2})\cong H_{n+2}(\pi;\operatorname{im}d_{1})\cong H_{n+3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}).

Since
\pi
is finite, the modules
H_{n+3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z})
are finite for every
n\geq-2
. Hence the
d_{3}
differentials
H_{n}(\pi;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{n-3}(\pi;\pi_{2}(X))
are isomorphisms since they are injective maps between finite modules of the same cardinality.
This concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

Since
X\to B
is 3-connected, the
d_{3}
-differentials are also isomorphisms for
B
. It follows that on the
p+q=4
-line of the infinity page, only the term
\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}H_{4}(\widetilde{B})
survives. This gives the required result.
∎

We are now in position to relate

H_{4}(B)
to hermitian forms on
H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.
As explained in Proposition
3.5
, for
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
or
\pi
finite, we have
H_{4}(B)\cong\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}H_{4}(\widetilde{B})
and Whitehead’s exact sequence implies that this latter group is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\Gamma(\pi_{2}(B))

[
Whi50
]
.
We refer to Section
12.2
for a brief recollection of
\Gamma
-groups, but for the moment the definition can be blackboxed: we only require the fact that
\Gamma(\pi_{2}(B))
can be identified with the subgroup of symmetric elements of
\pi_{2}(B)\otimes\pi_{2}(B)
since
\pi_{2}(B)
is free as an abelian group by Lemma
3.3
.

In order to relate
\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\Gamma(\pi_{2}(B))
to hermitian forms on
H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, consider

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}\colon\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\Gamma(\pi_{2}(B))

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{Herm}(\pi_{2}(B)^{*})

\displaystyle 1\otimes\left(a\otimes b\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left((f,g)\mapsto\overline{f(b)}g(a)\right).

Indeed the target of
\mathcal{B}
can be related to
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
using the evaluation-induced map

\operatorname{Herm}(\operatorname{ev})\colon\operatorname{Herm}(\pi_{2}(B)^{*})\cong\operatorname{Herm}(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*})\to\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))

and the map
\operatorname{ev}
can be analysed using the universal coefficient spectral sequence.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
(X,Y)
be a
4
-dimensional Poincaré pair such that
\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(X)=:\pi
is surjective, and let
c\colon X\to B
be a
3
-equivalence to its Postnikov
2
-type.
Assume

•

either that
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
and
\operatorname{Herm}(\operatorname{ev})\colon\operatorname{Herm}(\pi_{2}(B)^{*})\to\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
is injective,

•

or that
\pi
is finite and that
\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\Gamma(\pi_{2}(B))
is torsion free,

then the following composition is injective:

\displaystyle b\colon H_{4}(B)

\displaystyle\xleftarrow{\cong}\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}]}H_{4}(\widetilde{B})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}]}\Gamma(\pi_{2}(B))\xrightarrow{\mathcal{B}}\operatorname{Herm}(\pi_{2}(B)^{*})\xrightarrow{}\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])).

Additionally it satisfies

(3.7)

b(\sigma)(x,y)=\langle y,x\cap\sigma\rangle.

Proof.

Proposition
3.5
shows that the first map is an isomorphism.
The second map is known to be an isomorphism thanks to Whitehead’s exact sequence
[
Whi50
]
.
If
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
, then
\pi_{2}(B)
is projective by
Proposition

3.4
. In this case, the map
\mathcal{B}
is injective by
[
Hil09
, Theorem 7 with addendum]
. If
\pi
is finite, the map
\mathcal{B}
is injective if and only if
\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\Gamma(\pi_{2}(B))
is torsion free by
[
HKPR25
, Proposition 6.15]
.
It remains to show that the last map is injective if
\pi
is finite. If
\pi
is finite, then
H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
and hence
H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}
is surjective. It follows that
\operatorname{Herm}(\pi_{2}(B)^{*})\to\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
is injective as needed.
Finally, the equality in (
3.7
) follows from the commutativity of the diagram in
[
HKPR25
, (2.7)]
.
∎

3.4.
Pushouts of Poincaré pairs

This section argues that the pushout of
n
-dimensional Poincaré pairs along a homotopy equivalence is an
n
-dimensional Poincaré complex.

Wall proved that if
(X_{0},Y)
and
(X_{1},Y)
are
n
-dimensional Poincaré pairs that are CW pairs,
then the union
X_{0}\cup_{Y}X_{1}
(which is necessarily a CW complex) is an
n
-dimensional Poincaré complex
[
Wal67
, Theorem 2.1 i)]
.
The next proposition builds on this result to include more general unions.

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
be
n
-dimensional Poincaré pairs.
If
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
is a homotopy equivalence, then the pushout
X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}
is an
n
-dimensional Poincaré complex.

Proof.

By
Lemma

3.2
, there are homotopy equivalences
(X_{i},Y_{i})\xrightarrow{(f_{i},g_{i})}(X_{i}^{\prime},Y_{i}^{\prime})
with
(X_{i}^{\prime},Y_{i}^{\prime})
a CW pair for
i=0,1
.
Let
\overline{g}_{0}
be a homotopy inverse of
g_{0}
, and let
h^{\prime}\colon Y_{0}^{\prime}\to Y_{1}^{\prime}
be a cellular map that is homotopic to
g_{1}\circ h\circ\overline{g}_{0}
. The mapping cylinder
M(h^{\prime})
of
h^{\prime}
is a CW complex that contains
Y_{0}^{\prime}
as a subcomplex.
Since the projection
(\operatorname{pr},h^{\prime})\colon(M(h^{\prime}),Y_{0}^{\prime})\to(X_{1}^{\prime},Y_{1}^{\prime})
is a homotopy equivalence,
(M(h^{\prime}),Y_{0}^{\prime})
is a Poincaré pair.
By
[
Wal67
, Theorem 2.1 i)]
,
X_{0}^{\prime}\cup_{Y_{0}^{\prime}}M(h^{\prime})
is a Poincaré complex.
It remains to show that
X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}
and
X_{0}^{\prime}\cup_{Y_{0}^{\prime}}M(h^{\prime})
are homotopy equivalent.

By definition of
h^{\prime}
, there is a homotopy,
h^{\prime}\circ g_{0}\simeq f_{1}|\circ h\colon Y_{0}\to X_{1}
.
By homotopy extension, there exists a map
f_{1}^{\prime}\colon X_{1}\to X_{1}^{\prime}
that is homotopic to
f_{1}
and such that
h^{\prime}\circ g_{0}=f_{1}^{\prime}\circ h
.
Therefore the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{0}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(h^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{0}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Since
Y_{0}\to X_{0}
and
Y_{0}^{\prime}\to X_{0}^{\prime}
are cofibrations, the gluing lemma (see e.g.
[
MP12
, Lemma 2.1.3]
)
then ensures that there are homotopy equivalences

X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}\simeq X_{0}^{\prime}\cup_{h^{\prime}}X_{1}^{\prime}\simeq X_{0}^{\prime}\cup_{Y_{0}^{\prime}}M(h^{\prime}).

Thus
X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}
is a Poincaré complex, as required.
∎

We conclude with an observation concerning the fundamental group of such pushouts.

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
be two
n
-dimensional Poincaré pairs with isomorphic fundamental groups, and let
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
be a homotopy equivalence.
If
\iota_{j}\colon\pi_{1}(Y_{i})\to\pi_{1}(X_{i})
is surjective for
j=0,1
and if there is an isomorphism
u\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\pi_{1}(X_{1})
with
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
, then the inclusion
induces an isomorphism

\pi_{1}(X_{1})\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi_{1}(X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}).

Proof.

Since
X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}
is a pushout, so is
\pi_{1}(X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1})
.
Using that
\iota_{0}
and
\iota_{1}
are surjective, a direct verification shows that
\pi_{1}(X_{1})
also satisfies the universal property of the pushout.
The proposition then follows readily.
∎

Remark 3.10
.

We fix a choice of fundamental class for
X_{0}\cup_{h}\scalebox{0.75}[1.0]{$-$}X_{1}
.
Since
h
is orientation-preserving, under the composition

H_{4}(X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1})\to H_{4}(X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1},Y_{1})\xleftarrow{\cong}H_{4}(X_{0},Y_{0})\oplus H_{4}(X_{1},Y_{1}),

a fundamental class of
X_{0}\cup_{h}\scalebox{0.75}[1.0]{$-$}X_{1}
maps either to
(\scalebox{0.75}[1.0]{$-$}[X_{0}],[X_{1}])
or to
([X_{0}],\scalebox{0.75}[1.0]{$-$}[X_{1}])
.
In what follows, we pick the latter choice and occasionally write
X_{0}\cup_{h}\scalebox{0.75}[1.0]{$-$}X_{1}
for emphasis.

3.5.
An extension lemma

This short section records a technical lemma which is a small generalisation of the following statement “given a space
X_{0}
, if a map
c_{1}\colon X_{1}\to\penalty\ B
is
n
-connected, then the induced map
(c_{1})_{*}\colon[X_{0}^{(n)},X_{1}]\to[X_{0},B]
is surjective for any CW complex
X_{0}
”

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
(X_{0},D_{0})
be an
n
-dimensional relative CW-space, i.e
X_{0}
is obtained from
D_{0}
by attaching cells of dimension at most
n
.
Let
c_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B
be an
n
-connected map. Let
D_{1}\subseteq X_{1}
and let
h\colon D_{0}\to D_{1}
be given. For every map
c_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B
such that
c_{0}|_{D_{0}}\simeq c_{1}|_{D_{1}}\circ h
there exists a map
f\colon X_{0}\to X_{1}
extending
h
such that
c_{0}\simeq c_{1}\circ f
.

Proof.

We argue that without loss of generality we can assume that
c_{1}
is an inclusion and
c_{0}|_{D_{0}}=h
.
