By taking the mapping cylinder of
c_{1}
, we can assume that
c_{1}
is an inclusion: indeed writing
\operatorname{proj}\colon M(c_{1})\xrightarrow{\simeq}B
, if
h
extends to
f\colon X_{0}\to X_{1}
with
\operatorname{incl}_{B}\circ c_{0}\simeq\operatorname{incl}_{X_{1}}\circ f\colon X_{0}\to M(c_{1})
, then
c_{1}\circ f=\operatorname{proj}\circ\operatorname{incl}_{X_{1}}\circ f=\operatorname{proj}\circ\operatorname{incl}_{B}\circ c_{0}\simeq c_{0}.

We can further assume that
c_{0}|_{D_{0}}=h
:
homotopy extension
ensures that
h\colon D_{0}\to Y_{1}\subset B
extends to a map
c_{0}^{\prime}\colon X_{0}\to B
that is homotopic to
c_{0}
, so if
h
extends to
f\colon X_{0}\to X_{1}
with
c_{1}\circ h\simeq c_{0}^{\prime}
, then
c_{1}\circ h\simeq c_{0}
.

Since we are now assuming that
c_{1}
is the inclusion and
c_{0}|_{D_{0}}=h
, the map
c_{0}
determines a map of pairs
c_{0}\colon(X_{0},D_{0})\to(B,X_{1})
.
Since
c_{1}
is
n
-connected
[
Hat02
, Lemma 4.6]
implies that the map
c_{0}\colon(X_{0},D_{0})\to(B,X_{1})
is homotopic relative
D_{0}
to a map
f\colon X_{0}\to X_{1}
.
Since the homotopy is relative
D_{0}
, it follows that
f
extends
h
as required.
∎

4.
The primary and secondary obstructions

The next two sections aim to prove Theorem
2.8
.
Recall that given
4
-dimensional Poincaré pairs
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
and a homotopy equivalence
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
, this theorem describes two successive obstructions to
h
extending to a homotopy equivalence
X_{0}\to X_{1}
.
Before describing our strategy, we fix some notation.

Notation 4.1
.

Let
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
be
4
-dimensional Poincaré pairs with
\pi_{1}(X_{j})\cong\pi
, Postnikov
2
-type
B:=P_{2}(X_{1})
and
\iota_{j}\colon\pi_{1}(Y_{j})\to\pi_{1}(X_{j})
surjective for
j=0,1
.
Fix a degree one homotopy equivalence
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
.
Given
3
-connected maps
c_{j}\colon X_{j}\to B
for
j=0,1
, when we write
(B,X_{1})
or
(B,Y_{1})
, it is understood that we have replaced
B
by the mapping cylinder of
X_{1}\to B
.
We additionally assume that
c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h=c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}
.
Recall that that this ensures that
u:=(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}\circ(c_{0})^{*}\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\pi_{1}(X_{1})
satisfies
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
.

Section
4.1
reduces the extension problem to one concerning an equality of fundamental classes.
Section
4.2
then recasts this later problem in terms of relative intersection forms.

4.1.
Reduction to fundamental classes

We reduce the problem of extending the homotopy equivalence
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
to a question about the fundamental classes of
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
.
The argument is inspired by work of Hambleton-Kreck
[
HK88
]
.

We first describe a sufficient condition for a map extending
h
to be a homotopy equivalence.

Lemma 4.2
.

If a map
f\colon X_{0}\to X_{1}
extends
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
and satisfies
c_{1}\circ f\simeq c_{0}
, then it is a homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

First,
f
induces an isomorphism on
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
because
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
are
3
-connected.
Next, since
(X_{i},Y_{i})
is
1
-connected, we obtain
H_{k}(X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong H^{4-k}(X_{i},Y_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
for
k=3,4
.
Whitehead’s theorem implies that
f
is a homotopy equivalence.
∎

The next proposition shows that an equality involving fundamental classes ensures that the sufficient condition form Lemma
4.2
is satisfied

Proposition 4.3
.

If the maps
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
satisfy

(c_{0})_{*}([X_{0}])=(c_{1})_{*}([X_{1}])\in H_{4}(B,Y_{1}),

then there exists a map
f\colon X_{0}\to X_{1}
extending
h
such that
c_{1}\circ f\simeq c_{0}
.

Proof.

Since
c_{1}
is
3
-connected, Lemma
3.11
gives the existence of a map
f\colon X_{0}^{(3)}\to X_{1}^{(3)}\subset X_{1}
that restricts to
h
on
Y_{0}
and such that
c_{1}\circ f\simeq c_{0}.

The homotopy extension property (applied to
c_{1}\circ f\simeq c_{0}
on
X_{0}^{(3)}
) ensures the existence of a
3
-connected map
c_{0}^{\prime}\simeq c_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B
such that
c_{0}^{\prime}|_{X_{0}^{(3)}}=c_{1}|_{X_{1}^{(3)}}\circ f
.
Without loss of generality we can therefore assume that
c_{1}\circ f=c_{0}
on
X_{0}^{(3)}
.
Consider the obstruction theoretic problem of extending
f
over
X_{0}
rel
h
.

Using Lemma
3.2
we can assume that
X_{0}
is obtained from
Y_{0}
by attaching cells of dimension at most
4
.
Indeed, if there is a homotopy equivalence of pairs
(X_{0},Y_{0})\simeq(X_{0}^{\prime},Y_{0}^{\prime})
, we write
g\colon Y_{0}\simeq Y_{0}^{\prime}
for the induced homotopy equivalence, choose a homotopy inverse
\overline{g}
, and note that if
h\circ\overline{g}\circ g\colon Y_{0}^{\prime}\to Y_{1}
extends, then by homotopy extension applied to
h\circ\overline{g}\circ g\simeq h
, so does
h
.

Since
f
is defined on the whole of
X_{0}
apart from on the
4
-cells, the primary obstruction to our extension problem is a class in
H^{4}(X_{0},\partial X_{0};\pi_{3}(X_{1}))
whose vanishing ensures that
f|_{X_{0}^{(2)}\cup\partial X_{0}}
extends to a map
f\colon X_{0}\to X_{1}
rel
h
.
This extension would then automatically satisfy
c_{1}\circ f\simeq c_{0}
:
since
\pi_{3}(B)
and
\pi_{4}(B)
vanish, any homotopy
c_{1}\circ f|_{X_{0}^{(2)}}\simeq c_{0}|_{X_{0}^{(2)}}
can be extended rel.
h
to a homotopy
c_{1}\circ f\simeq c_{0}
.

We now analyse the obstruction in
H^{4}(X_{0},\partial X_{0};\pi_{3}(X_{1}))
.
The composition of the Hurewicz map and the map
\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}H_{4}(B,X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{4}(B,X_{1};\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
induces an isomorphism

\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\pi_{4}(B,X_{1})\cong\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}H_{4}(B,X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong H_{4}(B,X_{1}).

Here, the relative Hurewicz theorem applies because
c
is
3
-connected while the second isomorphism follows from the UCSS because
H_{i}(B,X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
for
i\leq 3
.

Since
B
is
3
-coconnected, we obtain the following composition of isomorphisms:

\displaystyle H^{4}(X_{0},Y_{0};\pi_{3}(X_{1}))

\displaystyle\cong H^{4}(X_{0},Y_{0};\pi_{4}(B,X_{1}))\cong H_{0}(X_{0};\pi_{4}(B,X_{1}))\cong\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\pi_{4}(B,X_{1})

\displaystyle\cong H_{4}(B,X_{1}).

We determine the image of the obstruction class in
H_{4}(B,X_{1})
through these isomorphisms.

Claim
.

The image of the obstruction class in
H_{4}(B,X_{1})
is given by

(c_{0})_{*}([X_{0}])\in\operatorname{im}(H_{4}(B,Y_{1})\to H_{4}(B,X_{1})).

Proof.

Write
[X_{0}]=\sum_{e}n_{e}\otimes\widetilde{e}\in C_{4}^{\operatorname{CW}}(X_{0},Y_{0};\mathbb{Z})
, where
n_{e}\in\mathbb{Z}
and the sum is taken over all
4
-cells
e
of
X_{0}\setminus Y_{0}
with
\widetilde{e}
its lift to the universal cover (with respect to the fixed basepoint).
Let
\alpha_{e}\colon S^{3}\to X_{0}^{(3)}\cup Y_{0}
be the attaching map of
e
.
The obstruction cocycle is the homomorphism
C_{4}(\widetilde{X}_{0},\widetilde{Y}_{0})\to\pi_{3}(X_{1})
that maps
\widetilde{e}
to the homotopy class
[S^{3}\xrightarrow{\widetilde{\alpha}_{e}}\widetilde{X}_{0}^{(3)}\cup\widetilde{Y}_{0}\xrightarrow{\widetilde{f}}\widetilde{X}_{1}]\in\pi_{3}(X_{1}).

We use
\theta(f)
to denote the cohomology class of this cocycle.

We describe a cocycle representative for the image of
[\theta(f)]
in
H^{4}(X_{0},Y_{0};\pi_{4}(B,X_{1}))
.
Consider the commutative diagram

\textstyle{S^{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\alpha}_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\subset}
\textstyle{\widetilde{X}_{0}^{(3)}\cup\widetilde{Y}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{X}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{c}_{1}}
\textstyle{D^{4}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{c}_{0}|_{\widetilde{e}}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{B}}

to see that
\widetilde{e}\mapsto\widetilde{c}_{0}|_{\widetilde{e}}
determines the required cocycle and use
\varphi
to denotes its cohomology class.

We describe the image of this cohomology class in
H_{0}(X_{0},Y_{0};\pi_{4}(B,X_{1}))\cong\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\pi_{4}(B,X_{1})
.
By definition this class is
\operatorname{PD}_{X_{0}}([\varphi])=\varphi\cap[X_{0}]
.
We deduce that

\operatorname{PD}_{X_{0}}([\varphi])=\varphi\cap\left[\sum_{e}n_{e}\otimes\widetilde{e}\right]=\left[\sum_{e}n_{e}\otimes\varphi(\widetilde{e})\right]=\left[\sum_{e}n_{e}\otimes\widetilde{c}_{0}|_{\widetilde{e}}\right].

Under the Hurewicz map,
\widetilde{c}_{0}|_{\widetilde{e}}\in\pi_{4}(B,X_{1})
is sent to
(c_{0})_{*}(\widetilde{e})\in H_{4}(B,X_{1})
,
so
\operatorname{PD}_{X_{0}}([\varphi])
is sent to

\sum_{e}n_{e}\otimes(c_{0})_{*}(\widetilde{e})=(c_{0})_{*}\left(\sum_{e}n_{e}\otimes\widetilde{e}\right)=(c_{0})_{*}([X_{0}]).

This concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

We now assume that
(c_{0})_{*}([X_{0}])=(c_{1})_{*}([X_{1}])\in H_{4}(B,Y_{1})
and conclude the proof of the proposition.
The long exact sequence of the triple
(B,X_{1},Y_{1})
ensures that

(c_{1})_{*}([X_{1}])=0\in\operatorname{im}(H_{4}(B,Y_{1})\to H_{4}(B,X_{1})).

Combining these two equalities implies that

(c_{0})_{*}([X_{0}])=0\in\operatorname{im}(H_{4}(B,Y_{1})\to H_{4}(B,X_{1})).

The claim then implies that the required obstruction vanishes and the preceding explanation guarantees that
h
extends to a map
f\colon X_{0}\to X_{1}
with
c_{1}\circ f\simeq c_{0}
.
∎

These results yield a tractable sufficient condition for
h
to extend to a homotopy equivalence.

Proposition 4.4
.

If the maps
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
satisfy

(c_{0})_{*}([X_{0}])=(c_{1})_{*}([X_{1}])\in H_{4}(B,Y_{1}),

then
h
extends to a homotopy equivalence
f\colon X_{0}\to X_{1}
with
c_{1}\circ f\simeq c_{0}
.

Proof.

Proposition
4.3
proves the existence of a map
f\colon X_{0}\to X_{1}
extending
h
such that
c_{1}\circ f\simeq c_{0}
.
Lemma
4.2
then implies that
f
is a homotopy equivalence.
∎

The next proposition recasts this sufficient condition in terms of the vanishing of a class in
H_{4}(B)
, thus placing us in a position to apply Proposition
3.6
.

Proposition 4.5
.

There is a unique homology class
\xi_{0}\in H_{4}(B)
, satisfying

j_{*}(\xi_{0})=(c_{0})_{*}([X_{0}])-(c_{1})_{*}([X_{1}])\in H_{4}(B,Y_{1}).

In particular, we have

\xi_{0}=0\ \ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ \ (c_{0})_{*}([X_{0}])=(c_{1})_{*}([X_{1}])\in H_{4}(B,Y_{1}).

In fact,
\xi_{0}=c_{*}([U])
, where
(U,c):=(X_{0}\cup_{h}\scalebox{0.75}[1.0]{$-$}X_{1},c:=c_{0}\cup c_{1})
.

Proof.

Consider the connecting map
\partial_{B}\colon H_{4}(B,Y_{1})\to H_{3}(Y_{1})
as well as the corresponding connecting maps
\partial_{0}
and
\partial_{1}
for the pairs
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
.
Since
h
has degree one,
a direct calculation shows that

\displaystyle\partial_{B}\circ(c_{0})_{*}([X_{0}])-\partial_{B}\circ(c_{1})_{*}([X_{1}])

\displaystyle=h_{*}\circ\partial_{0}([X_{0}])-\partial_{1}([X_{1}])=h_{*}([Y_{0}])-[Y_{1}]=0.

The long exact sequence of the pair
(B,Y_{1})
now ensures that the required
\xi_{0}\in H_{4}(B)
exists.
The uniqueness of
\xi_{0}
as well as the equivalence involving the vanishing of
\xi_{0}
follow because the inclusion induced map
H_{4}(B)\to H_{4}(B,Y_{1})
is injective.

Finally, we argue that
j_{*}\circ c_{*}([U])=(c_{0})_{*}([X_{0}])-(c_{1})_{*}([X_{1}])\in H_{4}(B,Y_{1})
as the uniqueness of
\xi_{0}
will then imply that
\xi_{0}=c_{*}([U])
.
Consider the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{H_{4}(U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{4}(U,Y_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{4}(X_{0},Y_{0})\oplus H_{4}(X_{1},Y_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{((c_{0})_{*},(c_{1})_{*})}
\textstyle{H_{4}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{4}(B,Y_{1}).}

The top composition sends
[U]
to
[X_{0}]-[X_{1}]
.
The conclusion now follows readily.
∎

We record the following observation on the hermitian form
b_{\xi_{0}}(x,y)=\langle x,y\cap\xi_{0}\rangle
on
H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.

Proposition 4.6
.

The hermitian form
b_{\xi_{0}}
satisfies
b_{\xi_{0}}=c^{*}b_{U}.

Proof.

Proposition
4.5
ensures that
\xi_{0}=c_{*}([U])
.
Now, given
x,y\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, the result follows from a calculation:
b_{\xi_{0}}(x,y)=\langle x,y\cap\xi_{0}\rangle=\langle x,y\cap c_{*}([U])\rangle=\langle c^{*}(x),c^{*}(y)\cap[U]\rangle=c^{*}b_{X}(x,y).

∎

4.2.
From fundamental classes to intersection forms

We continue with Notation
4.1
.
Additionally, as in Proposition
4.5
, we consider the unique homology class
\xi_{0}\in H_{4}(B)
satisfying

j_{*}(\xi_{0})=(c_{0})_{*}([X_{0}])-(c_{1})_{*}([X_{1}])\in H_{4}(B,Y_{1}).

Proposition
4.5
shows that
\xi_{0}\in H_{4}(B)
is an obstruction to
h
extending to a homotopy equivalence.
Proposition
3.6
states that the problem of deciding whether
\xi_{0}
vanishes can be expressed terms of the pairing
b_{\xi_{0}}(x,y)=\langle x,y\cap\xi_{0}\rangle
on
H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.
This section recasts the pairing
b_{\xi_{0}}
in terms of the relative intersection forms of
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
.
The outcome, namely Proposition
4.9
, is a preliminary version of Theorem
2.8
from the introduction.

The strategy outlined above relies on relative intersection forms.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
(X,Y)
be a
4
-dimensional Poincaré pair with
\pi:=\pi_{1}(X)
.

•

The
cohomological relative equivariant intersection form
is

\displaystyle b_{X}^{\partial}\colon H^{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle\beta,\operatorname{PD}_{X}(\alpha)\rangle=\langle\beta,\alpha\cap[X]\rangle.

•

The
relative equivariant intersection form
is

\displaystyle\lambda_{X}^{\partial}\colon H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H_{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle\operatorname{PD}_{X}^{-1}(y),x\rangle.

The appendix discusses these pairings in more detail but, for now, we return to the pairing
b_{\xi_{0}}
and collect some of its properties.

Proposition 4.8
.

The pairing
b_{\xi_{0}}\colon H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi],(x,y)\mapsto\langle x,y\cap\xi_{0}\rangle
satisfies the following properties.

•

Consider the map

j^{*}\colon H^{2}(B,Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).

For every
x\in H^{2}(B,Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and
y\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
,

b_{\xi_{0}}(j^{*}(x),y)=b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}(c_{0}^{*}(x),c_{0}^{*}(y))-b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}(c_{1}^{*}(x),c_{1}^{*}(y)).

•

If
b_{\xi_{0}}(j^{*}(-),-)\equiv 0
, then
b_{\xi_{0}}
induces a hermitian form

\displaystyle b(c_{0},c_{1})\colon H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\xleftarrow{c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}^{*}\times c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}^{*},\cong}\frac{H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}{\operatorname{im}(j^{*})}\times\frac{H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}{\operatorname{im}(j^{*})}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{b_{\xi_{0}}}\mathbb{Z}[\pi].

Proof.

The first point follows from a direct calculation involving the definition of
b_{\xi_{0}}
, the naturality of the cap product, the definition of
\xi_{0}
, and the naturality of the evaluation map:

\displaystyle b_{\xi_{0}}(j^{*}(x),y)

\displaystyle=\langle y,j^{*}(x)\cap\xi_{0}\rangle=\langle y,x\cap j_{*}(\xi_{0})\rangle=\langle y,x\cap((c_{0})_{*}([X_{0}])-(c_{1})_{*}([X_{1}]))\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle c_{0}^{*}(y),c_{0}^{*}(x)\cap[X_{0}]\rangle-\langle c_{1}^{*}(y),c_{1}^{*}(x)\cap[X_{1}]\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle c_{0}^{*}(y),\operatorname{PD}_{X_{0}}(c_{0}^{*}(x))\rangle-\langle c_{1}^{*}(y),\operatorname{PD}_{X_{1}}(c_{1}^{*}(x))\rangle=b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}(c_{0}^{*}(x),c_{0}^{*}(y))-b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}(c_{1}^{*}(x),c_{1}^{*}(y)).

We prove the second assertion.
If
b_{\xi_{0}}(j^{*}(-),-)\equiv 0
, then
b_{\xi_{0}}
induces a pairing

\frac{H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}{\operatorname{im}(j^{*})}\times H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi].

A direct calculation using the fact that
b_{\xi_{0}}
is hermitian shows that
b_{\xi_{0}}(x+j^{*}(a),y+j^{*}(b))=b_{\xi_{0}}(x,y)
.
It follows that
b_{\xi_{0}}
in fact descends to a pairing on
H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])/\operatorname{im}(j^{*})\times H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])/\operatorname{im}(j^{*})
.

Next, we claim that
c_{1}^{*}\colon H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is surjective.
Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{3}(B,Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{3}(X_{1},Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

and note that
H^{3}(X_{1},Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong H_{1}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
by Poincaré duality. It follows that
H^{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is surjective and hence so is
c_{1}^{*}\colon H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, as claimed.

The claim implies that there is is an exact sequence

H^{2}(B,Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\xrightarrow{j^{*}}H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\xrightarrow{c_{1}^{*}}H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to 0

and therefore
c_{1}^{*}
induces an isomorphism
H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])/\operatorname{im}(j^{*})\cong H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),
as required.
This concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Recall from Definitions
2.4
and
2.7
that we occasionally refer to
c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}-c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
as the
primary obstruction
and that, when this pairing vanishes, the hermitian form
b(c_{0},c_{1})
leads to the
secondary obstruction
.
The terminology is motivated by the following proposition which shows that the vanishing of these pairings
leads to our first sufficient condition for
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
to extend to a homotopy equivalence
X_{0}\to X_{1}
.

Proposition 4.9
.

Assume

•

either that
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
and
\operatorname{Herm}(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*})\to\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
is injective,

•

or
\pi_{1}(X_{i})
is finite and
\Gamma(\pi_{2}(X))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\mathbb{Z}
is torsion free.

If there exists a
3
-connected map
c_{i}\colon X_{i}\to B
for
i=0,1
such that
c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h=c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}
and

(1)

the pulled back pairings
c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}
and
c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
on
H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
are equal,

(2)

the hermitian form
b(c_{0},c_{1})
on
H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is the zero pairing,

then
\xi_{0}=0\in H_{4}(B)
and thus
h
extends to a homotopy equivalence
f\colon X_{0}\to X_{1}
with
c_{1}\circ f\simeq c_{0}
.

Proof.

Since
c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
on
H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, Proposition
4.8
implies that
b(c_{0},c_{1})
is defined.
If
b(c_{0},c_{1})
vanishes, then Proposition
4.8
implies that
b_{\xi_{0}}
vanishes as well.
The assumptions of the present proposition together with Proposition
3.6
then ensure that
\xi_{0}=0\in H_{4}(B).

Proposition
4.3
implies that
(c_{0})_{*}([X_{0}])=(c_{1})_{*}([X_{1}])\in H_{4}(B,Y_{1})
.
Proposition
4.4
now implies that
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
are homotopy equivalent over
B
rel
h
.
∎

5.
The action on hermitian forms and the proof of Theorem
2.8

This section, which continues with Notation
4.1
, aims to prove Theorem
2.8
.
Proposition
4.9
shows that
c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}-c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
constitutes a first obstruction to extending
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
to a homotopy equivalence, and suggests the hermitian form
b(c_{0},c_{1})\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
constitutes a secondary obstruction.
This section constructs a group
\mathcal{G}
and an
action
\mathcal{G}\curvearrowright\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
so that for a given
c_{1}
, the vanishing of
b(c_{0},c_{1})
in
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}
does not depend on the choice of

c_{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}(c_{1}):=\{c_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B\mid c_{0}\text{ is }3\text{-connected},c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h=c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}\text{ and }c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}\}\Big/\simeq.

Theorem
2.8
then follows readily from this construction and Proposition
4.9
.

In what follows, given a pair
(X,Y)
, we write

\displaystyle\operatorname{hAut}(X)=\{\varphi\colon X\to X\mid\varphi\text{ is a homotopy equivalence}\}/\simeq

\displaystyle\operatorname{hAut}_{Y}(X)=\{\varphi\colon X\to X\mid\varphi\text{ is a homotopy equivalence rel. }Y\}/\simeq\text{ rel. Y}.

Any homotopy equivalence
\varphi\colon B\to B
rel.
Y_{1}
is readily seen to induce the identity on
\pi_{1}(B).

In particular, any element of
\operatorname{hAut}_{Y_{1}}(B)
induces isomorphisms on the
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-homology of
B
.

Construction 5.1

(The group
\mathcal{G}
)
.

Consider the set

\mathcal{G}=\{\varphi\in\operatorname{hAut}_{Y_{1}}(B)\mid(\varphi\circ c_{1})^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}\}.

We leave it to the reader to verify that
\mathcal{G}
is a group under composition.

The following two lemmas serve as preparatory results for the verification that
\mathcal{G}
acts on
\mathcal{S}_{0}(c_{1})
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
(X,Y)
be a pair of spaces, and let
B
be a
3
-coconnected space.
If
f,f’\colon X\to B
are
3
-connected maps with
c:=f|_{Y}=f^{\prime}|_{Y}
both an inclusion and a cofibration,
then there is a homotopy equivalence
\phi\colon B\to B
that is the identity on
c(Y)
and such that
f^{\prime}\simeq\phi\circ f
relative
Y
.

Proof.

Let
\phi’\colon B\to B
be a homotopy equivalence such that
f’\simeq\phi’\circ f
and let
\mathbf{H}\colon f’\simeq\phi’\circ f
be a homotopy.
Restricting to
Y
produces a homotopy
\mathbf{H}|_{Y}\colon c\simeq\phi’\circ c
.
Since
c
is an inclusion, we can instead view this as a homotopy from
\operatorname{id}_{c(Y)}
to
\phi^{\prime}|_{c(Y)}
.
Use the homotopy extension property to extend
\mathbf{H}|_{Y}
to a homotopy
\mathbf{G}=(G_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}\colon B\times I\to B
between
G_{1}=\phi^{\prime}
and
a map
\phi:=G_{0}
that satisfies
\phi|_{c(Y)}=\operatorname{id}_{c(Y)}
.
Note also that
\phi
is a homotopy equivalence since it is homotopic to a homotopy equivalence.

The concatenation
\mathbf{H}^{\prime}\colon X\times I\to B
of the homotopies
\mathbf{H}\colon f’\simeq\phi’\circ f
and
\overline{\mathbf{G}}\circ f:=(G_{1-t}\circ f)_{t\in[0,1]}\colon\phi\circ f\simeq\phi’\circ f
produces a homotopy
f’\simeq\phi\circ f.

This is not yet the homotopy we require since it is not rel.
Y
.
Indeed on the boundary it is the map
Y\times I\to B
given by the concatenation of
\mathbf{H}|_{Y}
and its “reverse”
\overline{\mathbf{H}}_{Y}
.
This concatenation restricts to the identity on
Y\times\{0,1\}

and is homotopic relative
Y\times\{0,1\}
to the homotopy
\mathbf{I}\colon Y\times I\to B
given by
I_{t}=c
for every
t\in[0,1]
.
This latter homotopy is denoted
\mathbf{F}\colon Y\times I\times I\to B
.

Finally, we construct the rel.
Y
homotopy
f^{\prime}\simeq\phi\circ f
.
Let
\mathbf{F}^{\prime}\colon X\times\{0,1\}\times I\to B
be given by
\mathbf{F}^{\prime}(x,0,t)=f^{\prime}(x)
and
\mathbf{F}^{\prime}(x,1,t)=\phi\circ f^{\prime}(x)
and consider the map

\mathbf{H}^{\prime}\cup\mathbf{F}\cup\mathbf{F}^{\prime}\colon(X\times I\times\{0\})\cup\overbrace{(Y\times I\times I)\cup(X\times\{0,1\}\times I)}^{=\left((Y\times I)\cup(X\times\{0,1\})\right)\times I=:A\times I}\to B.

The homotopy extension property of
(X\times I,A)
ensures that it is possible to extend
\mathbf{F}\cup\mathbf{F}^{\prime}\colon A\times I\to B
to a map
(X\times I)\times I\to B
that agrees with
\mathbf{H}^{\prime}
on
X\times I\times\{0\}
.
Restricting this map to
X\times I\times\{1\}
gives the required homotopy
f’\simeq\phi\circ f
rel.
Y
.
This homotopy is indeed relative
Y
since on
Y\times I
it agrees with
\mathbf{F}_{1}=\mathbf{I}
by construction.
∎

We require the following strengthening of
Lemma

5.2
that weakens the requirement that the map
c\colon Y\to B
be an inclusion.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
(X,Y)
be a pair of spaces, let
B
be a
3
-coconnected space, and let
(B,Y^{\prime})
be a pair with
Y^{\prime}\subset B
a cofibration.
If
f,f’\colon X\to B
are
3
-connected maps with

c:=f|_{Y}=f^{\prime}|_{Y}\colon Y\to Y^{\prime}\subseteq B

a homotopy equivalence, then there is a homotopy equivalence
\phi\colon B\to B
that is the identity on
Y^{\prime}
and such that
f^{\prime}\simeq\phi\circ f
relative
Y
.

Proof.

Choose a homotopy inverse
c^{-1}\colon Y^{\prime}\to Y
of
c
and a homotopy
\mathbf{G}\colon Y^{\prime}\times I\to Y^{\prime}
from
c\circ c^{-1}
to
\operatorname{id}_{Y^{\prime}}
.
Consider the inclusion
(X,Y)\to(M(i\circ c^{-1}),Y^{\prime})
, where
M(i\circ c^{-1})
is the mapping cylinder of
Y^{\prime}\xrightarrow{c^{-1}}Y\xrightarrow{i}X
.
Taking
\mathbf{G}
on
Y^{\prime}\times I
, the maps
f
and
f^{\prime}
extend to maps

\widehat{f}:=f\cup\mathbf{G},\widehat{f}^{\prime}:=f^{\prime}\cup\mathbf{G}\colon(M(i\circ c^{-1}),Y^{\prime})\to(B,Y^{\prime})

that restrict to the identity on
Y^{\prime}
.
Here we are using the convention that the mapping cylinder of a map
g\colon C\to D
is
M(g):=(D\cup(C\times[0,1]))/\sim
, where
(c,0)\sim f(c)
.

By Lemma
5.2
, there is a homotopy equivalence
\phi\colon B\to B
that is the identity on
Y^{\prime}
and such that
\widehat{f}^{\prime}\simeq\phi\circ\widehat{f}
rel.
Y^{\prime}
, say via
\mathbf{H}=(H_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}\colon M(ic^{-1})\times I\to B
. Restricting
\mathbf{H}
to
X
gives a homotopy
\mathbf{H}|_{X}
from
f^{\prime}
to
\phi\circ f
. This homotopy is not yet rel.
Y
.

The remainder of the proof is devoted to showing that
\mathbf{H}|_{X}
is homotopic rel.
X\times\{0,1\}
to a homotopy
f^{\prime}\simeq\phi\circ f
that is rel.
Y
.
Write
M(c^{-1})
for the mapping cylinder of
c^{-1}\colon Y^{\prime}\to Y
and consider the map

\displaystyle\widehat{c}\colon Y\times I

\displaystyle\to M(c^{-1})

\displaystyle(y,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}(c(y),t)&\quad\text{ if }t>0,\\
(c^{-1}\circ c(y),0)&\quad\text{ if }t=0.\end{cases}

We also consider the homotopy

\mathbf{G}^{\prime}:=\widehat{f}\circ\widehat{c}\colon Y\times I\to B

from
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}_{0}=\widehat{f}\circ(c^{-1}\circ c,0)=c\circ(c^{-1}\circ c)
to
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}_{1}=\widehat{f}\circ(c,1)=c
,
and the composition

(5.4)

(Y\times I)\times I\xrightarrow{\widehat{c}\times\operatorname{id}_{I}}M(c^{-1})\times I\xrightarrow{\mathbf{H}|}B.

We have
\widehat{f}^{\prime}\circ\widehat{c}=\widehat{f}\circ\widehat{c}
since
\widehat{f}
and
\widehat{f}^{\prime}
are given by
\mathbf{G}
on
Y^{\prime}\times I
, and since
\mathbf{G}
takes values in
Y^{\prime}
and
\phi|_{Y^{\prime}}=\operatorname{id}_{Y^{\prime}}
, we also have
\phi\circ\widehat{f}\circ\widehat{c}=\widehat{f}\circ\widehat{c}
.
Hence (
5.4
) is a homotopy from
H_{0}\circ\widehat{c}=\widehat{f}^{\prime}\circ\widehat{c}=\widehat{f}\circ\widehat{c}=\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
to
H_{1}\circ\widehat{c}=\phi\circ\widehat{f}\circ\widehat{c}=\widehat{f}\circ\widehat{c}=\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
.
Additionally, again by definition of
\widehat{c}
, on
Y\times\{0\}\times I
, it is
\mathbf{H}|_{Y}\circ c^{-1}\circ c
, whereas on
Y\times\{1\}\times I
, it is
\mathbf{H}|_{Y^{\prime}}\circ c=c\times\operatorname{id}_{I}=:\mathbf{cte}_{c}
since
\mathbf{H}
is relative
Y^{\prime}
.
Hence on the boundary this homotopy restricts to

(\mathbf{G}^{\prime}\sqcup\mathbf{G}^{\prime})\cup\mathbf{cte}_{c}\cup(c^{-1}\circ c\circ\mathbf{H}|_{Y})\colon(Y\times I\times\{0,1\})\cup(Y\times\{1\}\times I)\cup(Y\times\{0\}\times I)\to B.

It then becomes apparent that, reparametrising this homotopy, the concatenation of
\overline{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
is homotopic rel.
Y\times\{0,1\}
to
c^{-1}\circ c\circ\mathbf{H}|_{Y}
.
Since the concatenation of
\overline{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
is homotopic rel.
Y\times\{0,1\}
to the constant homotopy at
G^{\prime}_{0}=c\circ c^{-1}\circ c
,
we conclude that
c^{-1}\circ c\circ\mathbf{H}|_{Y}\colon Y\times I\to B
is homotopic rel.
Y\times\{0,1\}
to the constant homotopy
Y\times I\to B
at
c\circ c^{-1}\circ c
.

Using a homotopy from
c^{-1}\circ c
to
\operatorname{id}_{Y}
, then shows that
\mathbf{H}|_{Y}
is homotopic rel.
Y\times\{0,1\}
to the constant homotopy
Y\times I\to Y
given at
c
.

Using homotopy extension as in the last paragraph of the proof of
Lemma

5.2
, it follows that
\mathbf{H}|_{X}
is homotopic rel.
X\times\{0,1\}
to a homotopy that is relative
Y
.
This homotopy ensures that
f^{\prime}\simeq\phi\circ f
relative
Y
as needed.
∎

Proposition 5.5
.

The group
\mathcal{G}
acts transitively on
\mathcal{S}_{0}(c_{1})
by composition.

Proof.

We verify that for
\varphi\in\mathcal{G}
and
c_{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}(c_{1})
, the composition
\varphi\circ c_{0}
again lies in
\mathcal{S}_{0}(c_{1})
.
Since
\varphi
is rel. boundary, we have
c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h=c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}=\varphi\circ c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}.

Next we must show that
(\varphi\circ c_{0})^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
.
To do so, apply
c_{0}^{*}(c_{1}^{*})^{-1}
to
(\varphi\circ c_{1})^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
and then use
c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
:

(\varphi\circ c_{0})^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}=c_{0}^{*}(c_{1}^{*})^{-1}(\varphi\circ c_{1})^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}=c_{0}^{*}(c_{1}^{*})^{-1}(c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})=c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}.

We prove that the action is transitive.
Given
c_{0},c_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}(c_{1})
, Lemma
5.3
ensures that there is a homotopy equivalence
\varphi\colon B\to B
rel.
Y_{1}
with
\varphi\circ c_{0}^{\prime}\simeq c_{0}
.
We verify that
\varphi\in\mathcal{G}
by using the definition of
\mathcal{S}_{0}(c_{1})

to get
(c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}=c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=(\varphi\circ c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}
and then applying
c_{1}^{*}\circ((c_{0}^{\prime})^{*})^{-1}
to obtain

c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}=(\varphi\circ c_{1})^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
.
This concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

We now move towards the construction of an action of
\mathcal{G}
on
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
.

Construction 5.6

(The map
\mathcal{G}\to\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
)
.

The idea is to run the definition of the secondary obstruction
b(c_{0},c_{1})
but with
(X_{1},\varphi\circ c_{1})
in place of
(X_{0},c_{0})
.
Here are the details.
Repeating the proof of Proposition
4.5
shows that the condition
\varphi\circ c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}=c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}
ensures the existence of a unique class
\xi\in H_{4}(B)
with

(5.7)

j_{*}(\xi)=(\varphi\circ c_{1})_{*}([X_{1}])-(c_{1})_{*}([X_{1}]).

Consider the pairing
b_{\xi}(a,b)=\langle b,a\cap\xi\rangle

on
H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.
Arguing as in Proposition
4.8
, the condition
(\varphi\circ c_{1})^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
ensures that this pairing induces a pairing on
H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])/\operatorname{im}(j^{*})
that can then be pulled back using
c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}^{*}
to a pairing

b(\varphi)\colon H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi].

Explicitly, for classes
a,b\in H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, pick
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
with
c_{1}^{*}(a^{\prime})=a,c_{1}^{*}(b^{\prime})=b
, the hermitian pairing is defined as

b(\varphi)(a,b):=\langle b^{\prime},a^{\prime}\cap\xi\rangle.

Proposition 5.8
.

The group
\mathcal{G}
acts on
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
by

\varphi\cdot\lambda:=b(\varphi)+\lambda.

Proof.

This proposition will follow readily from the following claim.

Claim
.

The following equality holds for every
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{G}
:

b(\psi\circ\varphi)=b(\psi)+b(\varphi).

Proof.

We set
x=(c_{1})_{*}([X_{1}])
for brevity.
As in the definition of
b(\varphi)
and
b(\psi)
(recall (
5.7
)), we pick
\xi_{\varphi},\xi_{\psi}\in H_{4}(B)
so that
j_{*}(\xi_{\varphi})=\varphi_{*}(x)-x
and
j_{*}(\xi_{\psi})=\psi_{*}(x)-x
.
It follows that

i_{*}(\psi_{*}(\xi_{\varphi})+\xi_{\psi})=\psi_{*}(\varphi_{*}(x))-\psi_{*}(x)+\psi_{*}(x)-x=\psi_{*}(\varphi_{*}(x))-x.

The definition of
b(\psi\circ\varphi)
now implies that for
a,b\in H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
such that
c_{1}^{*}(a^{\prime})=a
and
c_{1}^{*}(b^{\prime})=b
, we have

\displaystyle b(\psi\circ\varphi)(a,b)

\displaystyle=\langle b^{\prime},a^{\prime}\cap(\psi_{*}(\xi_{\varphi})+\xi_{\psi})\rangle=\langle\psi^{*}(b^{\prime}),\psi^{*}(a^{\prime})\cap\xi_{\varphi}\rangle+\langle b^{\prime},a^{\prime}\cap\xi_{\psi}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\psi^{*}(b^{\prime}),\psi^{*}(a^{\prime})\cap\xi_{\varphi}\rangle+b(\psi)(a,b).

Finally, note that since
\psi\circ c_{1}=c_{1}
we have
(c_{1})^{*}(\psi^{*}(a^{\prime}))=a
and
(c_{1})^{*}(\psi^{*}(b^{\prime}))=b
, and so we can use
\psi^{*}(a^{\prime})
and
\psi^{*}(b^{\prime})
to calculate
b(\varphi)
.
Namely, we have
b(\varphi)(a,b)=\langle\psi^{*}(b^{\prime}),\psi^{*}(a^{\prime})\cap\xi_{\varphi}\rangle
and the equality
b(\psi\circ\varphi)=b(\psi)+b(\varphi)
follows, thus proving the claim.
∎

For
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{G}
and
\lambda\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
, the claim implies that
(\psi\circ\varphi)\cdot\lambda=b(\psi)+b(\varphi)+\lambda
and, clearly, this equals
\psi\cdot(\varphi\cdot\lambda)
.
Finally, we note that
\operatorname{id}\in\mathcal{G}
acts as the identity: in this case,
i_{*}(0)=\operatorname{id}(x)-x
, so
b(\operatorname{id})=0
, whence
b(\operatorname{id})+\lambda=\lambda.

∎

The combination of this proposition with the next leads to the secondary obstruction from Definition
2.7
.

Proposition 5.9
.

If
c_{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}(c_{1})
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{G}
, then

b(\varphi\circ c_{0},c_{1})=b(c_{0},c_{1})-b(\varphi).

In particular, the hermitian form
b(c_{0},c_{1})
, once considered in
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}
, does not depend on the choice of
c_{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}(c_{1})
.

Proof.

We argue that the second assertion follows from the first.
We consider
c_{0},c_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}(c_{1})
and aim to prove that
b(c_{0},c_{1})=b(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1})\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}.

Proposition
5.5
ensures that there is a homotopy equivalence
\varphi\in\mathcal{G}
with
\varphi\circ c_{0}=c_{0}^{\prime}
.
Since
\varphi\in\mathcal{G}
and
c_{0}^{\prime}=\varphi\circ c_{0}
, the first assertion yields
b(\varphi)+b(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1})=b(c_{0},c_{1})
.
By definition of the action, this is
\varphi\cdot b(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1})=b(c_{0},c_{1})
, as required.
We now prove the first assertion.

For brevity, write
x_{0}:=c_{0}([X_{0}])
as well as
x_{0}^{\prime}:=(\varphi\circ c_{0})_{*}([X_{0}])
and
x_{1}:=(c_{1})_{*}([X_{1}])
.
Note that
x_{0}^{\prime}=\varphi_{*}(x_{0}).

Let
\xi_{0},\xi_{0}^{\prime},\xi_{\varphi}\in H_{4}(B)
be the unique classes that respectively satisfy
j_{*}(\xi_{0})=x_{1}-x_{0},j_{*}(\xi_{0}^{\prime})=x_{1}-x_{0}^{\prime}
and
j_{*}(\xi_{\varphi})=\varphi_{*}(x_{1})-x_{1}
, where
i\colon H_{4}(B)\to H_{4}(B,Y_{1})
denotes the inclusion induced map.
A direct calculation shows that

\displaystyle j_{*}(\xi_{\varphi}+\xi_{0}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\varphi_{*}(x_{1})-x_{1}+x_{1}-x_{0}^{\prime}=\varphi_{*}(x_{1})-\varphi_{*}(x_{0})=\varphi_{*}(x_{1}-x_{0})=\varphi_{*}(j_{*}(\xi_{0}))

\displaystyle=i_{*}(\varphi_{*}(\xi_{0})).

Since
i
is injective, this implies that
\xi_{\varphi}+\xi_{0}^{\prime}=\varphi_{*}(\xi_{0})
.
It follows that for
a,b\in H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
such that
c_{1}^{*}(a^{\prime})=a
and
c_{1}^{*}(b^{\prime})=b
, we have

\displaystyle\left(b(\varphi)+b(\varphi\circ c_{0},c_{1})\right)(a,b)

\displaystyle=\langle b^{\prime},a^{\prime}\cap(\xi_{\varphi}+\xi_{0}^{\prime})\rangle=\langle b^{\prime},a^{\prime}\cap\varphi_{*}(\xi_{0})\rangle=\langle\varphi^{*}(b^{\prime}),\varphi^{*}(a^{\prime})\cap\xi_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=b(c_{0},c_{1})(a,b).

The last equality holds because, since
\varphi\circ c_{1}=c_{1}
, we have
(c_{1})^{*}(\varphi^{*}(a^{\prime}))=a
and
(c_{1})^{*}(\varphi^{*}(b^{\prime}))=b
and so we can use
\varphi^{*}(a^{\prime})
and
\varphi^{*}(b^{\prime})
to calculate
b(c_{0},c_{1})(a,b)
.
This calculation establishes the equality
b(\varphi)+b(\varphi\circ c_{0},c_{1})=b(c_{0},c_{1})
required by the proposition.
∎

We can now prove Theorem
2.8
.

Theorem
2.8
.

Let
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
be
4
-dimensional Poincaré pairs with fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X_{i})\cong\pi
, Postnikov
2
-type
B:=P_{2}(X_{1})
and
\pi_{1}(Y_{i})\to\pi_{1}(X_{i})
surjective for
i=0,1
, and let
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
be a degree one homotopy equivalence.
Assume that

•

either
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
and the map
\operatorname{ev}^{*}\colon H^{2}(X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{**}\to H_{2}(X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}
is an isomorphism,

•

or
\pi_{1}(X_{i})
is finite and
\Gamma(\pi_{2}(X_{1}))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\mathbb{Z}
is torsion free.

The homotopy equivalence
h
extends to a homotopy equivalence
X_{0}\to X_{1}
if and only if
there exist
3
-connected maps
c_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B
and
c_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B
such that
c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h=c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}
and

(1)

the pulled back pairings
c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}
and
c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
on
H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
are equal,

(2)

the hermitian pairing
b(c_{0},c_{1})
vanishes in
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}
.

Additionally, when
b(c_{0},c_{1})=0\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
, the homotopy equivalence
f\colon X_{0}\to X_{1}
extending
h
can be chosen to satisfy
c_{1}\circ f\simeq c_{0}
.

Proof.

If
h
extends to a homotopy equivalence
f\colon X_{0}\to X_{1}
(which will necessarily have degree one), then we verify that picking an arbitrary
3
-connected map
c_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B
and setting
c_{0}:=c_{1}\circ f
yields the result.
Firstly, a rapid verification shows that
c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=f^{*}\circ c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
.
Secondly, we see that
(c_{0})_{*}([X_{0}])=(c_{1})_{*}\circ f_{*}([X_{0}])=(c_{1})_{*}([X_{1}])
, so Proposition
4.5
ensures that
\xi_{0}=0
and therefore
b_{\xi_{0}}=0.

This implies that
b(c_{0},c_{1})=0\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])).

We prove the converse.
Since
c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h=c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}
and
c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
, the pairing
b(c_{0},c_{1})
is defined.
Since
b(c_{0},c_{1})=0\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}
, there is a homotopy equivalence
\varphi\colon B\to B
rel.
Y_{1}
such that
b(c_{0},c_{1})-b(\varphi)=0
.
Consider
c_{0}^{\prime}:=\varphi\circ c_{0}
.
Proposition
5.9
yields

b(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1})=b(c_{0},c_{1})-b(\varphi)=0.

Thus,
c_{0}^{\prime}
satisfies all of the hypotheses from Proposition
4.9
which ensures that the homotopy equivalence
h
extends to a homotopy equivalence
X_{0}\to X_{1}
with
c_{1}\circ f\simeq c_{0}^{\prime}
.
Here, in the case that
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
, Proposition
4.9
requires that
\operatorname{Herm}(\pi_{2}(B)^{*})\to\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
is injective, but by Proposition
12.9
below, this condition holds if
\operatorname{ev}^{*}\colon H^{2}(X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{**}\to H_{2}(X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}
is an isomorphism.
∎

6.
Relative
k
-invariants

At this stage, we have proved Theorem
2.8
which shows that the vanishing of the primary and secondary obstructions suffices to extend a homotopy equivalence.
The objective of the next four sections is to rework this theorem so that the outcome does not refer to the
2
-type.
This requires some background on relative
k
-invariants which we now recall.

Given a pair
(X,Y)
that is homotopy equivalent to a CW pair and a map
\nu\colon X\to B\pi_{1}(X)
, the relative
k
-invariant is a class
k_{X,Y}^{\nu}\in H^{3}(M(\nu|_{Y}),Y;\pi_{2}(X))
, where
M(\nu|_{Y})\simeq B\pi_{1}(X)
denotes the mapping cylinder of
\nu|_{Y}
.
We note that up to isomorphism,
k_{X,Y}^{\nu}
does not depend on
\nu
and that its precise definition is not needed in the present article (we refer to
[
CK25
]
for the details) as we only require some of its properties.

Convention 6.1
.

Fix pairs of spaces
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
that are homotopy equivalent to CW complexes.
Additionally, fix a map
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
and an isomorphism
u\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\pi_{1}(X_{1})
satisfying
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
that we us to identify
\pi_{1}(X_{0})
with
\pi:=\pi_{1}(X_{1})
.
Given maps
\nu_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B\pi
and
\nu_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B\pi
, we write

(\operatorname{id},h)^{*}\colon H^{3}(M(\nu_{1}|_{Y_{1}}),Y_{0};\pi_{2}(X_{1}))\to H^{3}(M(\nu_{0}|_{Y_{0}}),Y_{0};\pi_{2}(X_{1}))

for the map induced by
h
on the cohomology of the relevant mapping cylinders.
We refer to
[
CK25
, Lemma 5.1]
for further details concerning the definition of this map.

We begin with the main property of the relative
k
-invariant.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
be pairs of spaces that are homotopy equivalent to CW pairs, and let
c_{1}\colon X_{1}\to P_{2}(X_{1})
be the
2
-type of
X_{1}
.
For a homotopy equivalence
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
, an isomorphism
u\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\pi_{1}(X_{1})
with
u\circ(\iota_{0})_{*}=(\iota_{1})_{*}\circ h
, and an
u
-invariant isomorphism
F\colon\pi_{2}(X_{0})\to\penalty\ \pi_{2}(X_{1})
, the following assertions are equivalent:

•

there is a
3
-connected map
c_{0}\colon X_{0}\to P_{2}(X_{1})
such that

(c_{0})_{*}=\operatorname{id},\quad c_{1}\circ h\simeq c_{0}|_{Y_{0}},\quad\text{and}\quad(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}(c_{0})_{*}=F;

•

the relative
k
-invariants satisfy

(\operatorname{id},h)^{*}(k_{X_{1},Y_{1}}^{\nu_{1}})=F_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}}^{\nu_{0}})\in H^{3}(M(\nu_{0}|_{Y_{0}}),Y_{0};\pi_{2}(X_{1}))

for every
\nu_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B\pi
and
\nu_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B\pi
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of
[
CK25
, Theorem 1.1]
.
In that paper,
F
was not assumed to be an isomorphism, so
c_{0}
was not claimed to be
3
-connected,.
∎

We record two additional properties of relative
k
-invariants.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
(X,Y)
be a pair of spaces that is homotopy equivalent to a CW pair, and write
i\colon Y\to X
for the inclusion.
Then, for every
\nu\colon X\to B\pi_{1}(X)
, the following equality holds:

(\operatorname{id},i)^{*}k_{X,X}^{\nu}=k_{X,Y}^{\nu}\in H^{3}(M(\nu|_{Y}),Y;\pi_{2}(X)).

Proof.

The proof can be found in
[
CK25
, Corollary 5.8]
.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
X
be a space, let
\nu\colon X\to B\pi_{1}(X)
be a map, set
\pi:=\pi_{1}(X)
, and given a
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module
A
, consider the
group homomorphism

\displaystyle\Phi\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(H_{2}(\widetilde{X}),A)

\displaystyle\to H^{3}(M(\nu),X;A)

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\mapsto\varphi_{*}(k_{X,X}^{\nu}).

Then, writing
\delta
for the connecting homomorphism in the exact sequence of the pair
(M(\nu),X)
, we have

\Phi\circ\operatorname{ev}=\delta\colon H^{2}(X;A)\to H^{3}(M(\nu),X;A).

Proof.

The proof can be found in
[
CK25
, Lemma 6.3]
.
∎

Convention 6.5
.

For better readability, during the next section (as well as in the introduction and in Section
2
), we write

h^{*}(k_{X_{1},Y_{1}})=F_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}})\in H^{3}(B\pi,Y_{0};\pi_{2}(X_{1}))

if the following equality holds for every
\nu_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B\pi
and
\nu_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B\pi
;

(\operatorname{id},h)^{*}(k_{X_{1},Y_{1}}^{\nu_{1}})=F_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}}^{\nu_{0}})\in H^{3}(M(\nu_{0}|_{Y_{0}}),Y_{0};\pi_{2}(X_{1})).

7.
The primary obstruction and compatible triples

The goal of this section is to reformulate the vanishing of the primary obstruction in terms of compatible triples, thus proving Theorem
2.9
.
Section
7.1
briefly discusses relative hermitian forms, Section
7.2
recalls the definition of compatible triples from the introduction, Section
7.3
proves Theorem
2.9
, and Section
7.4
focuses on situations where the
k
-invariant condition can be done away with.

7.1.
Relative hermitian forms

This short section concerns relative hermitian forms.

Definition 7.1
.

Let
R
be a ring and let
P,Q
be left
R
-modules.

•

A
relative hermitian form
consists of a pair of
R
-linear maps
(\widehat{\lambda}^{\partial},j)
with
j\colon P\to Q
and
\widehat{\lambda}^{\partial}\colon Q\to\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(P,R)}
such that
\widehat{\lambda}:=\widehat{\lambda}^{\partial}\circ j
is a hermitian form.
We refer to
\widehat{\lambda}
as the
hermitian form associated
to
(\widehat{\lambda}^{\partial},j).

The pairings corresponding to
\widehat{\lambda}^{\partial}
and
\widehat{\lambda}
are respectively denoted
\lambda^{\partial}\colon P\times Q\to R
and
\lambda^{\partial}\colon P\times P\to R
.

•

Two relative hermitian forms
(\widehat{\lambda}_{0}^{\partial},j_{0})
and
(\widehat{\lambda}^{\partial}_{1},j_{1})
are
isometric
if there are isomorphisms
F\colon P_{0}\to P_{1}
and
G\colon Q_{0}\to Q_{1}
with
G\circ j_{0}=j_{1}\circ F
and
\lambda_{1}^{\partial}(F(x),G(y))=\lambda_{0}^{\partial}(x,y)
for every
x\in P_{0}
and every
y\in Q_{0}
, i.e.
F
and
G
fit in the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{P_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{0}}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{Q_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\lambda}_{0}^{\partial}}
\scriptstyle{G}
\textstyle{P_{0}^{*}}
\textstyle{P_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{1}}
\textstyle{Q_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\lambda}_{1}^{\partial}}
\textstyle{P_{1}^{*}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F^{*}}

In particular, note that
F\colon\lambda_{0}\cong\lambda_{1}
is automatically an isometry.

The next example describes the main motivation for this algebraic notion.

Example 7.2
.

Given a
4
-dimensional Poincaré complex
(X,Y)
, the pair

\left(\lambda_{M}^{\partial},j_{*}\colon H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)])\to H_{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)])\right)

is a relative hermitian form.
Indeed, the associated form

H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)])\xrightarrow{j_{*}}H_{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)])\xrightarrow{\widehat{\lambda}_{X}^{\partial}}H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)])^{*}

is the equivariant intersection form of
(X,Y)
and is therefore hermitian by Lemma
A.9
.

The following lemma describes a situation in which an isometry of relative hermitian forms is determined by an isometry of the associated hermitian forms.

Lemma 7.3
.

If
(\widehat{\lambda}_{0}^{\partial},j_{0})
and
(\widehat{\lambda}^{\partial}_{1},j_{1})
are two relative hermitian forms such that
\widehat{\lambda}_{0}^{\partial}
and
\widehat{\lambda}_{1}^{\partial}
are isomorphisms, then an isometry
F\colon\lambda_{0}\cong\lambda_{1}
of the associated hermitian forms determines an isometry

(F,F_{!})\colon(\widehat{\lambda}_{0}^{\partial},j_{0})\cong(\widehat{\lambda}^{\partial}_{1},j_{1}).

Proof.

Given an isometry
F\colon\lambda_{0}\cong\lambda_{1}
, we obtain the commutative diagram

(7.4)

\textstyle{P_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{0}}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{Q_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\lambda}_{0}^{\partial},\cong}
\scriptstyle{F_{!}}
\textstyle{P_{0}^{*}}
\textstyle{P_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{1}}
\textstyle{Q_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\lambda}_{1}^{\partial},\cong}
\textstyle{P_{1}^{*},\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F^{*}}

where the central dashed isomorphism is defined by

F_{!}:=\left(\widehat{\lambda}_{1}^{\partial}\right)^{-1}\circ(F^{*})^{-1}\circ\widehat{\lambda}_{0}^{\partial}\colon Q_{0}\to Q_{1}.

The right square of (
7.4
) clearly commutes.
The left square also commutes because the outer rectangle commutes.
This shows that
(F,F_{!})
is an isometry of relative hermitian forms.
∎

7.2.
Compatible triples and pairs.

This section concerns compatible triples and pairs.

Definition 7.5
.

Let
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
be
4
-dimensional Poincaré pairs with
\pi_{1}(X_{i})\cong\pi
and for which
\iota_{j}\colon\pi_{1}(Y_{j})\to\pi_{1}(X_{j})
is surjective for
j=0,1
.
Let
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
be a homotopy equivalence and assume that there is an isomorphism
u\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\pi_{1}(X_{1})
with
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
.
An isometry
(F,G)
between the relative hermitian forms
(\lambda_{X_{0}}^{\partial},(j_{0})_{*})
and
(\lambda_{X_{1}}^{\partial},(j_{1})_{*})
is
compatible
with
h
if the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{2}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(j_{0})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0},Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G}
\textstyle{H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(j_{1})_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{1},Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

We refer to
(F,G,h)
as a
compatible triple
.

Convention 7.6
.

For the remainder of this section, we fix
Poincaré pairs
(X_{0},Y_{0}),(X_{1},Y_{1})
with Postnikov
2
-type
B
and a homotopy equivalence
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
as in Notation
2.3
.

The next result (mentioned in Remark
1.2
from the introduction) shows that for some fundamental groups (such as the trivial group,
\mathbb{Z}
, and finite groups), the isomorphism
G
is superfluous.

Proposition
2.11
.

If
H^{k}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
for
k=2,3
, then the existence of an isometry
F\colon\lambda_{X_{0}}\cong\lambda_{X_{1}}
with

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{2}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0},Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{!}}
\textstyle{H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{1},Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where
F_{!}=\operatorname{PD}_{X_{1}}\circ\operatorname{ev}_{1}^{-1}\circ F^{*}\operatorname{ev}_{0}\operatorname{PD}_{X_{0}}^{-1}
, ensures the existence of a compatible triple.

Proof.

The fact that
H^{k}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
for
k=2,3
implies that
\operatorname{ev}\colon H^{2}(X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}
is an isomorphism for
i=0,1
.
It follows that
\widehat{\lambda}_{X_{0}}^{\partial}
and
\widehat{\lambda}_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
are isomorphisms.
Lemma
7.3
then ensures that
(F,F_{!},h)
is a compatible triple.
∎

A pair
(F,h)
as in Proposition
2.11
is referred to as a
compatible pair
.
These have appeared previously in
[
Boy86
]
(for
\pi=1
) and in
[
CP23
]
(for
\pi\cong\mathbb{Z}
).

7.3.
The primary obstruction and compatible triples

Next, we prove the main statement of this section which makes it possible to reformulate the vanishing of the primary obstruction in terms of compatible triples (as stated in Theorem
2.9
).
The combination of Proposition
7.7
and Proposition
7.12
implies Theorem
2.9
.

Proposition 7.7
.

Assume that there is an isomorphism
u\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\pi_{1}(X_{1})
with
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
and that there is a compatible triple

\textstyle{H_{2}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0},Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G}
\textstyle{H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{H_{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{1},Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Assume that
F_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}})=h^{*}(k_{X_{1},Y_{1}})
, and

•

either that the map
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is injective, and that
H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong L\oplus T
, with
L
a free
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module and
T^{*}=0
.

•

or that
\pi
is finite and that
H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong L\oplus T\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, with
L
a free
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module and
T^{*}=0
.
Here,
\mathbb{Z}
is endowed with the
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module structure induced by the trivial
\pi
-action.

Given a
3
-connected map
c_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B
, there exists a
3
-connected map
c_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B
such that

c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}=c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h,\quad(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}(c_{0})_{*}=F,\quad\text{and}\quad(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}(c_{0})_{*}=G.

In particular,
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
satisfy

c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}.

Proof.

By Theorem
6.2
, the condition on the
k
-invariants ensures that there is a
c^{\prime}\colon X_{0}\to B
with
c^{\prime}|_{Y_{0}}\simeq c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h
, such that
c^{\prime}
is a
\pi_{1}
-isomorphism
and with
(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}c^{\prime}_{*}=F
.
Apply the homotopy extension property to
c^{\prime}|_{Y_{0}}\simeq c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h
so that
c^{\prime}\simeq c\colon X_{0}\to B
with
c|_{Y_{0}}=c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h
.
In particular,
c
induces a map of pairs
c\colon(X_{0},Y_{0})\to(B,Y_{1})
.

Claim
.

There exists a map

\psi\colon H_{2}(X_{0},Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

that makes the following diagram commute:

(7.8)

\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(j_{0})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{F-(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}c_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0},Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G-(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}c_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(j_{1})_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{1},Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).}

Proof.

Since we already have
F=(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}c_{*}
, we deduce that the following diagram commutes:

(7.9)

\textstyle{H_{2}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(i_{0})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(j_{0})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0},Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G-(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}c_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{0}}
\textstyle{H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{H_{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(i_{1})_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(j_{1})_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{1},Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{1}}
\textstyle{H_{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

In order to define
\psi
, it suffices to define a map

\psi^{\prime}\colon H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

with
(j_{1})_{*}\circ\psi^{\prime}\circ\partial_{0}=G-(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}c_{*}
.
Indeed
\psi
is then obtained as
\psi:=\psi^{\prime}\circ\partial_{0}
: a short verification ensures that
\psi
makes (
7.8
) commute.

Since the diagram in (
7.9
) commutes and the bottom sequence is exact, the map
G-(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}c_{*}
has image in
\ker(\partial_{1})
.
Thus for
\partial_{0}(x)\in H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, with
x\in H_{2}(X_{0},Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
exactness ensures that
G-(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}c_{*}(x)=(j_{1})_{*}(z)
for some
z\in H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.
The element
z
is only well defined in
\operatorname{coker}((i_{1})_{*})
leading to a map

\psi^{\prime\prime}\colon H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to\operatorname{coker}((i_{1})_{*}).

that satisfies

(i_{1})_{*}\circ\psi^{\prime\prime}\circ\partial_{0}(x)=(i_{1})_{*}(z)=(G-(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}c_{*})(x).

We will show that the projection
\operatorname{proj}\colon H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to\operatorname{coker}((i_{1})_{*})
induces a surjection

(7.10)

\operatorname{proj}_{*}\colon\operatorname{Hom}(H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))\to\operatorname{Hom}(H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),\operatorname{coker}((i_{1})_{*})).

Any lift
\psi^{\prime}\colon H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
of
\psi^{\prime\prime}
will satisfy
(j_{1})_{*}\circ\psi^{\prime}\circ\partial_{0}=(j_{1})_{*}\circ\psi^{\prime\prime}\circ\partial_{0}=G-(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}c_{*}
and thus give the map
\psi\colon H_{2}(X_{0},Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
required by the claim.

Towards showing that
\operatorname{proj}_{*}
is surjective, we assert that
\operatorname{ev}\circ\operatorname{PD}_{X_{1}}^{-1}\colon H_{2}(X_{1},Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}
restricts to an injection
\ker(\partial_{1})\to H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}.

Consider the following diagram:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{PD}_{X_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{i_{1}^{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{1},Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{1}}
\textstyle{H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{PD}_{Y_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H_{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).}

The vertical map is assumed to be injective in our first hypothesis; whereas in the second set of hypotheses,
\pi
is finite, and the injectivity is automatic because in that case
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
.
The triangle commutes by naturality, and the square commutes by standard properties of the Poincaré duality isomorphism.
A short diagram chase using the exactness of the top row shows that if
\operatorname{ev}\circ\operatorname{PD}_{X_{1}}^{-1}(x)=0
for
x\in\ker(\partial_{1})
, then
x=0.

This establishes the assertion.

The assertion produces an injection
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(T,\operatorname{coker}((i_{1})_{*}))\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(T,H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*})
.
This implies that
\operatorname{Hom}(T,\operatorname{coker}((i_{1})_{*}))=0
because

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(T,H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*})

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),T^{*})=0.

We deduce that the map
\operatorname{proj}_{*}
from (
7.10
) takes the form

\textstyle{\operatorname{proj}_{*}\colon{\overbrace{\operatorname{Hom}(H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))}^{=\operatorname{Hom}(T,H_{2})\oplus\operatorname{Hom}(L,H_{2})\oplus\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{Z}^{k},H_{2})}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\overbrace{\operatorname{Hom}(H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),\operatorname{coker}((i_{1})_{*})).}^{=\operatorname{Hom}(L,\operatorname{coker})\oplus\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{Z}^{k},\operatorname{coker})}}}

where the summands involving
\mathbb{Z}
are understood to occur only under the second set of hypotheses.

To show that this is surjective, we show that
\operatorname{Hom}(L,H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))\to\operatorname{Hom}(L,\operatorname{coker}((i_{1})_{*}))
and
\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{Z}^{k},H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))\to\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{Z}^{k},\operatorname{coker}((i_{1})_{*}))
are both surjective.

In the first case, this follows because
L
is free and
\operatorname{proj}\colon H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to\operatorname{coker}((i_{1})_{*})
is surjective.
We thus focus on the case in which the group
\pi
is finite.
Since
\pi
is finite, we first note that
H_{3}(X_{1},Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong H^{1}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0.

Then, by dualising the resulting short exact sequence
0\to H_{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to\operatorname{coker}((i_{1})_{*})\to 0
,
we obtain

\ldots\to\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{Z}^{k},H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))\xrightarrow{\operatorname{proj}_{*}}\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{Z}^{k},\operatorname{coker}((i_{1})_{*}))\to\overbrace{\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(\mathbb{Z}^{k},H_{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))}^{=H^{1}(\pi;H_{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))}\to\ldots

Thus it suffices to prove that the rightmost term vanishes.
Since the group
\pi
is finite,
H^{1}(\pi)=0
and
H^{k}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=\operatorname{Ext}^{k}(\pi,\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
for
k>0

whence the desired outcome:

H^{1}(\pi;H_{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))\cong H^{1}(\pi;H^{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))\cong H^{1}(\pi;H_{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*})=H^{1}(\pi;L\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{k})=0.

Thus the map
\operatorname{proj}_{*}
from (
7.10
) is surjective.
As explained above this gives the existence of the map
\psi
required by the claim.
∎

For
i=0,1
, note that since
H_{i}(X_{0},Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
, we have
\operatorname{Ext}^{3-i}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(H_{i}(X_{0},Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),\pi_{2}(B))=0
.
The UCSS then implies that the following evaluation map is surjective:

H^{2}(X_{0},Y_{0};\pi_{2}(B))\to\operatorname{Hom}(H_{2}(X_{0},Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),\pi_{2}(B))\ni(c_{1})_{*}\circ\psi

Here,
\psi
is the map from the claim.
Choose
[d]\in H^{2}(X_{0},Y_{0};\pi_{2}(B))
with
\operatorname{ev}([d])=(c_{1})_{*}\circ\psi
.
Proposition
C.1
applied with
(X,Y)=(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(B,B^{\prime})=(B,Y_{1})
and
f_{0}=c
shows that there exists a map
c_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B
with
c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}=c|_{Y_{0}}
and
(c_{0})_{*}=c_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\pi_{1}(B)
that makes the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(j_{0})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{(c_{0})_{*}-c_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0},Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(c_{0})_{*}-c_{*}}
\scriptstyle{(c_{1})_{*}\circ\psi}
\textstyle{H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{B}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(B,Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).}

Applying
(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}
to this diagram gives the following commutative diagram:

(7.11)

\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(j_{0})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}((c_{0})_{*}-c_{*})}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0},Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}((c_{0})_{*}-c_{*})}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(j_{1})_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{1},Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).}

Taking the difference of the diagrams in (
7.8
) and (
7.11
) gives

\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(j_{0})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{F-(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}(c_{0})_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0},Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G-(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}(c_{0})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(j_{1})_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{1},Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).}

This shows that
(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}(c_{0})_{*}=F
and
(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}(c_{0})_{*}=G
.
In particular since
c_{1}
is
3
-connected, this equality ensures that
c_{0}
is a
\pi_{2}
-isomorphism.
Since
\pi_{3}(B)=0
and Proposition
C.1
ensures that
c_{0}
is a
\pi_{1}
-isomorphism, we deduce that
c_{0}
is
3
-connected.

Since
(F,G)
is an isometry of relative forms, the conditions
(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}(c_{0})_{*}=F
and
(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}(c_{0})_{*}=G
are seen to be equivalent to
\lambda_{X_{0}}^{\partial}((c_{0})_{*}^{-1}(x),(c_{0})_{*}^{-1}(y))=\lambda_{X_{1}}^{\partial}((c_{1})_{*}^{-1}(x),(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}(y))
. Proposition
B.3
therefore ensures that
c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}.

∎

We now prove the converse of Proposition
7.7
.

Proposition 7.12
.

Let
c_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B
and
c_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B
be
3
-connected maps.
If
c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}=c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ\penalty\ h
and
c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
, then
u:=(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}\circ(c_{0})_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\pi_{1}(X_{1})
satisfies
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
and the triple
((c_{1})_{*}^{-1}(c_{0})_{*},(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}(c_{0})_{*},h)
is compatible.

Proof.

The assertion concerning
u
is immediate and we therefore focus on the second claim.
Since
c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}=h
and
c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}
is the inclusion, the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{2}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}(c_{0})_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0},Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}(c_{0})_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{1}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{1},Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{1}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Since
c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
, Proposition
B.3
implies that
(c_{0})_{*}^{-1}\lambda_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}\lambda_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
, i.e. that
(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}(c_{0})_{*}
gives rise to an isomorphism of relative hermitian forms.
∎

7.4.
Working without the
k
-invariant

We show that if either
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 2
or if
H_{4}(\pi)=0

and
H_{1}(Y_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}=0
, then the
k
-invariant condition in Theorem
2.9
can be done away with.
These results were stated in Proposition
2.12
.

We begin with the case where
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 2
.

Proposition 7.13
.

Let
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
be
4
-dimensional Poincaré pairs with fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X_{i})\cong\pi
and
\iota_{j}\colon\pi_{1}(Y_{i})\to\pi_{1}(X_{i})
surjective for
i=0,1
.
Fix a degree one homotopy equivalence
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
and assume there is a group isomorphism
u\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\pi_{1}(X_{1})
that satisfies
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
.
If
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 2
and if there is a compatible triple
(F,G,h)
, then

F_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}})=h^{*}(k_{X_{1},Y_{1}})\in H^{3}(B\pi,Y_{0};\pi_{2}(X_{1})).

Proof.

According to Convention
6.5
, given
\nu_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B\pi
and
\nu_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B\pi
, the proposition requires we prove
F_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}}^{\nu_{0}})=(\operatorname{id},h)^{*}(k_{X_{1},Y_{1}}^{\nu_{1}})
.
The idea of the proof is to run a diagram chase on the diagram in (
7.14
) below, but doing so requires some preliminary observations.

We write
G^{!}\colon H^{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
for the map
\operatorname{PD}_{X_{1}}^{-1}\circ G\circ\operatorname{PD}_{X_{0}}
.
By definition of a compatible triple and the commutativity of the following diagram we have
F^{*}\circ\operatorname{ev}\circ G^{!}=\operatorname{ev}
and
i_{0}^{*}=h^{*}\circ i_{1}^{*}\circ G^{!}
:

\textstyle{H^{2}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{PD}_{Y_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H^{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{0}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{PD}_{X_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{G^{!}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}}
\textstyle{H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0},Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{0}}
\textstyle{H_{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{1},Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{1}}
\textstyle{H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{PD}_{Y_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{h^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{PD}_{X_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{F^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}

Let
\Phi_{i}\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(H_{2}(X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))\to H^{3}(M(\nu_{i}),X_{i};H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
be the homomorphism from
Lemma

6.4
and consider the following diagram in which
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-coefficients are understood:

(7.14)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}(H_{2}(X_{0}),H_{2}(X_{0}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}(H_{2}(X_{0}),H_{2}(X_{1}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}(H_{2}(X_{1}),H_{2}(X_{1}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{3}(M(\nu_{0}),X_{0};H_{2}(X_{0}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{3}(M(\nu_{0}),X_{0};H_{2}(X_{1}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{3}(M(\nu_{1}),X_{1};H_{2}(X_{1}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{3}(M(\nu_{0}|_{Y_{0}}),Y_{0};H_{2}(X_{0}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{3}(M(\nu_{0}|_{Y_{0}}),Y_{0},;H_{2}(X_{1}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{3}(M(\nu_{1}|_{Y_{1}}),Y_{1},;H_{2}(X_{1})).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{*}{,}\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{*}{,}\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\operatorname{id},i_{0})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{*}{,}\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\operatorname{id},i_{0})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\operatorname{id},i_{1})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{*}{,}\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\operatorname{id},h)^{*}{,}\cong}

Here and in the remainder of this proof, we write
(\operatorname{id},i_{0})^{*},(\operatorname{id},i_{1})^{*}
and
(\operatorname{id},h^{*})
to emphasise the presence of the mapping cylinders; see e.g.
[
CK25
, Lemma 5.1]
.
The left-hand squares in the above diagram commute: each horizontal map is obtained by applying
F
to the coefficients.

Claim
.

The right hand square in the diagram (
7.14
) commutes:

(\operatorname{id},i_{0})^{*}\circ\Phi_{0}\circ F^{*}=(\operatorname{id},h)^{*}\circ(\operatorname{id},i_{1})^{*}\circ\Phi_{1}.

Proof.

Since
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 2
,
\operatorname{ev}\colon H^{2}(X_{1};H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
is surjective.
Hence it suffices to show that

(\operatorname{id},i_{0})^{*}\circ\Phi_{0}\circ F^{*}\circ\operatorname{ev}=(\operatorname{id},h)^{*}\circ(\operatorname{id},i_{1})^{*}\circ\Phi_{1}\circ\operatorname{ev}.

Using that
F^{*}\circ\operatorname{ev}\circ G^{!}=\operatorname{ev}
, the equality
\Phi_{i}\circ\operatorname{ev}=\delta_{i}
(from
Lemma

6.4
), and the naturality of the connecting homomorphism in the long exact sequence of a pair, we have

\displaystyle(\operatorname{id},i_{0})^{*}\circ\Phi_{0}\circ F^{*}\circ\operatorname{ev}

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id},i_{0})^{*}\circ\Phi_{0}\circ\operatorname{ev}\circ(G^{!})^{-1}

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id},i_{0})^{*}\circ\delta_{0}\circ(G^{!})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\delta_{0}\circ i_{0}^{*}\circ(G^{!})^{-1}.

Rewrite
i_{0}^{*}=h^{*}\circ i_{1}^{*}\circ G^{!}
as
i_{0}^{*}\circ(G^{!})^{-1}=h^{*}\circ i_{1}^{*}\colon H^{2}(X_{1};H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))\to H^{2}(Y_{0};H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
.
Using this, together with the equality
\Phi_{i}\circ\operatorname{ev}=\delta_{i}
(again
Lemma

6.4
), and the naturality of the connecting homomorphism in the long exact sequence of a pair, we have

\displaystyle\delta_{0}\circ i_{0}^{*}\circ(G^{!})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\delta_{0}\circ h^{*}\circ i_{1}^{*}

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id},h)^{*}\circ\delta_{1}\circ i_{1}^{*}

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id},h)^{*}\circ(\operatorname{id},i_{1})^{*}\circ\delta_{1}

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id},h)^{*}\circ(\operatorname{id},i_{1})^{*}\circ\Phi_{1}\circ\operatorname{ev}.

This completes the proof of the claim.
∎

We can now conclude the proof of the proposition.
Consider
\operatorname{id}_{H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}
as an element of the top left module of the diagram in (
7.14
): it maps to
\operatorname{id}_{H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}
on the top right and then down to
k_{X_{1},Y_{1}}^{\nu_{1}}
by the combination of Lemma
6.4
and Lemma
6.3
.
Since Lemmas
6.4
and
6.3
again ensure that
\operatorname{id}_{H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}
maps to
k_{X_{0},Y_{0}}^{\nu_{0}}
in the bottom left, the commutativity of the entire diagram implies that

F_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}}^{\nu_{0}})=(\operatorname{id},h)^{*}(k_{X_{1},Y_{1}}^{\nu_{1}})
,
as required.
∎

Next, we turn to the case where
H_{4}(\pi)=0
.

Proposition 7.15
.

Let
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
be
4
-dimensional Poincaré pairs with fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X_{i})\cong\pi
and
\iota_{j}\colon\pi_{1}(Y_{i})\to\pi_{1}(X_{i})
surjective for
i=0,1
.
Fix a homotopy equivalence
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
and assume there is a group isomorphism
u\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\pi_{1}(X_{1})
that satisfies
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
.
Finally, fix a
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-isomorphism
F\colon\pi_{2}(X_{0})\cong\pi_{2}(X_{1})
.

If

H_{4}(\pi)=0
and
H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}=0
, then
F_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}})=h^{*}(k_{X_{1},Y_{1}}).

Proof.

According to Convention
6.5
, given
\nu_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B\pi
and
\nu_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B\pi
, the proposition requires we prove
F_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}}^{\nu_{0}})=(\operatorname{id},h)^{*}(k_{X_{1},Y_{1}}^{\nu_{1}})
.
Using
c_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B\supset Y_{1}
for the
2
-type, we do so by constructing a
c\colon X_{0}\to B
with
c=c_{1}\circ h
and
F_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}}^{\nu_{0}})=(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}c_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}}^{\nu_{0}})=(\operatorname{id},h)^{*}(k_{X_{1},Y_{1}}^{\nu_{1}})
.

We begin by constructing the map
c
.
The dimension shifting argument used during the proof of Proposition
3.5
yields
H_{n}(\pi;\pi_{2}(B))\cong H_{n+3}(\pi)
.
Poincaré duality and the UCSS then give

H^{3}(X_{0},Y_{0};\pi_{2}(B))\cong H_{1}(X_{0};\pi_{2}(B))=\operatorname{Tor}_{1}^{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(\mathbb{Z},\pi_{2}(B))=H_{1}(\pi;\pi_{2}(B))\cong H_{4}(\pi)=0.

Obstruction theory then ensures that there is a
c^{\prime}\colon X_{0}\to B
with
c^{\prime}\simeq c_{1}\circ h
