and such that
c^{\prime}
is a
\pi_{1}
-isomorphism.
Apply the homotopy extension property to
c^{\prime}\simeq c_{1}\circ h
in order to obtain a map
c\simeq c^{\prime}\colon X_{0}\to B
with
c=c_{1}\circ h
.
Applying Theorem
6.2
with
F=(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}c_{*}
gives

(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}c_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}}^{\nu_{0}})=(\operatorname{id},h)^{*}(k_{X_{1},Y_{1}}^{\nu_{1}}).

It remains to prove that
F_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}}^{\nu_{0}})=(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}c_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}})
.
For
i=0,1
, Poincaré duality and the UCSS give isomorphisms

H_{3}(X_{i},Y_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong H^{1}(X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong H^{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).

The same reasoning also yields
H_{2}(Y_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong H^{1}(Y_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong H^{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, where the second isomorphism uses
H_{1}(Y_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}=0
.
The naturality of the UCSS implies that the connecting homomorphism
H_{3}(X_{i},Y_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(Y_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is an isomorphism.
We therefore obtain the short exact sequence

0\to H_{2}(X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\xrightarrow{(j_{i})_{*}}H_{2}(X_{i},Y_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{1}(Y_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to 0.

Consider the long exact sequences of coefficients in cohomology for
M(\nu_{0}|_{Y_{0}})
.
Since both
F
and
(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}c_{*}
induce
h_{*}
on
H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, we obtain the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{H^{2}(M(\nu_{0}|_{Y_{0}}),Y_{0};H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{H^{3}(M(\nu_{0}|_{Y_{0}}),Y_{0};H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{*}-(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}c_{*}}
\scriptstyle{(j_{0})_{*}}
\textstyle{\ldots}
\textstyle{H^{2}(M(\nu_{0}|_{Y_{0}}),Y_{0};H_{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{3}(M(\nu_{0}|_{Y_{0}}),Y_{0};H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])).}

Since
(j_{0})_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}}^{\nu_{0}})=0
(see
[
CK25
, Lemma 6.1]
),
k_{X_{0},Y_{0}}^{\nu_{0}}
pulls back to
H^{2}(M(\nu_{0}|_{Y_{0}}),Y_{0};H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
.
A diagram chase now yields
F_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}}^{\nu_{0}})=(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}c_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}}^{\nu_{0}})=(\operatorname{id},h)^{*}(k_{X_{1},Y_{1}}^{\nu_{1}}),
as needed.
∎

8.
Twisted Künneth calculations in
Y\times S^{1}

We begin our analysis of the secondary obstruction.
Since the subsequent sections involve heavy use of twisted cup and cross products, Section
8.1
introduces some background on the topic, whereas Section
8.2
then focuses on calculations in
Y\times S^{1}
.

8.1.
Twisted homology and cohomology

We recall the definition of twisted (co)homology as well as twisted cup, cap and cross products, mostly following the notation from
[
CN21
]
.
Further details can be found in the appendix.
Let
S
be a ring with involution.
In what follows, given a left
S
-module
M
, we write
\overline{V}
for the right
S
-module whose underlying group agrees with that of
V
but with the
S
-module structure
v\cdot s=\overline{s}v
for
v\in V
and
s\in S
.

Definition 8.1
.

Given a pair
(X,Y)
, a ring
R
and a
(R,\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)])
-bimodule
M
,
the twisted chain complex
and
twisted cochain complex
of
(X,Y)
with coefficients in
M
are respectively defined as the following chain complexes of left
R
-modules:

\displaystyle C_{*}(X,Y;M)

\displaystyle:=M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)]}C_{*}(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{Y}),

\displaystyle C^{*}(X,Y;M)

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{right-}\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)]}(\overline{C_{*}(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{Y}}),M).

The
twisted (co)homology of
(X,Y)
with coefficients in
M
is the homology of these chain complexes:

\displaystyle H_{*}(X,Y;M)

\displaystyle:=H_{*}(C_{*}(X,Y;M)),

\displaystyle H^{*}(X,Y;M)

\displaystyle:=H_{*}(C^{*}(X,Y;M)).

These are left
R
-modules
with actions given by
r\cdot(m\otimes\sigma)=rm\otimes\sigma
and
(r\cdot f)(\sigma)=rf(\sigma)
for
f\in C^{*}(X,Y;M),\sigma\in C_{*}(X,Y;M)
and
m\in M
.
Note also that
f(\gamma\sigma)=f(\sigma\cdot\gamma^{-1})=f(\sigma)\gamma^{-1}
for every
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(X)
.

Construction 8.2
.

Consider a
ring
R
with involution and two
(R,\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
–modules
M
and
N
.
We equip the abelian group
M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}N
with the structure of an
(R,\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\times R)
–bimodule, denoted
M\boxtimes N
, as follows.
The left and right actions of
R
are via

r\cdot(m\otimes n)\cdot s=(r\cdot m)\otimes(\overline{s}\cdot n)

for
r,s\in R
and
m\in M
and
n\in N
.
The right
\pi
–action is the diagonal action

(m\otimes n)\cdot g=(m\cdot g)\otimes(n\cdot g)

for
g\in\pi
and
m\in M
and
n\in N
.
As in
[
CN21
]
, it is to stress the diagonal action that we use the symbol
\boxtimes
instead of the more common
\otimes
.
Homology and cohomology with coefficients in
M\boxtimes N
inherits the structure of an
(R,R)
-bimodule.

This construction is relevant to twisted cup, cap and cross products.
These will be reviewed in detail in the appendix, but since they will be used heavily in the next section, we record some relevant facts.
For a space
X
and
(R,\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)])
-bimodules
M,N,
the
twisted cup product
and
twisted cap product
are
(R,R)
-linear pairings

\displaystyle\cup\colon H^{i}(X;M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\overline{H^{j}(X;N)}\to H^{i+j}(X;M\boxtimes N),

\displaystyle\cap\colon H^{i}(X;M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\overline{H_{k}(X;N)}\to H_{k-i}(X;M\boxtimes N).

Similarly for spaces
X_{1},X_{2}
, rings
R_{1},R_{2},R
and a
(R_{1},\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{1})]\times R)
-bimodule
M
as well as a
(R,\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{2})]\times R_{2})
-bimodule
N
, there are twisted cross products

\displaystyle\times\colon H_{k}(X_{1};M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\overline{H_{l}(X_{2};N)}\to H_{k+l}(X_{1}\times X_{2};M\boxtimes N),

\displaystyle\times\colon H^{k}(X_{1};M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\overline{H^{l}(X_{2};N)}\to H^{k+l}(X_{1}\times X_{2};M\boxtimes N).

Here we identify
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{1})\times\pi_{1}(X_{2})]
with
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{1})]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{2})]
and endow the
(R_{1},R_{2})
-bimodule
M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}N
with the right
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{1})\times\pi_{1}(X_{2})]
-diagonal action given by
(m_{1},m_{2})\cdot(\gamma_{1}\otimes\gamma_{2})=(m_{1}\gamma_{1},m_{2}\gamma_{2})
so that it becomes a
(R_{1},\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{1})\times\pi_{1}(X_{2})]\times R_{2})
-bimodule.

The definition and properties of these pairings will be discussed in greater detail in the appendix.
Presently, we note a helpful characterisation of the
0
-th twisted homology with
M\boxtimes N
-coefficients.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
X
be a
space, let
\varphi\colon\pi_{1}(X)\to\pi
be a surjection, let
\widehat{X}\to X
be the
\pi
-cover corresponding to
\ker(\varphi)
, and let
M
and
N
be
(R,\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
–bimodules.
The map
(M\boxtimes N)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}C_{0}(\widehat{X})\to M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\overline{N}
given by
(m\boxtimes n)\otimes[\operatorname{pt}]\mapsto m\otimes n
induces an isomorphism of
(R,R)
–modules

\varpi_{0}\colon H_{0}\big(X;M\boxtimes N\big)\to M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\overline{N},

where
\overline{N}
denotes
N
turned into a
(\mathbb{Z}[\pi],R)
–bimodule using the involutions on
R
and
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
.

Additionally, the map
m\otimes n\mapsto(m\boxtimes n)\otimes 1
induces an isomorphism of
(R,R)
–modules

M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\overline{N}\to(M\boxtimes N)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\mathbb{Z}.

Proof.

The first assertion follows because, as a left
R
-bimodule,
H_{0}\big(X;M\boxtimes N\big)
is isomorphic (via the augmentation) to the
R
-bimodule of coinvariants

M\boxtimes N\Big/\mathbb{Z}\langle 1-g\mid g\in\pi\rangle

[
Fri23
, Twisted
H_{0}
-Proposition]
.
That this is an isomorphism of
(R,R)
-bimodules now follows from the definitions of the actions.
The second assertion follows from a direct verification.
∎

8.2.
Calculations for
Y\times S^{1}

This section introduces a coefficient system on
Y\times S^{1}
that will be used frequently in the subsequent sections.
We then record a calculation involving this coefficient system and an application of the Künneth theorem.

Construction 8.4

(A coefficient system on
Y\times S^{1}
)
.

The composition
\pi_{1}(Y\times S^{1})\xrightarrow{p_{Y}}\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi
of the projection and inclusion induced maps endows the group ring
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
with the structure of a right
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(Y\times S^{1})]
-module, and thus of a
(\mathbb{Z}[\pi],\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(Y\times S^{1})])
-bimodule.

In order to apply the Künneth theorem; we describe an equivalent description of this module.
Endow
S^{1}
with the trivial coefficient system
\mathbb{Z}
.
Consider the projection
p_{S^{1}}\colon Y\times S^{1}\to S^{1}
.
Recall that
p_{Y}^{*}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\boxtimes_{\mathbb{Z}}p_{S^{1}}^{*}(\mathbb{Z})
denotes
p_{Y}^{*}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}p_{S^{1}}^{*}(\mathbb{Z})
with the diagonal
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(Y\times S^{1})]
-action.
We assert that multiplication gives rise to the following isomorphism of
(\mathbb{Z}[\pi],\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(Y\times S^{1})])
-bimodules:

\displaystyle\mu\colon p_{Y}^{*}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\boxtimes_{\mathbb{Z}}p_{S^{1}}^{*}(\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle\to p_{Y}^{*}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle a\otimes b

\displaystyle\mapsto ab.

The fact that this is an isomorphism of left
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-modules is clear.
For
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(Y\times S^{1})],a\in\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
and
b\in\mathbb{Z}
, the following equalities establish the right
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(Y\times S^{1})]
-linearity:

\mu((a\otimes b)\gamma)=\mu(a(p_{Y})_{*}(\gamma)\otimes b(p_{S^{1}})_{*}(\gamma))=\mu(a(p_{Y})_{*}(\gamma)\otimes b)=a(p_{Y})_{*}(\gamma)b=\mu(a\otimes b)\gamma.

Proposition 8.5
.

Using the notation above, the cross product induces isomorphissm

\displaystyle\operatorname{Kun}\colon H_{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\oplus H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\to H_{2}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\boxtimes\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{2}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),

\displaystyle(a,b)

\displaystyle\mapsto i_{Y}(a)+(b\times 1_{H_{1}(S^{1})}).

\displaystyle\operatorname{Kun}\colon H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\oplus H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\to H^{2}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\boxtimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z})\cong H^{2}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle(a,b)

\displaystyle\mapsto(a\times 1_{H^{0}(S^{1})})+(b\times 1_{H^{1}(S^{1})})=p_{Y}^{*}(a)+(p_{Y}^{*}(b)\cup p_{S^{1}}^{*}(1_{H^{1}(S^{1})})).

Proof.

Apply the Künneth theorem from Proposition
A.13
with
M=R_{1}=\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
and
N=R_{2}=\mathbb{Z}
.
Note that both
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are free abelian groups, so the theorem does indeed apply.
We identify the
(\mathbb{Z}[\pi],\mathbb{Z})
-bimodule
M_{1}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}M_{2}=\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}
with
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
.
Since the abelian group
H_{i}(S^{1})
is either free or zero, the
\operatorname{Tor}_{1}^{\mathbb{Z}}
terms and
\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{1}
terms (in cohomology) vanish.
For
i=1,2
, we then use the identifications
H_{i}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}H_{2-i}(S^{1})\cong H_{i}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and similarly in cohomology.
∎

9.
The secondary obstruction and even forms

The goal of this section is to analyse the secondary obstruction and to prove Theorem
2.22
.
Continuing with Notation
4.1
, the main technical result of this section (namely Theorem
9.4
) shows that for any sesquilinear form
q
on
H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, there exists a homotopy
\mathbf{H}=(H_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
from
c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}
to itself, such that the homotopy
\Xi(\mathbf{H})\colon B\to B
obtained by applying homotopy extension belongs to
\mathcal{G}
and satisfies
\Xi(\mathbf{H})\cdot b(c_{0},c_{1})=b(c_{0},c_{1})+q+q^{*}
.
Theorem
2.22
will follow fairly promptly: roughly speaking, when
b(c_{0},c_{1})
is even, it will then be possible to modify
c_{0}
, resulting in a new
3
-connected map for which the resulting secondary invariant vanishes.

In order to formulate the aforementioned technical theorem, we first introduce some notation and describe the “homotopy extension map”
\Xi
more precisely.

Notation 9.1
.

Set
Y:=Y_{1}

and assume that
c_{1}|_{Y}\colon Y\to B
is the inclusion.
We also fix once and for all basepoints
y_{0}\in Y
and
b_{0}:=c_{1}(y_{0})\in B
.
We use
(y_{0},1)\in Y\times S^{1}
as a basepoint for
Y\times S^{1}
.
In what follows, we consider homotopies
\mathbf{H}=(H_{t}\colon Y\to B)_{t\in[0,1]}
from
c_{1}|_{Y}
to
c_{1}|_{Y}
.
Occasionally we will think of
\mathbf{H}
as a map
\mathbf{H}\colon(Y\times[0,1],Y\times\{0,1\})\to(B,Y).

More frequently, since
\mathbf{H}
is a map
\mathbf{H}\colon Y\times[0,1]\to B
with
H_{0}=c_{1}|_{Y}=H_{1}
, we will think of
\mathbf{H}
as a map
\mathbf{H}\colon Y\times S^{1}\to B
with
\mathbf{H}|_{Y\times\{1\}}=c_{1}|_{Y}
the inclusion.
We say that
\mathbf{H}
is
trivial on the
S^{1}
-factor
if the map
S^{1}\to B,t\mapsto H_{t}(y_{0})
is nullhomotopic.
Consider

\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1}|_{Y}):=\{\mathbf{H}:=(H_{t}\colon Y\to B)_{t\in[0,1]}\mid H_{0}=c_{1}|_{Y}=H_{1}\text{ and\penalty\ $\mathbf{H}$ is trivial on\penalty\ $S^{1}$-factor}\}.

Observe that the condition
H_{0}=c_{1}|_{Y}=H_{1}
ensures that
\mathbf{H}
is basepoint preserving.

Construction 9.2

(The homotopy extension map
\Xi
)
.

We construct a map

\Xi\colon\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1}|_{Y})\to\operatorname{hAut}_{Y}(B),

where, recall,
\operatorname{hAut}_{Y}(B)
denotes the group of rel.
Y
homotopy classes of maps
B\to B
.
Apply the homotopy extension property to a homotopy
\mathbf{H}=(H_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
with
H_{0}=c_{1}|_{Y}=H_{1}
.
The outcome is a homotopy
\widetilde{H}_{t}\colon B\to B
that extends
\mathbf{H}
and satisfies
\widetilde{H}_{0}=\operatorname{id}_{B}
.
Consider the map

\displaystyle\Xi\colon\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1}|_{Y})

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{hAut}_{Y}(B)

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}

\displaystyle\mapsto\widetilde{H}_{1}.

Here note that
\widetilde{H}_{1}\colon B\to B
is a homotopy equivalence because
\widetilde{H}_{1}\simeq\widetilde{H}_{0}=\operatorname{id}_{B}
by construction.
Also, the homotopy equivalence
\widetilde{H}_{1}
is rel.
Y
because
\widetilde{H}_{1}|_{Y}=H_{1}=c_{1}|_{Y}
is the inclusion.

In general, the homotopy equivalence
\Xi(\mathbf{H})
does not belong to the group

\mathcal{G}=\{\varphi\in\operatorname{hAut}_{Y_{1}}(B)\mid(\varphi\circ c_{1})^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}\}.

and so it is not possible to use
\Xi(\mathbf{H})
to act on
b(c_{0},c_{1})
.
We therefore consider the subset of
\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1}|_{Y})
of homotopies whose image under
\Xi
do belong to
\mathcal{G}
.

Construction 9.3

(The subset
\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1}|_{Y})(c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})
.)
.

We already noted in Construction
9.2
that for any homotopy
\mathbf{H}=(H_{t}\colon Y\to B)_{t\in[0,1]}
with
H_{0}=c_{1}|_{Y}=H_{1}
, the homotopy equivalence
\Xi(\mathbf{H})=\widetilde{H}_{1}
satisfies
\widetilde{H}_{1}\circ c_{1}|_{Y}=H_{1}=c_{1}|_{Y}
.
We then consider the set of those
\mathbf{H}
such that
\Xi(\mathbf{H})
satisfies the second defining condition of
\mathcal{G}
:

\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1}|_{Y})(c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}):=\{\mathbf{H}\in\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1}|_{Y})\mid(\Xi(\mathbf{H})\circ c_{1})^{*}(b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}\}.

This way, the inclusion
\Xi(\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1}|_{Y})(c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})\subset\mathcal{G}
follows from the definition of
\mathcal{G}
.

The main technical result of this section is now the following.

Theorem 9.4
.

For any homotopy
\mathbf{H}\in\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1})(c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})
, there is a corresponding sesquilinear form
q_{\mathbf{H}}\in\Sesq(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
such that the homotopy equivalence
\Xi(\mathbf{H})\colon B\to B
acts on the set
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
of hermitian forms as

\Xi(\mathbf{H})\cdot\lambda=\lambda+q_{\mathbf{H}}+q_{\mathbf{H}}^{*}\quad\quad\text{ for any }\lambda\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).

Conversely,
if the map
\operatorname{ev}^{*}\colon H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{**}\to H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}
is an isomorphism and

•

either the module
H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is projective,

•

or
\pi
is finite and
H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong I\oplus\mathbb{Z}[\pi]^{\oplus k}
for some
k\geq 0
,

then for any sesquilinear form
q\in\Sesq(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
, there is a homotopy
\mathbf{H}\in\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1})(c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})
with
q=q_{\mathbf{H}}.

The proof of this theorem occupies the remainder of this section.
Given a homotopy
\mathbf{H}
, Section
9.1
constructs the sequilinear form
q_{\mathbf{H}}
and determines the action of
\Xi(\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1})(c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}))
on
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])).

Section
9.2
proves that for any
q\in\Sesq(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
, there exists a homotopy
\mathbf{H}\in\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1})(c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})
with
q=\penalty\ q_{\mathbf{H}}
.

Before embarking on this proof, we attempt to share some intuition.

Remark 9.5
.

We outline the intuition underlying Theorem
9.4
and its proof.
We emphasise that all of what follows is informal.
First, recall the intuition underlying the definition of the hermitian form
b(c_{0},c_{1})
.
Given
x\in H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, since
\widetilde{X}_{0}
and
\widetilde{X}_{1}
are simply-connected, loops representing
\operatorname{PD}_{y}(x)
are nullhomotopic.
This leads to immersed discs
D_{0}(x)\subset\widetilde{X}_{0}
and
D_{1}(x)\subset\widetilde{X}_{1}
.
Taking the union of these discs yields an immersed sphere
S(x)=D_{0}(x)\cup D_{1}(x)
representing the Poincaré dual of
c^{*}(x^{\prime})\in H^{2}(X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.
We then think of
b(c_{0},c_{1})(x,y)
as
\lambda_{X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}}(S(x),S(y))
.

The main idea of this section is that the discs can be modified in a way that changes
b(c_{0},c_{1})
by an even form.
Here are slightly more (yet still informal) details underlying this idea.

•

A map
\phi\colon H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
gives rise to a homotopy
\mathbf{H}\colon Y\times I\to Y\to B
from
c_{1}
to
c_{1}
.
Here is the intuition for building
\mathbf{H}
:
Modify the projection
\operatorname{proj}_{1}\colon Y\times I\to Y
by mapping
2
-cells of the form
I\times\alpha
to
\operatorname{proj}_{1}(I\times\alpha)+\phi(\alpha)
and compose the resulting map
Y\times I\to Y
with
c_{1}\colon Y\to B.

•

Implanting this homotopy in a collar of
\partial\widetilde{X}_{i}
changes
b(c_{0},c_{1})
, thought of as a hermitian form on
H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, by
q_{\mathbf{H}}+q_{\mathbf{H}}^{*}
where
q_{\mathbf{H}}
is the sesquilinear form defined by
\phi
via
H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\xrightarrow{\phi}H_{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}
.
Here, the idea is that changing
c_{1}
in a collar changes the sphere
S(x)=D_{0}(x)\cup D_{1}(x)
by adding
\phi(x)
in the collar of the discs
D_{0}(x)
and
D_{1}(x)
.
This changes
\lambda_{X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}}(S(x),S(y))
by adding the expression
\lambda_{Y}(\phi(x),y)+\lambda_{Y}(x,\phi(y))
which corresponds to the expression
q_{\mathbf{H}}+q_{\mathbf{H}}^{*}
.

Unfortunately, the presence of the maps
c_{0},c_{1}
and
c
as well as numerous algebraic constraints make this geometric idea quite challenging and technical to carry out.

9.1.
The action on hermitian forms

This section defines the sesquilinear form
q_{\mathbf{H}}
mentioned in Theorem
9.4
and studies the action of
\Xi(\mathbf{H})
on
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
.
The definition of
\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1}|_{Y})(c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})
from Construction
9.3
is simple to state but inconvenient to understand the action on
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])).

The objective is thus to reformulate the definition of
\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1}|_{Y})(c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})
so as to better understand this action.
The main challenge is to find a condition on a homotopy
\mathbf{H}
that is equivalent to it satisfying
(\Xi(\mathbf{H})\circ c_{1})^{*}(b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
.

Recalling that
j\colon B\to(B,Y_{1})
denotes the inclusion, we begin by recording a result concerning the fundamental class of
Y\times S^{1}
.

Lemma 9.6
.

The following equation holds:

(9.7)

(\widetilde{H}_{1}\circ c_{1})_{*}([X_{1},Y])=(c_{1})_{*}([X_{1},Y])+j_{*}(\mathbf{H}_{*}([Y\times S^{1}]))\in H_{4}(B,Y).

Proof.

Let
M(i)
be the mapping cylinder of the inclusion
i\colon Y\to X_{1}
. Since the inclusions of
Y
into
M(i)
given by
Y\times\{0\}
and
Y\times\{1\}
are homotopic, there is a map
f\colon(X_{1},Y)\to(M(i),Y\times\{1\})
homotopic to the inclusion. In particular,
f
is a homotopy equivalence and thus degree one.

Consider the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{1},Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M(i),Y\times\{1\})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{1}\times I,Y\times\{1\})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B,Y).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times\{1\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}\cup\mathbf{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathbf{H}}\circ(c_{1}\times\operatorname{id})}

The lower triangle commutes by definition of
\widetilde{\mathbf{H}}
.
The upper triangle commutes up to homotopy.
Since the diagram commutes up to homotopy and
f
has degree one,

(9.8)

(\widetilde{H}_{1}\circ c_{1})_{*}([X_{1},Y])=(c_{1}\cup\mathbf{H})_{*}([M(i),Y\times\{1\}]).

In order to evaluate
(c_{1}\cup\mathbf{H})_{*}([M(i),Y\times\{1\}])
, consider the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{H_{4}(M(i),Y\times\{1\})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(c_{1}\cup\mathbf{H})_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{4}(M(i),Y\times\{0,1\})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{(c_{1}\cup\mathbf{H})_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{4}(X_{1},Y)\oplus H_{4}(Y\times I,Y\times\{0,1\})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}(c_{1})_{*}&(\mathbf{H})_{*}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{H_{4}(B,Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{H_{4}(B,Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{H_{4}(B,Y).}

Since the top row maps
[(M(i),Y\times\{1\}]
to
([X_{1},Y],[Y\times I,Y\times\{0,1\}]
, we deduce that

(9.9)

(c_{1}\cup\mathbf{H})_{*}[M(i),Y\times\{1\}]=(c_{1})_{*}([X_{1},Y])+\mathbf{H}_{*}([Y\times[0,1],Y\times\{0,1\}]).

Next, note that the quotient map
q\colon(Y\times I,Y\times\{0,1\})\to(Y\times S^{1},Y)
has degree one
and consider the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{4}(Y\times I,Y\times\{0,1\})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{4}(Y\times S^{1},Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{4}(Y\times S^{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{4}(B,Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{4}(B,Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{4}(B).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{H}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{H}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{H}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}}

Since the top isomorphisms in the top row map fundamental class to fundamental class, it follows that
\mathbf{H}_{*}([Y\times I,Y\times\{0,1\}])=j_{*}(\mathbf{H}_{*}([Y\times S^{1}]))
.
Combining this with (
9.8
) and (
9.9
) establishes (
9.7
) and thus concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

In order to understand the equation
(\Xi(\mathbf{H})\circ c_{1})^{*}(b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
, we need to unwind the definition of
(\Xi(\mathbf{H})\circ c_{1})^{*}(b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})
.
This requires some notation.

Notation 9.10
.

Consider the multiplication map
\operatorname{mult}\colon\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\overline{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]},(x,y)\mapsto x\overline{y}

and, given a group
\pi
, a
space
X
and a surjection
\pi_{1}(X)\twoheadrightarrow\pi
, the composition

\operatorname{mult}_{0}\colon H_{0}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\boxtimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\xrightarrow{\varpi_{0},\cong}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\overline{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{mult}}\mathbb{Z}[\pi].

Here the isomorphism
\varpi_{0}
was introduced in Lemma
8.3
.

Given a pair
(X,A)
, a calculation (carried out in Lemma
A.6
) shows that for
\varphi\in H^{k}(X,A;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and
x\in H_{k}(X,A;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, the following equality holds:

(9.11)

\operatorname{mult}_{0}(\varphi\cap x)=\overline{\langle\varphi,x\rangle}\in\mathbb{Z}[\pi].

Here recall that
\langle\varphi,x\rangle
denotes the evaluation of
\varphi
at
x
.

Furthermore, another calculation (carried out in Lemma
A.9
) shows that for
\sigma\in H_{4}(X)
, the pairing
b_{\sigma}\colon H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi],(x,y)\mapsto\langle y,x\cap\sigma\rangle
agrees with the hermitian pairing

\displaystyle b_{\sigma}^{\cup}\colon H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]

(9.12)

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\operatorname{mult}_{0}((x\cup y)\cap\sigma).

The next lemma takes a first pass at unpacking the definition of
(\Xi(\mathbf{H})\circ c_{1})^{*}(b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})
.

Lemma 9.13
.

For
a\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and
r\in H^{2}(B,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, the following equalities hold:

\displaystyle(\Xi(\mathbf{H})\circ c_{1})^{*}(b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})(r,a)=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}(r,a)+\operatorname{mult}_{0}((\mathbf{H}^{*}(j^{*}(r))\cup\mathbf{H}^{*}(a))\cap[Y\times S^{1}])\in\mathbb{Z}[\pi].

Proof.

Recall that, by definition,
b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}(x,y)=\langle y,x\cap[X_{1},Y]\rangle.

The definition of
\mathbf{H}
and the naturality of the evaluation map, then imply that

(\Xi(\mathbf{H})\circ c_{1})^{*}(b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})(r,a)=\langle(\widetilde{H}_{1}\circ c_{1})^{*}(a),(\widetilde{H}_{1}\circ c_{1})^{*}(r)\cap[X_{1},Y]\rangle=\langle a,r\cap(\widetilde{H}_{1}\circ c_{1})_{*}([X_{1},Y])\rangle.

The lemma therefore hinges on understanding
(\widetilde{H}_{1}\circ c_{1})_{*}([X_{1},Y])
.
Lemma
9.6
and (
9.10
) yield

\displaystyle(\Xi(\mathbf{H})\circ c_{1})^{*}(b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})(r,a)

\displaystyle=\langle a,r\cap(c_{1})_{*}([X_{1},Y])\rangle+\langle a,r\cap j_{*}(\mathbf{H}_{*}([Y\times S^{1}]))\rangle

\displaystyle=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}(r,a)+\langle\mathbf{H}^{*}(a),\mathbf{H}^{*}(j^{*}(r))\cap[Y\times S^{1}]\rangle

\displaystyle=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}(r,a)+b_{Y\times S^{1}}(\mathbf{H}^{*}(j^{*}(r)),\mathbf{H}^{*}(a))

\displaystyle=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}(r,a)+\operatorname{mult}_{0}((\mathbf{H}^{*}(j^{*}(r))\cup\mathbf{H}^{*}(a))\cap[Y\times S^{1}]).

This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma
9.13
implies that, in order to understand the pairing
(\Xi(\mathbf{H})\circ c_{1})^{*}(b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})
, we need to study the pairing
(\alpha,\beta)\mapsto\operatorname{mult}_{0}((\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)\cup\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta))\cap[Y\times S^{1}])
for
\alpha,\beta\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.
This requires the following construction.

Construction 9.14
.

Associated to the homotopy
\mathbf{H}
is the map
\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}\colon H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}.

obtained by composing
\mathbf{H}^{*}
with the inverse of the Künneth isomorphism and the projection onto the second component:

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}\colon H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\xrightarrow{\mathbf{H}^{*}}H^{2}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Kun}^{-1}}H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\oplus H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\operatorname{proj}_{2}}H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\operatorname{ev}}H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}

For brevity we set
\psi:=\operatorname{proj}_{2}\circ\operatorname{Kun}^{-1}
.

The next couple of lemmas aim to prove the following technical result.
The reader wishing to skip ahead to the proof of this result can instead consult the proof following Lemma
9.20
.

Proposition 9.15
.

For
\alpha,\beta\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, we have the following equation in
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
:

\operatorname{mult}_{0}((\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)\cup\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta))\cap[Y\times S^{1}])=\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}(\beta)(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha)\cap[Y])+\overline{\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}(\alpha)(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\beta)\cap[Y])}.

The strategy is as follows.
Lemma
9.16
describes
(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)\cup\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta))\cap[Y\times S^{1}]
as a sum of two expressions.
Lemma
9.18
investigates the first and shows that after applying
\operatorname{mult}_{0}
, it reduces to
\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}(\beta)(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha)\cap[Y])
.
Lemma
9.20
applies a similar reasoning to the second term and the conclusion then follows promptly.

We begin by studying
(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)\cup\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta))\cap[Y\times S^{1}]
.

Lemma 9.16
.

For
\alpha,\beta\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, we have the following equation in
H_{0}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\boxtimes\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)

\displaystyle\cup\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta))\cap[Y\times S^{1}]=

\displaystyle\left(\overbrace{p_{Y}^{*}(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha))}^{\in H^{2}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}\cup\left(\overbrace{p_{Y}^{*}(\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta)))}^{\in H^{1}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}\cup\overbrace{p_{S^{1}}^{*}(1_{H^{1}(S^{1})})}^{\in H^{1}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z})}\right)\right)\cap[Y\times S^{1}]

(9.17)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left(\left(\underbrace{p_{Y}^{*}(\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)))}_{\in H^{1}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}\cup\underbrace{p_{S^{1}}^{*}(1_{H^{1}(S^{1})})}_{\in H^{1}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z})}\right)\cup\underbrace{p_{Y}^{*}(c_{1}|^{*}_{Y}(\beta))}_{\in H^{2}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}\right)\cap[Y\times S^{1}].

Proof.

Given
\alpha,\beta\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, we start by understanding the cup product
\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)\cup\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta).

To this effect, we study the images of
\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)
and
\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta)
under the inverse Künneth isomorphism

\displaystyle H^{2}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\boxtimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z})\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\oplus H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle z\mapsto(\iota_{Y}^{*}(z),\psi(z)).

Here
\iota_{Y}\colon Y\to Y\times S^{1}
denotes the inclusion, whereas the formula for
\psi
is irrelevant.
Applying the formula for the twisted Künneth isomorphism from Proposition
8.5
, we then obtain the following equality in
H^{2}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\boxtimes\mathbb{Z}):

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Kunneth}\circ\operatorname{Kunneth}^{-1}(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha))

\displaystyle=p_{Y}^{*}(\iota_{Y}^{*}(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)))+p_{Y}^{*}(\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)))\cup p_{S^{1}}^{*}(1_{H^{1}(S^{1})})

\displaystyle=p_{Y}^{*}(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha))+p_{Y}^{*}(\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)))\cup p_{S^{1}}^{*}(1_{H^{1}(S^{1})}).

For
\alpha,\beta\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, we use this to calculate
\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)\cup\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta)
.
To make sense of this cup product, it is helpful to return to thinking of
H^{2}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\boxtimes\mathbb{Z})
as
H^{2}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
so that this cup product belongs to
H^{4}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\boxtimes\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.

First,
H^{4}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\boxtimes\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
implies

\overbrace{p_{Y}^{*}(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha))}^{\in H^{2}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}\cup\overbrace{p_{Y}^{*}(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\beta))}^{\in H^{2}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}=p_{Y}^{*}(\overbrace{c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha)\cup c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\beta)}^{\in H^{4}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\boxtimes\mathbb{Z}[\pi])})=0.

Consider the isomorphism
\operatorname{flip}_{23}\colon\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\boxtimes\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\boxtimes\mathbb{Z}\boxtimes\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\boxtimes\mathbb{Z}\boxtimes\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\boxtimes\mathbb{Z}

given by
\operatorname{flip}_{23}(a\otimes b\otimes c\otimes d)=a\otimes c\otimes b\otimes d.

Write
(\operatorname{flip}_{23})_{*}
for the induced map on twisted cohomology.
The graded commutativity of the cup product and
H^{2}(S^{1})=0
imply that

\displaystyle\left(p_{Y}^{*}(\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)))\cup p_{S^{1}}^{*}(\overbrace{1_{H^{1}(S^{1})})}^{\in H^{1}(S^{1})}\right)\cup\left(p_{Y}^{*}(\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta)))\cup p_{S^{1}}^{*}(\overbrace{1_{H^{1}(S^{1})})}^{\in H^{1}(S^{1})}\right)

\displaystyle=-(\operatorname{flip}_{23})_{*}\left(p_{Y}^{*}(\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)))\cup p_{Y}^{*}(\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta)))\cup\underbrace{p_{S^{1}}^{*}(1_{H^{1}(S^{1})})\cup p_{S^{1}}^{*}(1_{H^{1}(S^{1})})}_{=0}\right)=0.

The announced calculation of
(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)\cup\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta))\cap[Y\times S^{1}]
now follows readily.
∎

We successively analyze the two summands that appear in Lemma
9.16
.

Lemma 9.18
.

The following equality holds in
H_{0}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\boxtimes\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
:

\displaystyle\left(\overbrace{p_{Y}^{*}(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha))}^{\in H^{2}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}\cup\left(\overbrace{p_{Y}^{*}(\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta)))}^{\in H^{1}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}\cup\overbrace{p_{S^{1}}^{*}(1_{H^{1}(S^{1})})}^{\in H^{1}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z})}\right)\right)\cap[Y\times S^{1}]

(9.19)

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\overbrace{c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha)}^{\in H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}\cup\overbrace{\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta))}^{\in H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}\right)\cap[Y]\right)\times\overbrace{1_{H_{0}(S^{1};\mathbb{Z})}}^{\in H_{0}(S^{1};\mathbb{Z})}.

Furthermore, after applying
\operatorname{mult}_{0}
, the following equality holds in
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
:

\operatorname{mult}_{0}\left(\left(p_{Y}^{*}(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha))\cup\left(p_{Y}^{*}(\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta)))\cup p_{S^{1}}^{*}(1_{H^{1}(S^{1})})\right)\right)\cap[Y\times S^{1}]\right)=\operatorname{mult}_{0}\left((c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha)\cup\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta)))\cap[Y]\right).

Proof.

First, using the associativity
and naturality of the cup product,
the left hand side of (
9.18
) can be rewritten as

\left(\left(\overbrace{p_{Y}^{*}(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha)\cup\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta)))}^{\in H^{3}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\boxtimes\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}\right)\cup\overbrace{p_{S^{1}}^{*}(1_{H^{1}(S^{1})})}^{\in H^{1}(Y\times S^{1})}\right)\cap[Y\times S^{1}].\penalty\

Dimensional considerations ensure that
[Y\times S^{1}]=[Y]\times[S^{1}]\in H_{4}(Y\times S^{1}).

Using the definition of the cross product we can therefore rewrite the previous equality as

\left(\left(\overbrace{c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha)}^{\in H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}\cup\overbrace{\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta))}^{\in H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}\right)\times\overbrace{1_{H^{1}(S^{1})}}^{\in H^{1}(S^{1})}\right)\cap([Y]\times[S^{1}]).

Using properties of the cross product see e.g.
[
CN21
, Lemma 5.2 item (2)]
), this equals

\displaystyle\overbrace{(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha)\cup\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta)))\cap[Y])}^{\in H_{0}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}\times\overbrace{(1_{H^{1}(S^{1})}\cap[S^{1}])}^{\in H_{0}(S^{1};\mathbb{Z})}=\overbrace{(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha)\cup\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta)))\cap[Y])}^{\in H_{0}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}\times\overbrace{1_{H_{0}(S^{1})}}^{\in H_{0}(S^{1};\mathbb{Z})}.

To obtain the last sentence of the lemma, apply Remark
A.14
from the appendix.
∎

We focus on the second summand of Lemma
9.16
, namely

\left(\left(\overbrace{p_{Y}^{*}(\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)))}^{\in H^{1}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}\cup\overbrace{p_{S^{1}}^{*}(1_{H^{1}(S^{1})})}^{\in H^{1}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z})}\right)\cup\overbrace{p_{Y}^{*}(c_{1}|^{*}_{Y}(\beta))}^{\in H^{2}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}\right)\cap[Y\times S^{1}].

Since twisted cup products only satisfy graded commutativity “up to isomorphism”, we cannot immediately exchange the terms in this expression and apply the same reasoning as in Lemma
9.18
.
Fortunately, this becomes possible after applying
\operatorname{mult}_{0}
, as the next lemma demonstrates.

Lemma 9.20
.

The following equality holds in
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{mult}_{0}\left(\left(\left(p_{Y}^{*}(\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)))\cup p_{S^{1}}^{*}(1_{H^{1}(S^{1})})\right)\cup p_{Y}^{*}(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\beta))\right)\cap[Y\times S^{1}]\right)

\displaystyle=\overline{\operatorname{mult}_{0}\left(\left(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\beta)\cup\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)\right)\cap[Y]\right)}.

Proof.

The first of the following equalities follows from the symmetry of twisted cup products (see
Lemma

A.5
), whereas the second follows as in the proof of
Lemma

9.18
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{mult}_{0}\left(\left(\left(p_{Y}^{*}(\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)))\cup p_{S^{1}}^{*}(1_{H^{1}(S^{1})})\right)\cup p_{Y}^{*}(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\beta))\right)\cap[Y\times S^{1}]\right)

\displaystyle=\overline{\operatorname{mult}_{0}\left(\left(p_{Y}^{*}(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\beta))\cup\left(p_{Y}^{*}(\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)))\cup p_{S^{1}}^{*}(1_{H^{1}(S^{1})})\right)\right)\cap[Y\times S^{1}]\right)}

\displaystyle=\overline{\operatorname{mult}_{0}\left(\left(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\beta)\cup\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)\right)\cap[Y]\right)}.

This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

We can conclude the proof of Proposition
9.15
which, recall, states that for
\alpha,\beta\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, we have the following equation in
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
:

\operatorname{mult}_{0}((\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)\cup\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta))\cap[Y\times S^{1}])=\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}(\beta)(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha)\cap[Y])+\overline{\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}(\alpha)(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\beta)\cap[Y])}.

Proof of Proposition
9.15
.

The combination of Lemmas
9.16
,
9.18
and
9.20
gives

\displaystyle\operatorname{mult}_{0}((\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)\cup\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta))\cap[Y\times S^{1}])

\displaystyle=\operatorname{mult}_{0}((c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha)\cup\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta)))\cap[Y])+\overline{\operatorname{mult}_{0}\left(\left(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\beta)\cup\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)\right)\cap[Y]\right)}.

Use the symmetry of twisted cup products (see
Lemma

A.5
),
to rewrite this as

\overline{\operatorname{mult}_{0}\left(\left(\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta))\cup c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha)\right)\cap[Y]\right)}+\operatorname{mult}_{0}\left(\left(\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha))\cup c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\beta)\right)\cap[Y]\right).

Applying the equality
(x\cup y)\cap z=x\cap(y\cap z)
and the
equality
\operatorname{mult}_{0}(\varphi\cap x)=\overline{\langle\varphi,x\rangle}\in\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
from (
9.11
) now yields:

\displaystyle\overline{\operatorname{mult}_{0}(\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta))\cap(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha)\cap[Y])}+\operatorname{mult}_{0}(\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha))\cap(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\beta)\cap[Y])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\beta)),c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha)\cap[Y]\rangle+\overline{\langle\psi(\mathbf{H}^{*}(\alpha)),c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\beta)\cap[Y]\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}(\beta)(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha)\cap[Y])+\overline{\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}(\alpha)(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\beta)\cap[Y])}.

This concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

The next proposition leads to the announced reformulation of the set
\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1}|_{Y})(c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}).

Proposition 9.21
.

Given
\mathbf{H}\in\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1}|_{Y})
, the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

(\Xi(\mathbf{H})\circ c_{1})^{*}(b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
.

(2)

The map
\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}\colon H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
factors through
H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, meaning that there exists a homomorphism
\phi\colon H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
such that
\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}=\phi\circ c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}:

\textstyle{H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{H}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{H^{2}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}}
\textstyle{H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}.}
\textstyle{H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}

Proof.

Given
\alpha,\beta\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, set

b_{\mathbf{H}}(\alpha,\beta):=\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}(\beta)(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha)\cap[Y])+\overline{\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}(\alpha)(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\beta)\cap[Y])}.

Lemma
9.13
and Proposition
9.15
imply that for every
a\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and
r\in H^{2}(B,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
,

(\Xi(\mathbf{H})\circ c_{1})^{*}(b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})(r,a)=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}(r,a)+b_{\mathbf{H}}(j^{*}(r),a).

It therefore suffices to show that
b_{\mathbf{H}}(j^{*}(r),a)=0
for every
a\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and
r\in H^{2}(B,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
if and only if there exists a
\phi\in\operatorname{Hom}(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*})
such that
\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}=\phi\circ c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}.

We first assume that
\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}=\phi\circ c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}
exists and prove that
b_{\mathbf{H}}(j^{*}(r),a)=0
for every
a\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and
r\in H^{2}(B,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.
This follows because, by exactness of the sequence of the pair
(B,Y)
, we have
c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}\circ j^{*}=0
and therefore

\displaystyle b_{\mathbf{H}}(j^{*}(r),a)

\displaystyle=\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}(a)(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(j^{*}(r))\cap[Y])+\overline{\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}(j^{*}(r))(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(a)\cap[Y])}

\displaystyle=\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}(a)(\underbrace{c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(j^{*}(r))}_{=0}\cap[Y])+\overline{\phi(\underbrace{c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(j^{*}(r))}_{=0})(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(a)\cap[Y])}=0.

We now prove the converse: we assume that
b_{\mathbf{H}}(j^{*}(r),a)=0
for every
a\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and every
r\in H^{2}(B,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and prove that
\phi
exists.
Consider the exact sequence

\textstyle{H^{2}(B,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(c_{1})|^{*}_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{0.}
\textstyle{H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}}

The rightmost zero follows from the fact, proved in Proposition
4.8
, that
(c_{1})|^{*}_{Y}
is surjective.
This diagram shows that proving
\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}\circ j^{*}=0
suffices to establish the existence of
\phi
.
To do so, we first note that by exactness we have for every
a\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and
r\in H^{2}(B,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=b_{\mathbf{H}}(j^{*}(r),a)=\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}(a)(\overbrace{c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(j^{*}(r))}^{=0}\cap[Y])+\overline{\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}(j^{*}(r))(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(a)\cap[Y])}=\overline{\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}(j^{*}(r))(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(a)\cap[Y])}.

Since the map
c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}\colon H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is surjective, this implies

\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}(j^{*}(r))(x)=0
for every
x\in H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.
It follows that
\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}(j^{*}(r))=0\in H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}
for every
r\in H^{2}(B,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.
As mentioned above, this implies that
\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}=\phi\circ c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}
for some map
\phi\colon H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}
, as required.
∎

In order to describe the action of
\Xi(\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1}|_{Y})(c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}))
on
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
, we construct the sesqulinear form
q_{\mathbf{H}}
that appears in the statement of Theorem
9.4
.

Construction 9.22

(The sesquilinear form
q_{\mathbf{H}}\in\Sesq(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
)
.

Given a homotopy
\mathbf{H}\in\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1}|_{Y})(c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})
and a map
\phi\colon H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}
with
\phi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{H}}=\phi\circ c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}
(which exists by Proposition
9.21
), consider the pairing

\displaystyle q_{\mathbf{H}}\colon H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]

\displaystyle(a,b)

\displaystyle\mapsto\phi(b)(a\cap[Y]).

The next proposition describes the action of
\Xi(\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1}|_{Y})(c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}))
on
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
thereby proving the first half of Theorem
9.4
.

Proposition 9.23
.

Given a homotopy
\mathbf{H}\in\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1}|_{Y})(c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})
, the action of
\Xi(\mathbf{H})\in\mathcal{G}
on
\lambda\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
is by

\Xi(\mathbf{H})\cdot\lambda=\lambda+q_{\mathbf{H}}+q_{\mathbf{H}}^{*}

where
q_{\mathbf{H}}\in\operatorname{Hom}(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*})
is the pairing from Construction
9.22
associated to
\mathbf{H}
and to a
\phi\colon H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}
with
\phi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{H}}=\phi\circ c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}
.

Proof.

Recall that the action of
\varphi\in\mathcal{G}
on
\lambda\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
is by
\varphi\cdot\lambda=\lambda+b(\varphi)
where
b(\varphi)(a,b)=\langle\beta,\alpha\cap\xi\rangle
, where
\alpha,\beta\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
are such that
c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha)=a
and
c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\beta)=b.

By (
9.10
), this can be rewritten as

b(\varphi)(a,b)=\langle\beta,\alpha\cap\xi\rangle=\operatorname{mult}_{0}((\alpha\cup\beta)\cap\xi).

Recall additionally that
\xi\in H_{4}(B)
is the unique class such that

j_{*}(\xi)=(\varphi\circ c_{1})_{*}([X_{1}])-(c_{1})_{*}([X_{1}])\in H_{4}(B,Y).

We apply this definition to
\varphi=\Xi(\mathbf{H}).

First, note that

\Xi(\mathbf{H})\cdot\lambda=\lambda+b(\Xi(\mathbf{H}))=\lambda+b(\widetilde{H}_{1}).

To determine the class
\xi
, recall that we proved in Lemma
9.13
that

(\widetilde{H}_{1}\circ c_{1})_{*}([X_{1},Y])=(c_{1})_{*}([X_{1},Y])+j_{*}(\mathbf{H}_{*}([Y\times S^{1}]))\in H_{4}(B,Y).

We can therefore take
\xi=\mathbf{H}_{*}([Y\times S^{1}])
in the definition of
b(\widetilde{H}_{1})
.
Thus, for
a,b\in H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
with
c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha)=a,c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\beta)=b
for
\alpha,\beta\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, the definition of
b(\widetilde{H}_{1})
and Proposition
9.15
imply that

\displaystyle b(\widetilde{H}_{1})(a,b)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{mult}_{0}((\alpha\cup\beta)\cap\mathbf{H}_{*}([Y\times S^{1}]))=\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}(\beta)(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha)\cap[Y])+\overline{\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}(\alpha)(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\beta)\cap[Y])}

\displaystyle=\phi\circ c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\beta)(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha)\cap[Y])+\overline{\phi\circ c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\alpha)(c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}(\beta)\cap[Y])}=\phi(b)(a\cap[Y])+\overline{\phi(a)(b\cap[Y])}

\displaystyle=q_{\mathbf{H}}(a,b)+\overline{q_{\mathbf{H}}(b,a)}=q_{\mathbf{H}}(a,b)+q_{\mathbf{H}}^{*}(a,b).

This concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

9.2.
Realisation

In order to prove Theorem
9.4
, it remains to show that for any sesquilinear form
q\in\Sesq(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
, there is a
\mathbf{H}\in\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1})(c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})
with
q=q_{\mathbf{H}}
.
Here, given
\phi\colon H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}
with
\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}=\phi\circ c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}
, recall from Construction
9.22
that

(
\star
)

q_{\mathbf{H}}(a,b):=\phi(b)(a\cap[Y]).

Plan 9.24
.

Fix
q\in\Sesq(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
.
In order to prove that there exists
\mathbf{H}\in\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1})(c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})
with
q=q_{\mathbf{H}}
, we first note that there is a
\phi\colon H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}
with
q(a,b)=\phi(b)(a\cap[Y])

for every
a,b\in H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).

Indeed the composition

\phi\colon H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\xrightarrow{q}H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}\xrightarrow{(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}^{-1})^{*}}H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}

is readily seen to satisfy

(9.25)

\phi(b)(a\cap[Y])=q(b)(a).

So we have to prove that for any
\phi
there is a
\mathbf{H}\in\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1})(c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})
with
\phi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{H}}=\phi\circ c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}.

Indeed, combining (
9.25
) with (

⋆

‣
9.2
), it would then follow that

q_{\mathbf{H}}(a,b)=\phi(b)(a\cap[Y])=q(a,b).

Summarising, we need to prove that for every homomorphism
\phi\colon H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
there is a
\mathbf{H}\in\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1})(c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})
with
\phi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{H}}=\phi\circ c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}
.

We begin with two algebraic lemmas.

Lemma 9.26
.

Assume that one of the two following hypotheses hold:

(1)

either
P
is a projective
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module,

(2)

or
\pi
is finite and
P
is stably isomorphic to the augmentation ideal
I
.

For every
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module
L
and every
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-linear map
f\colon P^{*}\to L^{*}
there is a unique
g\colon L\to P
with
g^{*}=f
.

Proof.

During this proof, given a
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module
V
, we write
\operatorname{ev}_{V}\colon V\to V^{**}
for
\operatorname{ev}_{V}(x)(f)=f(x).

A quick verification shows that
V^{*}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{ev}_{V^{*}}}V^{***}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{ev}_{V}^{*}}V^{*}
is the identity on
V^{*}
.

We begin with the projective case.
Since
P
is projective,
\operatorname{ev}_{P}\colon P\to P^{**}
is an isomorphism and we define
g:=\operatorname{ev}_{P}^{-1}\circ f^{*}\circ\operatorname{ev}_{L}
.
Since
\operatorname{ev}_{P}
is an isomorphism, we have
(\operatorname{ev}_{P}^{*})^{-1}=\operatorname{ev}_{P^{*}}
.
Thus taking the dual of
g
, we obtain

g^{*}=\operatorname{ev}_{L}^{*}\circ f^{**}\circ(\operatorname{ev}_{P}^{*})^{-1}=\operatorname{ev}_{L}^{*}\circ f^{**}\circ\operatorname{ev}_{P^{*}}=\operatorname{ev}_{L}^{*}\circ\operatorname{ev}_{L^{*}}\circ f=f.

It remains to show that
g
is uniquely determined by
g^{*}=f
.
Assume that
h^{*}=f
for some map
h\colon L\to P
. Then

\operatorname{ev}_{P}\circ h=h^{**}\circ\operatorname{ev}_{L}=f^{*}\circ\operatorname{ev}_{L}=g^{**}\circ\operatorname{ev}_{L}=\operatorname{ev}_{P}\circ g.

Since
\operatorname{ev}_{P}
is an isomorphism,
h=g
and thus
g
is uniquely determined by
g^{*}=f
as claimed.

Next, we prove the second assertion in the particular case where
P\cong I\oplus\mathbb{Z}[\pi]^{\oplus n-1}
.
Dualising the exact sequence
0\to P\xrightarrow{i}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]^{\oplus n}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\circ\operatorname{pr}_{1}}\mathbb{Z}\to 0
, we obtain the exact sequence

0\to\mathbb{Z}^{*}\xrightarrow{(\varepsilon\circ\operatorname{pr}_{1})^{*}}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi]^{\oplus n})^{*}\xrightarrow{i^{*}}P^{*}\to 0.

To obtain the rightmost zero, we used that
\pi
is finite to deduce
\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}^{1}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=H^{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0.

Consider the composition
f\circ i^{*}\colon(\mathbb{Z}[\pi]^{\oplus n})^{*}\to\penalty\ L^{*}
.
Since
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]^{\oplus n}
is projective, the first item yields a unique
g\colon L\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]^{\oplus n}
that satisfies
g^{*}=f\circ i^{*}
. It remains to show
\operatorname{im}(g)\subset P
or equivalently that
\varepsilon\circ\operatorname{pr}_{1}\circ g=0
.
Indeed this will imply
g^{*}\circ i^{*}=f\circ i^{*}
and thus, since
i^{*}
is surjective, that
g^{*}=f
.
By definition
g^{*}\circ(\varepsilon\circ\operatorname{pr}_{1})^{*}=f\circ i^{*}\circ(\varepsilon\circ\operatorname{pr}_{1})^{*}=0
.
Consider multiplication by the norm element
\mathcal{N}\in\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
as an element of
\mathbb{Z}^{*}
; here it is because
\pi
is finite that we can consider the norm element.
We deduce that

0=g^{*}\circ(\varepsilon\circ\operatorname{pr}_{1})^{*}(\mathcal{N})=\mathcal{N}\circ\varepsilon\circ\operatorname{pr}_{1}\circ g.

Since
\mathcal{N}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
is injective, it follows that
\varepsilon\circ\operatorname{pr}_{1}\circ g=0
as needed.
This concludes the proof of the second assertion in the case
P\cong I\oplus\mathbb{Z}[\pi]^{\oplus n-1}
.

We now claim that the lemma holds for a module
P
if it holds for
Q:=P\oplus\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
.
Consider

\begin{pmatrix}f&0\end{pmatrix}\colon Q^{*}\cong P^{*}\oplus\mathbb{Z}[\pi]^{*}\to L^{*}.

Since the lemma holds for
Q
by assumption, there exists a unique
g\colon L\to Q\cong P\oplus\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
such that
g^{*}=(f\ 0)
.
It remains to show
\operatorname{im}(g)\subset P
or equivalently
\operatorname{pr}_{2}\circ g=0
, where
\operatorname{pr}_{2}\colon Q\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
is the projection.
Again, this will imply that
(g^{*},0)=(f,0)
and thus
g^{*}=f
.
By definition we have
g^{*}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{2}^{*}=(f\ 0)^{*}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{2}^{*}=0
.
Hence
0=g^{*}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{2}^{*}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]})=\operatorname{pr}_{2}\circ g
, as claimed.

We now prove the second assertion.
If
P
is stably isomorphic to
I
, then there exist
n,k\geq 0
such that
P\oplus\mathbb{Z}[\pi]^{\oplus k}\cong I\oplus\mathbb{Z}[\pi]^{\oplus n-1}=:Q
. By the second case above, the lemma holds for
Q
and by repeatedly applying the claim, it holds for
P
.
This concludes the proof of the second assertion and therefore the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 9.27
.

Let
M
and
N
be left
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-modules and let
f\colon M\to N
be a left
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-linear map.
If
f^{*}\colon N^{*}\to M^{*}
is an isomorphism, then

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(f,A^{*})\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{left-}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(N,A^{*})\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{left-}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(M,A^{*})

is an isomorphism of abelian groups for every right
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module
A
.

If
A
is a
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-bimodule, then
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(f,A^{*})
is an isomorphism of right
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-modules.

Proof.

Using the tensor-hom adjunction, we obtain the commutative diagram of abelian groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{left-}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(N,A^{*})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{bi-}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(N\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}A,\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{right-}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(A,N^{*})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{left-}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(M,A^{*})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{bi-}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}A,\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{right-}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(A,M^{*}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(f,A^{*})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f\otimes\operatorname{id}_{A})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(A,f^{*})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

If follows that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(f,A^{*})
is an isomorphism as claimed.
∎

We apply these lemmas to the situation at hand.

Construction 9.28

(The map
f\colon H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
)
.

Fix
\phi\colon H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}
and consider the evaluation
\operatorname{ev}_{B}\colon H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}
.
Assume that

\operatorname{ev}_{B}^{*}\colon H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{**}\to H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}

is an isomorphism.
Lemma
9.27
applied with
f=\operatorname{ev}_{B}
and
A=H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
ensures that

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{ev}_{B},H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*})\colon\operatorname{Hom}(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*},H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*})\to\operatorname{Hom}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*})\penalty\

is an isomorphism.
Since
\phi\circ c_{1}|^{*}_{Y}
belongs to the target of this map, we deduce that there exists a map
g\colon H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}
making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ev}_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

If we additionally assume that
H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-projective or that
\pi
is finite and that
H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is stably the augmentation ideal, then Lemma
9.26
gives a unique

f\colon H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

whose dual satisfies
f^{*}=g.

We describe a condition that is sufficient for
\phi
to be realised by a homotopy
\mathbf{H}
.

Proposition 9.29
.

Assume the map
\operatorname{ev}^{*}\colon H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{**}\to H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}
is an isomorphism and

•

either the module
H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is projective,

•

or
\pi
is finite and
H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong I\oplus\mathbb{Z}[\pi]^{\oplus k}
for some
k\geq 0
.

If
\mathbf{H}\colon Y\times S^{1}\to B
extends
c_{1}|_{Y}\colon Y\to B
and satisfies

\textstyle{H_{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\oplus H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}i_{*}&\left(-\times[S^{1}]\right)\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{((c_{1}|_{Y})_{*},f)}
\textstyle{H_{2}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{H}_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),}

where
f
is the map from Construction
9.28
, then
\mathbf{H}
realises
\phi
, meaning that

\textstyle{H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}.}

Proof.

As explained in Construction
9.28
, the assumptions of the proposition ensure that
f
is defined.
During this proof, given two
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-modules
A
and
B
, we identify
\operatorname{Hom}(A\oplus B,\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
with the direct sum
\operatorname{Hom}(A,\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\oplus\operatorname{Hom}(B,\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).

Consider the following diagram in which the top and bottom squares are readily seen to commute:

(9.30)

\textstyle{H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{Y}}
\textstyle{\overline{\operatorname{Hom}(H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}}
\textstyle{H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\oplus H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{proj}_{2}}
\textstyle{\overline{\operatorname{Hom}(H_{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\oplus H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{proj}_{2}^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{Y\times S^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Kun}^{-1},\cong}
\textstyle{\overline{\operatorname{Hom}(H_{2}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}i_{*}&\left(-\times[S^{1}]\right)\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{B},\cong}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{H}^{*}}
\textstyle{\overline{\operatorname{Hom}(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathbf{H}_{*})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}

We claim that the middle square commutes, i.e. that
\operatorname{ev}=(i_{*},(-\times[S^{1}]))^{*}\circ\operatorname{ev}_{Y\times S^{1}}\circ\operatorname{Kun}.

Given
\varphi\in H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),\psi\in H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and
a\in H_{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),b\in H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, we apply successively, the definition of
\operatorname{Kun}
, the linearity (and then naturality) of the evaluation, and the definition of the cross product to obtain

\displaystyle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}i_{*}&(-\times[S^{1}])\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{*}\circ\operatorname{ev}_{Y\times S^{1}}\circ\operatorname{Kun}(\varphi,\psi)(a,b)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}i_{*}&(-\times[S^{1}])\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{*}\circ\operatorname{ev}_{Y\times S^{1}}\circ(p_{Y}^{*}(\varphi)+(p_{Y}^{*}(\psi)\cup p_{S^{1}}^{*}(1_{H^{1}(S^{1})})))(a,b)

\displaystyle=\langle p_{Y}^{*}(\varphi),i_{*}(a)\rangle+\langle p_{Y}^{*}(\psi)\cup p_{S^{1}}^{*}(1_{H^{1}(S^{1})})),b\times[S^{1}]\rangle=\langle\varphi,a\rangle+\langle\psi\times 1_{H^{1}(S^{1})},b\times[S^{1}]\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi,a\rangle+\langle\psi,b\rangle=\operatorname{ev}(\varphi,\psi)(a,b).

For the penultimate equality we use Lemma
A.6
and Remark
A.14
to obtain

\displaystyle\langle\psi\times 1_{H^{1}(S^{1})},b\times[S^{1}]\rangle

\displaystyle=\overline{\operatorname{mult}_{0}((\psi\times 1_{H^{1}(S^{1})})\cap(b\times[S^{1}]))}=\overline{\operatorname{mult}_{0}((\psi\cap b)\times(1_{H^{1}(S^{1})}\cap[S^{1}]))}

\displaystyle=\overline{\operatorname{mult}_{0}(\psi\cap b)}=\langle\psi,b\rangle.

We have now established the diagram above commutes.
Thus, as claimed,

\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}:=\operatorname{ev}_{Y}\circ\operatorname{proj}_{2}\circ\operatorname{Kun}^{-1}\circ\mathbf{H}^{*}=f^{*}\circ\operatorname{ev}_{B}=g\circ\operatorname{ev}_{B}=\phi\circ c_{1}|_{Y}^{*},

where the third and fourth equality follow from the definitions of
f
and
g
in
9.28
.
∎

Recall that we write the homology Künneth isomorphism as

\displaystyle\operatorname{Kun}\colon H_{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\oplus H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{2}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle(a,b)

\displaystyle\mapsto i_{*}(a)+b\times 1_{H_{1}(S^{1})}.

We can now carry out Plan
9.24
.

Proposition 9.31
.

Assume the map
\operatorname{ev}^{*}\colon H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{**}\to H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}
is an isomorphism and

•

either the module
H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is projective,

•

or
\pi
is finite and
H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong I\oplus\mathbb{Z}[\pi]^{\oplus k}
for some
k\geq 0
.

For every homomorphism
\phi\colon H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, there is a homotopy
\mathbf{H}\colon Y\times[0,1]\to B
extending
c_{1}|_{Y}\sqcup c_{1}|_{Y}\colon Y\times\{0,1\}\to B
, that is trivial on the
S^{1}
-factor, and such that
\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}=\phi\circ c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}.

In particular,

\mathbf{H}\in\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1})(c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}).

Proof.

The first two assumptions ensure that there is a map
f\colon H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
associated to
\phi
as in Construction
9.28
.
Consider the following diagram

\textstyle{H_{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\operatorname{id}\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{H_{2}(Y\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Kun}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{j_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\oplus H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&f\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).}
\textstyle{H_{2}(Y\times S^{1},Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}

The fact that
i
is injective and
j
is surjective follows because the inclusion
i\colon Y\to Y\times S^{1}
is split by the projection
\operatorname{pr}\colon Y\times S^{1}\to Y
.
Thus, since
(0\ f)\circ\operatorname{Kun}^{-1}
vanishes on
H_{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, there is a map
\psi\colon H_{2}(Y\times S^{1},Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
satisfying
\psi\circ j_{*}=(0\ f)\circ\operatorname{Kun}^{-1}
.
Apply Theorem
C.2
to
f_{0}:=c\circ\operatorname{pr}\colon Y\times S^{1}\to B
in order to obtain a map
\mathbf{H}\colon Y\times S^{1}\to B
that satisfies
H|_{Y}=c\circ\operatorname{pr}|_{Y}=c|_{Y}
and
\mathbf{H}_{*}=(c\circ\operatorname{pr})_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(Y\times S^{1})\to\pi_{1}(B)
as well as
\psi\circ j_{*}=(c\circ\operatorname{pr})_{*}-\mathbf{H}_{*}
.
It follows that

(0,-f)\circ\operatorname{Kun}^{-1}=\psi\circ j_{*}=(c\circ\operatorname{pr})_{*}-\mathbf{H}_{*}=((c_{1})_{*},0)\circ\operatorname{Kun}^{-1}-\mathbf{H}_{*}.

We thus obtain
\mathbf{H}_{*}=((c_{1})_{*},f)\circ\operatorname{Kun}^{-1}
.
Proposition
9.29
then ensures that
\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}=\phi\circ c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}
, as required.
Since
\mathbf{H}_{*}=(c\circ\operatorname{pr})_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(Y\times S^{1})\to\pi_{1}(B)
, we see that
\mathbf{H}
is trivial on the
S^{1}
-factor.
Thus
\mathbf{H}\in\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1})
and, since
\phi_{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}=\phi\circ c_{1}|_{Y}^{*}
, Proposition
9.21
gives
\mathbf{H}\in\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1})(c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}).

∎

We can now prove the main technical result of this section.

Theorem
9.4
.

For any homotopy
\mathbf{H}\in\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1})(c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})
, there is a corresponding sesquilinear form
q_{\mathbf{H}}\in\Sesq(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
such that the homotopy equivalence
\Xi(\mathbf{H})\colon B\to B
acts on the set
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
of hermitian forms as

\Xi(\mathbf{H})\cdot\lambda=\lambda+q_{\mathbf{H}}+q_{\mathbf{H}}^{*}\quad\quad\text{ for any }\lambda\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])).

Conversely,
if the map
\operatorname{ev}^{*}\colon H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{**}\to H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}
is an isomorphism and

•

either the module
H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is projective,

•

or
\pi
is finite and
H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong I\oplus\mathbb{Z}[\pi]^{\oplus k}
for some
k\geq 0
,

then for any sesquilinear form
q\in\Sesq(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
, there is a homotopy
\mathbf{H}\in\operatorname{Homotopy}(c_{1})(c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial})
with
q=q_{\mathbf{H}}.

Proof.

The sesquilinear form
q_{\mathbf{H}}
is constructed in Construction
9.22
, whereas the description of the action of
\Xi(\mathbf{H})
can be found in Proposition
9.23
.
The realisation of a sesquilinear form
q
by a homotopy
\mathbf{H}
follows by combining Plan
9.24
with Proposition
9.31
.
∎

We now prove Theorem
2.22
whose statement we recall for the reader’s convenience.

Theorem
2.22
.

Assume that
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
.
Let
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
be
4
-dimensional Poincaré pairs with fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X_{i})\cong\pi
, Postnikov
2
-type
B:=P_{2}(X_{1})
and
\pi_{1}(Y_{i})\to\pi_{1}(X_{i})
surjective for
i=0,1
, and let
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
be a degree one homotopy equivalence.
Assume that
\operatorname{ev}^{*}
is an isomorphism as in Definition
2.14
.

If there are
3
-connected maps
c_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B
and
c_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B
such that
c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h=c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}
as well as
c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
and
b(c_{0},c_{1})
is even hermitian, then

b(c_{0},c_{1})=0\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}.

In fact, there is a
c_{0}^{\prime}\simeq c_{0}
with
c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h=c_{0}^{\prime}|_{Y_{0}}
such that
b(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1})=0\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
.

Proof.

Since the
c_{i}
are
3
-connected,
\operatorname{ev}^{*}
is an isomorphism if and only if
\operatorname{ev}_{B}^{*}
is an isomorphism.
Recall also from Proposition
3.4
that
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
if and only if
\pi_{2}(X_{i})
is projective for
i=0,1
, which is in turn equivalent to
H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
being projective.
The assumptions of Theorem
9.4
are therefore satisfied.

Assume that
b(c_{0},c_{1})=q+q^{*}
for some sesquilinear form
q
.
Theorem
9.4
produces a homotopy
\mathbf{H}=(H_{t}\colon Y_{1}\to B)_{t\in[0,1]}
from
c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}
to itself with
q_{\mathbf{H}}=-q
.
Consider also the homotopy equivalence
\Xi(\mathbf{H})\colon B\to B
rel.
Y_{1}
obtained by applying the homotopy extension theorem (recall Construction
9.2
).
Write
g\colon B\to B
for the homotopy inverse of
\Xi(\mathbf{H})
and consider
c_{0}^{\prime}:=g\circ c_{0}
.
Since
\Xi(\mathbf{H})\in\mathcal{G}
, Propositions
5.9
and
9.23
show that

b(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1})=b(c_{0},c_{1})-b(g)=b(c_{0},c_{1})+b(\Xi(\mathbf{H}))=\Xi(\mathbf{H})\cdot b(c_{0},c_{1})=b(c_{0},c_{1})-(q+q^{*})=0.

Since
b(c_{0},c_{1})=b(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1})+b(g)=g\cdot b(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1})
, we deduce that
b(c_{0},c_{1})=0\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}
.
For the final sentence of the theorem, recall from Construction
9.2
that
\Xi(\mathbf{H})=\widetilde{H}_{1}
is rel.
Y_{1}
and is homotopic (though not rel.
Y_{1}
) to
\operatorname{id}_{B}
, whence
c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h=c_{0}^{\prime}|_{Y_{0}}
and
c_{0}^{\prime}\simeq c_{0}
.
This concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

10.
The secondary obstruction for weakly even forms

We prove Theorems
2.17
and
2.18
.
The first result follows promptly from Theorem
9.2
, whereas the second requires some preliminary lemmas.

Notation 10.1
.

Let
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
be
4
-dimensional Poincaré pairs with fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X_{j})\cong\pi
, Postnikov
2
-type
B:=P_{2}(X_{1})
and
\iota_{j}\colon\pi_{1}(Y_{j})\to\pi_{1}(X_{j})
surjective for
j=0,1
.
Fix a degree one homotopy equivalence
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
.

The first main result of this section follows promptly from Theorem
9.2
.

Theorem
2.17
.

Fix
\pi
with
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
.
Assume
\operatorname{ev}^{*}
is an isomorphism as in Definition
2.14
,
the equivariant intersection form
\lambda_{X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}}
is weakly even, and
H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong L\oplus T
with
L
free and
T^{*}=0
.
If there are
3
-connected maps
c_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B,c_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B
with
c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h=c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}
and
c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
, then

b(c_{0},c_{1})=0\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}.

In fact, there is a
c_{0}^{\prime}\simeq c_{0}
with
c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h=c_{0}^{\prime}|_{Y_{0}}
such that
b(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1})=0\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
.

Proof.

The obstruction
b(c_{0},c_{1})
arises by pulling back the equivariant intersection form of the
4
-dimensional Poincaré complex
X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}
, and modding out by a subset of the resulting radical.
Thus if
\lambda_{X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}}
is weakly even hermitian, then so is
b(c_{0},c_{1})
.
Since
H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong L\oplus T
with
L
free and
T^{*}=0
, Lemma
2.19
ensures that
b(c_{0},c_{1})
is even.
Since
b(c_{0},c_{1})
is even,
Theorem
9.2
ensures both that
b(c_{0},c_{1})=0\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}
and that there is a
c_{0}^{\prime}\simeq c_{0}
with
c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h=c_{0}^{\prime}|_{Y_{0}}
such that
b(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1})=0\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
.
∎

The remainder of this section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
2.18

Notation 10.2
.

Fix
3
-connected maps
c_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B,c_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B
such that
c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}=c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h
with
c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
so that
b(c_{0},c_{1})
is defined.
Elsewhere in this article (e.g. in Section
4
) we have been writing
c:=c_{0}\cup c_{1}\colon X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}\to B
.
We continue writing
c
for this map but, in this section alone, we write
c_{0}\cup c_{1}\colon X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}\to B\cup_{\operatorname{id}_{Y_{1}}}B

so that
c\colon X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}\to B
is obtained from
c_{0}\cup c_{1}
as
c=r\circ(c_{0}\cup c_{1})
where
r\colon B\cup_{\operatorname{id}_{Y_{1}}}B\to B
denotes the fold map.
Finally, we set
B\cup_{Y_{1}}B:=B\cup_{\operatorname{id}_{Y_{1}}}B
and
b_{U}:=b_{X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}}
.

Lemma 10.3
.

The following assertions hold:

•

The form
b_{U}
is weakly even if and only if the form
(c_{0}\cup c_{1})^{*}b_{U}
is weakly even.

•

The form
b(c_{0},c_{1})
is weakly even if and only if the form
r^{*}(c_{0}\cup c_{1})^{*}b_{U}
is weakly even.

Proof.

The first assertion is clear because
c_{0}\cup c_{1}
induces an isomorphism on second (co)homology.
We therefore focus on the second.
The form
b(c_{0},c_{1})
is isometric (via
(c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}^{*})^{-1}
) to a form obtained from
c^{*}b_{U}
by modding out by a subset of its radical.
It follows that
b(c_{0},c_{1})
is weakly even if and only if
c^{*}b_{U}=r^{*}(c_{0}\cup c_{1})^{*}b_{U}
is weakly even.
∎

Lemma 10.4
.

Consider the inclusion
i_{1}\colon B\to B\cup_{Y_{1}}B
of the first factor and the composition

\eta\colon H^{2}(B,Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\xleftarrow{\cong,\exc}H^{2}(B\cup_{Y_{1}}B,B)\xrightarrow{j_{B}^{*}}H^{2}(B\cup_{Y_{1}}B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).

The form
(c_{0}\cup c_{1})^{*}b_{U}
is weakly even if and only if
r^{*}(c_{0}\cup c_{1})^{*}b_{U}
and
\eta(c_{0}\cup c_{1})^{*}b_{U}
are weakly even.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that every
x\in H^{2}(B\cup_{Y_{1}}B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
can be written as
r^{*}(x_{1})+\eta(x_{2})
for
x_{1}\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and
x_{2}\in H^{2}(B,Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.
First, consider the long exact sequence of the pair
(B\cup_{Y_{1}}B,B)
.
The definition of
\eta
together with the fact that the fold map
r\colon B\cup_{Y_{1}}B\to B
splits the inclusion
i_{2}\colon B\to B\cup_{Y_{1}}B
then leads to the following commutative diagram in which the bottom row (and therefore the top row) is exact:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}(B,Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\scriptstyle{\exc}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H^{2}(B\cup_{Y_{1}}B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{2}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}(B\cup_{Y_{1}}B,B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{B}^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2}(B\cup_{Y_{1}}B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{2}^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Since
i_{2}^{*}r^{*}=\operatorname{id}_{H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}
, we have
x-r^{*}i_{2}^{*}(x)\in\ker(i_{2}^{*})=\operatorname{im}(\eta)
.
Setting
x_{1}:=i_{2}^{*}(x)\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, it follows that
x-r^{*}(x_{1})=\eta(x_{2})
for some
x_{2}\in H^{2}(B,Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, therefore proving the lemma.
∎

To prove the next lemma, it is convenient to consider the equivariant intersection form of
X_{i}
on cohomology instead of homology.
Since the notation
b_{X_{i}}
is already in use, we instead write

\displaystyle\operatorname{PD}_{X_{i}}^{*}\lambda_{X_{i}}\colon H^{2}(X_{i},Y_{i})\times H^{2}(X_{i},Y_{i})

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi],

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\lambda_{X_{i}}(\operatorname{PD}_{X_{i}}(x),\operatorname{PD}_{X_{i}}(y)).

Using
j_{i}\colon H_{2}(X_{i})\to H_{2}(X_{i},Y_{i})
to denote the inclusion induced map, observe that

\operatorname{PD}_{X_{i}}^{*}\lambda_{X_{i}}(x,y)=\langle\operatorname{PD}_{X_{i}}^{-1}\circ j_{i}\circ\operatorname{PD}_{X_{i}}(y),\operatorname{PD}_{X_{i}}(x)\rangle=\langle j_{i}^{*}(y),\operatorname{PD}_{X_{i}}(x)\rangle.

Lemma 10.5
.

The following equality holds:

\eta(c_{0}\cup c_{1})^{*}b_{U}=c_{0}^{*}\operatorname{PD}_{X_{0}}^{*}\lambda_{X_{0}}.

In particular, if
\lambda_{X_{0}}
is weakly even, then
\eta(c_{0}\cup c_{1})^{*}b_{U}
is weakly even.

Proof.

For the last
sentence, note that if
\lambda_{X_{0}}
is weakly even, then so is
c_{0}^{*}\operatorname{PD}_{X_{0}}^{*}\lambda_{X_{0}}
.
We therefore focus on proving that
\eta(c_{0}\cup c_{1})^{*}b_{U}=c_{0}^{*}\operatorname{PD}_{X_{0}}^{*}\lambda_{X_{0}}.

Recall that for
x,y\in H^{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, we have
\operatorname{PD}_{X_{0}}^{*}\lambda_{X_{0}}(x,y)=\langle j_{0}^{*}(y),\operatorname{PD}_{X_{0}}(x)\rangle
.

For
a,b\in H^{2}(B,Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, it therefore follows that

(10.6)

c_{0}^{*}\operatorname{PD}_{X_{0}}^{*}\lambda_{X_{0}}=\langle j_{0}^{*}c_{0}^{*}(b),\operatorname{PD}_{X_{0}}(c_{0}^{*}(a))\rangle.

The remainder of the proof consists of calculating
\eta(c_{0}\cup c_{1})^{*}b_{U}(a,b)
and verifying that we obtain the same outcome.
First, we note that

\displaystyle\eta(c_{0}\cup c_{1})^{*}b_{U}(a,b)

\displaystyle=b_{U}((c_{0}\cup c_{1})^{*}\circ\eta(b),(c_{0}\cup c_{1})^{*}\circ\eta(a))

(10.7)

\displaystyle=\langle(c_{0}\cup c_{1})^{*}\circ\eta(b),\operatorname{PD}_{X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}}\circ(c_{0}\cup c_{1})^{*}\circ\eta(a)\rangle.

The main intermediate step consists of the following claim.

Claim
.

Writing
i^{X_{0}\to X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}}\colon X_{0}\to X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}
for the inclusion, the following equality holds:

\operatorname{PD}_{X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}}\circ(c_{0}\cup c_{1})^{*}\circ\eta=i_{*}^{X_{0}\to X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}}\circ\operatorname{PD}_{X_{0}}\circ c_{0}^{*}\colon H^{2}(B,Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1},\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).

Proof.

The equality in the statement is equivalent to the outermost routes of the following diagram (in which
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-coefficient are understood) agreeing:

\textstyle{H^{2}(B,Y_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{0}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{H^{2}(X_{0},Y_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{PD}_{X_{0}}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{X_{0}\to X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}}_{*}}
