\textstyle{H^{2}(B\cup_{Y_{1}}B,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(c_{0}\cup c_{1})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\exc}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{j_{B}^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2}(X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1},X_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{incl}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\exc}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H^{2}(X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{PD}_{X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}).}
\textstyle{H^{2}(B\cup_{Y_{1}}B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(c_{0}\cup c_{1})^{*}}

We verify that this diagram does indeed commute.
The square and triangle clearly commute, and
\eta=j_{B}^{*}\circ\exc
by definition.
To see that the pentagon commutes, rewrite it as

\textstyle{H^{2}(X_{0},Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\cap[X_{0}]}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{X_{0}\to X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}}_{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2}(X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1},X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exc,\cong}
\scriptstyle{-\cap\operatorname{incl}_{*}([X_{0}])}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{incl}^{*}}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}
\textstyle{H^{2}(X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\cap[X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}]}
\textstyle{H_{2}(X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}

and use the naturality of relative cap product as e.g. in
[
tD08
, 18.1.1 (1)]
.
More precisely, in the notation from this reference, for the top square we use naturality with respect to the map
(X_{0},Y_{0},\emptyset)\to(X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1},X_{0},\emptyset)
(with
u=[X_{0}]\in H_{4}(X_{0},Y_{0})
) whereas for the bottom square it is with respect to
(X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1},\emptyset,\emptyset)\to(X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1},X_{0},\emptyset)
(with
u=[X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}]\in H_{4}(X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1})
).
This concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

Using the claim, we can rewrite (
10
) as

\eta(c_{0}\cup c_{1})^{*}b_{U}(a,b)=\langle(c_{0}\cup c_{1})^{*}\circ\eta(b),i_{*}^{X_{0}\to X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}}\circ\operatorname{PD}_{X_{0}}\circ c_{0}^{*}(a)\rangle.

Using the naturality of evaluation and the equality
(c_{0}\cup c_{1})\circ i^{X_{0}\to X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}}=i^{B\to B\cup_{Y_{1}}B}\circ c_{0}
, this equality can in turn be recast as

\eta(c_{0}\cup c_{1})^{*}b_{U}(a,b)=\langle c_{0}^{*}\circ i_{B\to B\cup_{Y_{1}}B}^{*}\circ\eta(b),\operatorname{PD}_{X_{0}}\circ c_{0}^{*}(a)\rangle.

This expression is seen to equal (
10.6
) by verifying that
c_{0}^{*}\circ i_{B\to B\cup_{Y_{1}}B}^{*}\circ\eta=j_{0}^{*}\circ c_{0}^{*}
; indeed all the maps besides
c_{0}^{*}
are inclusion induced.
∎

Proposition 10.8
.

Assume that the hermitian form
\lambda_{X_{0}}\cong\lambda_{X_{1}}
is weakly even and that the first obstruction vanishes:
c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
.
The following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

the hermitian form
b(c_{0},c_{1})
is weakly even.

(2)

the hermitian form
b_{U}
is weakly even.

Proof.

If
\lambda_{X_{0}}\cong\lambda_{X_{1}}
is weakly even, then Lemma
10.5
ensures that
\eta(c_{0}\cup c_{1})^{*}b_{U}
is weakly even.
The combination of the first item of Lemma
10.3
and Lemma
10.4
then shows that
b_{U}
is weakly even if and only if
r^{*}(c_{0}\cup c_{1})^{*}b_{U}
is weakly even.
The second item of Lemma
10.3
shows that this is equivalent to
b(c_{0},c_{1})
being weakly even.
∎

Finally we prove the main result of this section, namely Theorem
2.18
.

Theorem
2.18
.

Assume that the hermitian form
\lambda_{X_{0}}\cong\lambda_{X_{1}}
is weakly even.
If the secondary obstruction
b(c_{0},c_{1})
vanishes in
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}
, then
b_{U}
is weakly even.

Proof.

We claim that if
\lambda_{X_{1}}
is weakly even, then so is
b(\varphi)
for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{G}
.
Recall that
b(\varphi)
is obtained by running the same definition as for
b(c_{0},c_{1})
but with
(X_{1},\varphi\circ c_{1})
in place of
(X_{0},c_{0}).\penalty\

Repeat the proofs of Lemmas
10.3
,
10.4
and
10.5
but replacing all instances of
U:=X_{0}\cup_{h}X_{1}
by
X_{1}\cup_{Y_{1}}X_{1}:=X_{1}\cup_{\operatorname{id}_{Y_{1}}}X_{1}
.
Since we assumed that
\lambda_{X_{1}}
is weakly even, the outcome is an analogue of Proposition
10.8
:
b(\varphi)
is weakly even if and only if
b_{X_{1}\cup_{Y_{1}}X_{1}}
is weakly even.
In particular,
b(\varphi)
being weakly even is independent of
\varphi
and so, the claim reduces to proving that
b(\operatorname{id}_{B})
is weakly even.
In this case, we can take
\xi=0
in Construction
5.6
, from which it follows that
b(\operatorname{id}_{B})=0
which is indeed weakly even.
This concludes the proof of the claim.

We conclude the proof of the theorem.
If the pairing
b(c_{0},c_{1})
vanishes in
\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}
, then
b(c_{0},c_{1})=b(\varphi)
for some
\varphi\in\mathcal{G}
, and the claim ensures then ensures that this pairing is weakly even.
Proposition
10.8
implies that
b_{U}
is weakly even.
∎

11.
The secondary obstruction for odd forms

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
2.15
.
We recall the relevant notation, state the main technical result needed in the proof, explain how Theorem
2.15
follows and then prove the technical step.

Notation 11.1
.

Recall from the end of Section
2.6
that given a

3
-dimensional Poincaré complex
Y
and an epimorphism
\pi_{1}(Y)\twoheadrightarrow\pi
, we set

I^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]):=\operatorname{im}(H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\xrightarrow{\operatorname{ev}}\operatorname{Hom}(H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to\operatorname{Hom}(H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),\mathbb{Z}_{2})),

where the second map is induced by augmentation modulo
2
.
Given
x\in\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
, write
(x)_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
for the coefficient of the neutral element and consider the group homomorphism

\displaystyle\widehat{}\ \colon\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))

\displaystyle\to I^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\mapsto(x\mapsto b(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}^{-1}(x),\operatorname{PD}_{Y}^{-1}(x))_{1}\mod 2).

When
\pi
has no nontrivial elements of order
2
, the kernel of this map consists of weakly even forms on
H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
(Lemma
2.20
) or equivalently (by Lemma
2.19
), if
H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong L\oplus T
with
L
free and
T^{*}=0
, of even forms.

Given a
4
-dimensional Poincaré pair
(X,Y)
with
\pi_{1}(Y)\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{1}(X)=:\pi
surjective, we associate to a homomorphism
\phi\colon H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
the hermitian form

\displaystyle q_{\phi}\colon H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\lambda_{X}(\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x)),\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(y))).

The main technical result of this section is the following.

Theorem 11.2
.

Let
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
be
4
-dimensional Poincaré pairs with fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X_{i})\cong\pi
, Postnikov
2
-type
B:=P_{2}(X_{1})
and
\pi_{1}(Y_{i})\to\pi_{1}(X_{i})
surjective for
i=0,1
, and let
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
be a degree one homotopy equivalence.
Let
c_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B,c_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B
be

3
-connected maps such that
c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}=c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h
with
c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
.

Assume that one of the two following conditions hold:

•

either the map
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is injective, and
H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong L\oplus T
, with
L
a free
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module and
T^{*}=0
.

•

or
\pi
is finite and
H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong L\oplus T\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, with
L
a free
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module and
T^{*}=0
.
Here,
\mathbb{Z}
is endowed with the
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module structure induced by the trivial
\pi
-action.

For every map
\phi\colon H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, there is a
3
-connected map
c_{0}^{\prime}\colon X\to B
satisfying
c_{0}^{\prime}|_{Y_{0}}=c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h
as well as
(c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
, and

\widehat{b}(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1})=\widehat{b}(c_{0},c_{1})-\widehat{h^{*}q_{\phi}}.

We explain how this result implies Theorem
2.15
, whose statement we now recall.

Theorem
2.15
.

Assume
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
.
Let
(X_{1},Y_{1})
be a
4
-dimensional Poincaré pair with
\pi_{1}(X_{1})\cong\penalty\ \pi
, Postnikov
2
-type
B:=P_{2}(X_{1})
and
\pi_{1}(Y_{1})\to\pi_{1}(X_{1})
surjective, and let
c_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B
be a
3
-connected map.
Assume that
\operatorname{ev}^{*}
is an isomorphism and that
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is injective.
If
\lambda_{X_{1}}
is odd and
H_{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong L\oplus T
with
L
free and
T^{*}=0
, then

\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}=0.

In particular, given another
4
-dimensional Poincaré pair with
\pi_{1}(X_{0})\cong\pi
, Postnikov
2
-type
B
, and
\pi_{1}(Y_{0})\to\pi_{1}(X_{0})
surjective, and a
3
-connected map
c_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B
with
c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}=c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h
with
c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
, the secondary obstruction vanishes:

b(c_{0},c_{1})=0\in\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))/\mathcal{G}.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.24
, it is equivalent to show that
I^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])/\mathcal{G}=0
, i.e. that every element
f\in I^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
can be written as
f=\widehat{b}(\varphi)
for some
\varphi\in\mathcal{G}
.
Given
f\in I^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, define

\phi\colon H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

by picking a basis
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
for
L
, setting
\phi(h_{*}^{-1}(x_{i})):=0
if
f(x_{i})=0

and
\phi(h_{*}^{-1}(x_{i})):=y
if
f(x_{i})=1
.
This determines
\phi
: since
T^{*}=0
and
H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is projective,
\phi
necessarily vanishes on
T
.
By construction
\widehat{h^{*}q_{\phi}}=f.

Theorem
11.2
(applied with
X_{0}=X_{1}
and
c_{0}=c_{1}
)
gives a
3
-connected map
c_{1}^{\prime}\colon X_{1}\to B
with
c_{1}^{\prime}|_{Y_{1}}=c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h,(c_{1}^{\prime})^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
, and

\widehat{b}(c_{1}^{\prime},c_{1})=\widehat{b}(c_{1},c_{1})-\widehat{h^{*}q_{\phi}}=\widehat{h^{*}q_{\phi}}=f.

Since
c_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}(c_{1})
, Proposition
5.5
(again with
X_{0}=X_{1}
) implies that there is a homotopy equivalence
\varphi\in\mathcal{G}
with
c_{1}^{\prime}\simeq\varphi\circ c_{1}
.
Proposition
5.9
then implies that
b(c_{1}^{\prime},c_{1})=b(c_{1},c_{1})-b(\varphi)=-b(\varphi)
.
Thus
f=\widehat{b}(c_{1}^{\prime},c_{1})=\widehat{b}(\varphi)
with
\varphi\in\mathcal{G}
, as required.
∎

The remainder of this section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
11.2
.

11.1.
Changing compatible triples

The first step in the proof of Theorem
11.2
consists of showing how a given compatible triple can be modified using a homomorphism
\phi\colon H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.
This is the content of Proposition
11.5
below.

Notation 11.3
.

Let
(X,Y)
be a
4
-dimensional Poincaré pair with
\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(X)=:\pi
surjective.
Given
\phi\colon H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, define
\psi\colon H_{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
as the composition

\displaystyle\psi\colon H_{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\xleftarrow{\operatorname{PD}_{X},\cong}H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\xrightarrow{\operatorname{ev}_{X}}H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}\xrightarrow{\phi^{*}}H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}

\displaystyle\xleftarrow{\operatorname{ev}_{Y},\cong}H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])/H^{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\xrightarrow{\delta}H^{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\operatorname{PD}_{X},\cong}H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).

Here,
\delta
is the connecting homomorphism for the pair
(X,Y)
, and to obtain the penultimate map, we use that
H^{1}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong H^{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.
Finally we write
i\colon Y\to X
and
j\colon(X,\emptyset)\to(X,Y)
for the inclusions.

The next lemma describes how, starting from the compatible triple
(\operatorname{id}_{H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])},\operatorname{id}_{H_{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])},\operatorname{id}_{Y})
, the maps
\phi
and
\psi
lead to a new compatible triple.

Lemma 11.4
.

Let
(X,Y)
be a
4
-dimensional Poincaré pair with
\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(X)=:\pi
surjective.
The following is a compatible triple:

(F,G,\operatorname{id}_{Y_{0}}):=(\operatorname{id}_{H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}-\psi\circ j_{*},\operatorname{id}_{H_{2}(X_{0},Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}+j_{*}\circ\phi\circ\partial,\operatorname{id}_{Y_{0}}).

Proof.

Using
\partial\colon H_{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
to denote the connecting homomorphism, we need to show that
i_{*}\circ F=i_{*},\partial\circ G=\partial
and
G\circ j_{*}=j_{*}\circ F
.
By definition of
F
and
G
, this amounts to proving that
\psi\circ j_{*}\circ i_{*}=0,\partial\circ j_{*}\circ\phi\circ\partial=0
and
j_{*}\circ\phi\circ\partial\circ j_{*}=j_{*}\circ\psi\circ\circ j_{*}
.
The first two equalities are clear because exactness guarantees that
j_{*}\circ i_{*}=0
and
\partial\circ j_{*}=0
.
For the third, observe that both expressions are zero, one because of
\partial\circ j_{*}=0
and second because
j\circ\psi
contains the expression
j\circ\operatorname{PD}_{X}\circ\delta=\operatorname{PD}_{X}\circ j^{*}\circ\delta=0.

For
(F,G,\operatorname{id}_{Y})
to be a compatible triple, it remains to verify that
\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(F(x),G(y))=\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(x,y)
for every
x\in H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and every
y\in H_{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).

In other words, we have to show that

\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(x,y)=\lambda_{X}^{\partial}((\operatorname{id}_{H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}-\psi\circ j_{*}(x)),(\operatorname{id}_{H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}+j_{*}\circ\phi\circ\partial)(y)).

We first establish
\lambda_{X}(\psi\circ j_{*}(x),j_{*}\circ\phi\circ\partial(y))=0
and then
\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(\psi\circ j_{*}(x),y)-\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(x,j_{*}\circ\phi\circ\partial(y))=0
.

We assert that
\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(\psi\circ j_{*}(x),j_{*}\circ\phi\circ\partial(y))=0
. Observe that the image of
\psi
is contained in
\operatorname{im}(i\colon H_{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
(to see this, use
\operatorname{PD}_{X}\circ\delta=i\circ\operatorname{PD}_{Y}
).
This observation ensures that
\psi\circ j_{*}(x)
pairs trivially under
\lambda_{X}^{\partial}
with every element of
H_{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.
The assertion follows immediately.

Next we claim that
\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(\psi\circ j_{*}(x),y)=\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(x,j_{*}\circ\phi\circ\partial(y))
for all
x\in H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),y\in H_{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.
Using Lemma
11.4
, we hence have to show that
\langle\operatorname{PD}_{X}^{-1}(\psi\circ j_{*}(x)),y\rangle=\langle\operatorname{PD}_{X}^{-1}(x),j_{*}\circ\phi\circ\partial(y)\rangle
.
This is a direct calculation which, besides the third equality, follows from the definitions:

\displaystyle\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(\psi\circ j_{*}(x),y)

\displaystyle=\langle\operatorname{PD}_{X}^{-1}(\psi\circ j_{*}(x)),y\rangle=\operatorname{ev}_{X}(\operatorname{PD}_{X}^{-1}(\psi\circ j_{*}(x)))(y)=\phi^{*}(\operatorname{PD}_{X}^{-1}(j_{*}(x)))(\partial(y))

\displaystyle=\langle\operatorname{PD}_{X}^{-1}(j_{*}(x)),\phi\circ\partial(y)\rangle=\langle j^{*}\circ\operatorname{PD}_{X}^{-1}(x),\phi\circ\partial(y)\rangle=\langle\operatorname{PD}_{X}^{-1}(x),j_{*}\circ\phi\circ\partial(y)\rangle

\displaystyle=\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(x,j_{*}\circ\phi\circ\partial(y)).

To justify the third equality, note that
\psi
involves
\operatorname{PD}_{X}
in the last step of its definition and that the following square commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])/H^{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ev}_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ev}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial^{*}}

This concludes the proof of the claim and thus the proof that
(F,G,\operatorname{id}_{Y})
is a compatible triple.
∎

The next proposition shows how
\phi
and
\psi
can be used to change a given compatible triple.

Proposition 11.5
.

Let
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
be
4
-dimensional Poincaré pairs with fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X_{i})\cong\pi
, and
\pi_{1}(Y_{i})\to\pi_{1}(X_{i})
surjective for
i=0,1
, and let
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
be a degree one homotopy equivalence. Given a compatible triple
(F,G,h)
and
\phi\colon H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, the following is again a compatible triple

(F-F\circ\psi\circ j_{*},G+G\circ j_{*}\circ\phi\circ\partial,h).

Proof.

Given two compatible triples
(F_{1},G_{1},h_{1})
and
(F_{2},G_{2},h_{2})
, the componentwise composition
(F_{1}\circ F_{2},G_{1}\circ G_{2},h_{1}\circ h_{2})
is again a compatible triple.
Since Lemma
11.4
ensures that the triple
(\operatorname{id}_{H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}-\psi\circ j_{*},\operatorname{id}_{H_{2}(X_{0},Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}+j_{*}\circ\phi\circ\partial,\operatorname{id}_{Y_{0}})
is compatible, it follows that

(F-F\circ\psi\circ j_{*},G+G\circ j_{*}\circ\phi\circ\partial,h)=(F,G,h)\circ(\operatorname{id}_{H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}-\psi\circ j_{*},\operatorname{id}_{H_{2}(X_{0},Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}+j_{*}\circ\phi\circ\partial,\operatorname{id}_{Y_{0}})

is again a compatible triple.
∎

11.2.
Proof of Theorem
11.2

After some preliminary lemmas, we prove Theorem
11.2
.

Notation 11.6
.

Fix a
4
-dimensional Poincaré pair
(X,Y)
with
\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(X):=\pi
surjective and Postnikov
2
-type
B:=P_{2}(X)
.
Additionally, fix
\phi\colon H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and consider the associated
\psi\colon H_{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
(recall Notation
11.3
).
Let
c_{0}^{\prime},c_{0}\colon X\to B
be
3
-connected maps such that
c|_{Y}:=c_{0}|_{Y}=c_{0}^{\prime}|_{Y}
and assume that
c|_{Y}
is an inclusion. Furthermore, assume that
(c_{0},c_{0}^{\prime})
induces the compatible triple

(F,G,\operatorname{id}_{Y}):=(\operatorname{id}_{H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}-\psi\circ j_{*},\operatorname{id}_{H_{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}+j_{*}\circ\phi\circ\partial,\operatorname{id}_{Y}).

Proposition
B.3
ensures that
(c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}b_{X}^{\partial}=c_{0}^{*}b_{X}^{\partial}

so that the secondary obstruction
b(c_{0},c_{0}^{\prime})
is defined.
Here, recall that we write
j\colon(X,\emptyset)\to(X,Y)
for the inclusion induced map.

Lemma 11.7
.

For
x\in H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and
y\in H^{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, we have

b_{X}^{\partial}(y,(c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x})-c_{0}^{*}(\widehat{x}))=\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(\operatorname{PD}_{X}(y),j_{*}\circ\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x)))

where
\widehat{x}\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is a preimage of
x\in H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
under
H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\xrightarrow{c|_{Y}^{*}}H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.

Proof.

The equality
(c_{0}^{\prime})_{*}^{-1}(c_{0})_{*}=\operatorname{id}_{H_{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}+j_{*}\circ\phi\circ\partial\colon H_{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
implies that

(11.8)

(c_{0})_{*}=(c_{0}^{\prime})_{*}+(c_{0}^{\prime})_{*}\circ j_{*}\circ\phi\circ\partial.

We begin with the following intermediate calculation, where the third equality uses (
11.8
) and the fifth equality uses that
(c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}b_{X}^{\partial}=c_{0}^{*}b_{X}^{\partial}
as well as
\partial(\operatorname{PD}_{X}\circ c_{0}^{*}(\widehat{x}))=\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(c|_{Y}^{*}\widehat{x})=\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x)
:

\displaystyle\langle y,c_{0}^{*}(\widehat{x})\cap[X]\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle(c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}((c_{0}^{\prime})^{*})^{-1}(y),c_{0}^{*}(\widehat{x})\cap[X]\rangle=\langle((c_{0}^{\prime})^{*})^{-1}(y),(c_{0}^{\prime})_{*}(c_{0}^{*}(\widehat{x})\cap[X])\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle((c_{0}^{\prime})^{*})^{-1}(y),(c_{0})_{*}(c_{0}^{*}(\widehat{x})\cap[X])\rangle-\langle((c_{0}^{\prime})^{*})^{-1}(y),(c_{0}^{\prime})_{*}\circ j_{*}\circ\phi\circ\partial(c_{0}^{*}(\widehat{x})\cap[X])\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle((c_{0}^{\prime})^{*})^{-1}(y),\widehat{x}\cap(c_{0})_{*}[X]\rangle-\langle((c_{0}^{\prime})^{*})^{-1}(y),(c_{0}^{\prime})_{*}\circ j_{*}\circ\phi\circ\partial(c_{0}^{*}(\widehat{x})\cap[X])\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle((c_{0}^{\prime})^{*})^{-1}(y),\widehat{x}\cap(c_{0}^{\prime})_{*}[X]\rangle-\langle((c_{0}^{\prime})^{*})^{-1}(y),(c_{0}^{\prime})_{*}\circ j_{*}\circ\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x))\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle y,(c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x})\cap[X]\rangle-\langle y,j_{*}\circ\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x))\rangle.

Applying Lemma
B.1
and the conclusion of this calculation yields:

\displaystyle b_{X}^{\partial}(y,(c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x})-c_{0}^{*}(\widehat{x}))

\displaystyle=\overline{\langle y,((c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x})-c_{0}^{*}(\widehat{x}))\cap[X]\rangle}=\overline{\langle y,j_{*}\circ\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x))\rangle}

\displaystyle=b_{X}^{\partial}(y,\operatorname{PD}^{-1}_{X}\circ j_{*}\circ\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x)))=\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(\operatorname{PD}_{X}(y),j_{*}\circ\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x))).

This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Continuing with the notation from Lemma
11.7
, since
(c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x}),c_{0}^{*}(\widehat{x})\in H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
both map to
x\in H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, the difference
(c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x})-c_{0}^{*}(\widehat{x})
lies in
\operatorname{im}(j^{*}\colon H^{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
.
This observation is used implicitly in the statement of the next lemma.

Lemma 11.9
.

Let
x\in H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, and assume
\widehat{x}\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
satisfies
c|_{Y}^{*}(\widehat{x})=x
.
For an element
y\in H^{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
with
j^{*}(y)=(c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x})-c_{0}^{*}(\widehat{x})\in H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, the following holds:

b_{X}^{\partial}(y,(c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x})-c_{0}^{*}(\widehat{x}))=\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x)),j_{*}\circ\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x))).

Proof.

The equality
\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(a,j_{*}(b))=\lambda_{X}(a,b)
and
\lambda_{X}
being hermitian imply
\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(a,j_{*}(b))=\overline{\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(b,j_{*}(a))}
.
Using Lemma
11.7
, the equality
\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(a,j_{*}(b))=\overline{\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(b,j_{*}(a))}
and
j^{*}(y)=(c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x})-c_{0}^{*}(\widehat{x})
,
we obtain

\displaystyle b_{X}^{\partial}(y,(c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x})-c_{0}^{*}(\widehat{x}))

\displaystyle=\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(\operatorname{PD}_{X}(y),j_{*}\circ\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x)))=\overline{\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x)),j_{*}\operatorname{PD}_{X}(y))}

\displaystyle=\overline{\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x)),\operatorname{PD}_{X}(j^{*}(y)))}=\overline{b_{X}^{\partial}(\operatorname{PD}_{X}^{-1}\circ\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x)),j^{*}(y))}

\displaystyle=\overline{b_{X}^{\partial}(\operatorname{PD}_{X}^{-1}\circ\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x)),(c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x})-c_{0}^{*}(\widehat{x}))}=\overline{\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x)),j_{*}\circ\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x)))}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x)),j_{*}\circ\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x))).

This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

In order to make sense of the next result, it is helpful to recall from Notation
11.1
that the form
q_{\phi}\colon H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
is defined as
q_{\phi}(x,y)=\lambda_{X}(\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x)),\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(y))).

Proposition 11.10
.

The following equalities hold in
I^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
:

\widehat{b}(c_{0},c_{0}^{\prime})=\widehat{b}(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{0}^{\prime})+\widehat{q}_{\phi}=\widehat{q}_{\phi}.

Proof.

During this proof, we write
X\cup X:=X\cup_{\operatorname{id}_{Y}}\scalebox{0.75}[1.0]{$-$}X
.
For
x\in H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, the definition of the secondary obstruction
yields

(11.11)

\displaystyle b(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{0}^{\prime})(x,x)

\displaystyle=b_{X\cup X}((c_{0}^{\prime}\cup c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x}),(c_{0}^{\prime}\cup c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x})),

\displaystyle b(c_{0},c_{0}^{\prime})(x,x)

\displaystyle=b_{X\cup X}((c_{0}\cup c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x}),(c_{0}\cup c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x})),

where
\widehat{x}\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is a preimage of
x
under
H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\xrightarrow{c|_{Y}^{*}}H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.

The remainder of the proof is devoted to calculating
q_{\phi}(x,x).

First, some notation.
Write

\displaystyle j_{X\cup X}\colon(X\cup X,\emptyset)\to(X\cup X,Y)

\displaystyle i_{k}\colon(X,Y)\to(X\cup X,Y)\quad\text{for }k=0,1.

The subscript in
j_{X\cup X}
is used to distinguish this map from
j\colon(X,\emptyset)\to(X,Y)
.

Thinking of
j_{X\cup X}
as a map
(X\cup X,\emptyset,\emptyset)\to(X\cup X,\emptyset,Y)
, note that
j_{X\cup X}^{*}=\operatorname{id}_{H^{2}(X\cup X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}
.
Thus for
\alpha\in H^{2}(X\cup X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and
\sigma\in H_{4}(X\cup X)
the relation between cap and cups (as e.g. in
[
tD08
, 18.1.1 (1)]
) ensures that

(11.12)

(j_{X\cup X})_{*}(\alpha\cap\sigma)=\alpha\cap(j_{X\cup X})_{*}(\sigma).

Choose
z\in H^{2}(X\cup X;Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
so that
j_{X\cup X}^{*}(z)=(c_{0}^{\prime}\cup c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x})-(c_{0}\cup c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x})\in H^{2}(X\cup X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.
Such a preimage exists because the summands map to
x\in H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).

Claim
.

The following equality holds:

q_{\phi}(x,x)=b_{X\cup X}(j_{X\cup X}^{*}(z),j_{X\cup X}^{*}(z)).

Proof.

Applying the equation in (
11.12
) to
\alpha=j_{X\cup X}^{*}(z)\in H^{2}(X\cup X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),
yields

(j_{X\cup X})_{*}(j_{X\cup X}^{*}(z)\cap[X\cup X])=j_{X\cup X}^{*}(z)\cap(j_{X\cup X})_{*}([X\cup X]).

Using this equality as well as

(j_{X\cup X})_{*}([X\cup X])=(i_{0})_{*}([X])-(i_{1})_{*}([X])\in H_{4}(X\cup X,Y)\cong H_{4}(X,Y)\oplus H_{4}(X,Y),

we then obtain

\displaystyle b_{X\cup X}(j_{X\cup X}^{*}(z),j_{X\cup X}^{*}(z))

\displaystyle=\langle z,(j_{X\cup X})_{*}(j_{X\cup X}^{*}(z)\cap[X\cup X])\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle z,j_{X\cup X}^{*}(z)\cap(j_{X\cup X})_{*}([X\cup X])\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle z,j_{X\cup X}^{*}(z)\cap(i_{0})_{*}([X])\rangle-\langle z,j_{X\cup X}^{*}(z)\cap(i_{1})_{*}([X])\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle z,(i_{0})_{*}(i_{0}^{*}\circ j_{X\cup X}^{*}(z)\cap[X])\rangle-\langle z,(i_{1})_{*}(i_{1}^{*}\circ j_{X\cup X}^{*}(z)\cap[X])\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle z,(i_{0})_{*}(j^{*}\circ i_{0}^{*}(z)\cap[X])\rangle-\langle z,(i_{1})_{*}(j^{*}\circ i_{1}^{*}(z)\cap[X])\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle i_{0}^{*}(z),j^{*}\circ i_{0}^{*}(z)\cap[X]\rangle-\langle i_{1}^{*}(z),j^{*}\circ i_{1}^{*}(z)\cap[X]\rangle.

In the fifth equality, the relation
i_{0}^{*}\circ j_{X\cup X}^{*}=j^{*}\circ i_{0}^{*}
(where
i_{0}^{*}
is relative on one side and absolute on the other) holds because
i_{0}\circ j,j_{X\cup X}\circ i_{0}\colon(X,\emptyset)\to(X\cup X,Y)
are both given by inclusion of the first factor.
The same reasoning is also used with
i_{1}
in place of
i_{0}
.

The definition of
z\in H^{2}(X\cup X;Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
ensures that
i_{1}^{*}(z)=0\in H^{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and that
i_{0}^{*}(z)\in H^{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
satisfies
j^{*}(i_{0}^{*}(z))=(c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x})-c_{0}^{*}(\widehat{x})\in H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.
These observations together with Lemma
B.1
yield

\displaystyle b_{X\cup X}(j_{X\cup X}^{*}(z),j_{X\cup X}^{*}(z))

\displaystyle=\langle i_{0}^{*}(z),j^{*}i_{0}^{*}(z)\cap[X]\rangle=\langle i_{0}^{*}(z),((c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x})-c_{0}^{*}(\widehat{x}))\cap[X]\rangle

\displaystyle=\overline{b_{X}^{\partial}(i_{0}^{*}(z),(c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x})-c_{0}^{*}(\widehat{x}))}.

Combining this equality with Lemma
11.9
(applied with
y=i_{0}^{*}(z)
) and the definition of
q_{\phi}
, we get

\displaystyle b_{X\cup X}(j_{X\cup X}^{*}(z),j_{X\cup X}^{*}(z))

\displaystyle=\overline{b_{X}^{\partial}(i_{0}^{*}(z),(c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x})-c_{0}^{*}(\widehat{x}))}=\overline{\lambda^{\partial}_{X}(\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x)),j_{*}\circ\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x)))}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{\partial}_{X}(\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x)),j_{*}\circ\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x)))=\lambda_{X}(\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x)),\phi(\operatorname{PD}_{Y}(x)))

\displaystyle=q_{\phi}(x,x).

This concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

We can now conclude the proof of the proposition.
The claim, together with the equality
j_{X\cup X}^{*}(z)=(c_{0}^{\prime}\cup c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x})-(c_{0}\cup c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x})
(by definition of
z
) and (
11.11
), yields

\displaystyle q_{\phi}(x,x)

\displaystyle=b_{X\cup X}(j_{X\cup X}^{*}(z),j_{X\cup X}^{*}(z))

\displaystyle=b_{X\cup X}((c_{0}^{\prime}\cup c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x})-(c_{0}\cup c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x}),(c_{0}^{\prime}\cup c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x})-(c_{0}\cup c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x}))

\displaystyle=b(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{0}^{\prime})(x,x)+b(c_{0},c_{0}^{\prime})(x,x)-b_{X\cup X}((c_{0}^{\prime}\cup c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x}),(c_{0}\cup c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x}))-b_{X\cup X}((c_{0}\cup c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x}),(c_{0}^{\prime}\cup c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x}))

\displaystyle=b(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{0}^{\prime})(x,x)+b(c_{0},c_{0}^{\prime})(x,x)-b_{X\cup X}((c_{0}^{\prime}\cup c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x}),(c_{0}\cup c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x}))-\overline{b_{X\cup X}((c_{0}^{\prime}\cup c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x}),(c_{0}\cup c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}(\widehat{x}))}.

The equality
\widehat{b}(c_{0},c_{0}^{\prime})=\widehat{b}(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{0}^{\prime})+\widehat{q}_{\phi}
follows from the definition of
\ \widehat{}\
.
Finally,
b(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{0}^{\prime})\equiv 0
, by definition of the secondary obstruction, whence the equalities
\widehat{b}(c_{0},c_{0}^{\prime})=\widehat{b}(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{0}^{\prime})+\widehat{q}_{\phi}=\widehat{q}_{\phi}.

This concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

We can now prove Theorem
11.2
.
Recall that under a certain number of assumptions, this result states that for every map
\phi\colon H_{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, there is a
3
-connected map
c_{0}^{\prime}\colon X\to B
satisfying
c_{0}^{\prime}|_{Y_{0}}=c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}\circ h
as well as
(c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
, and
\widehat{b}(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1})=\widehat{b}(c_{0},c_{1})-\widehat{h^{*}q_{\phi}}.

Proof of Theorem
11.2
.

Our first task is to construct the map
c_{0}^{\prime}
.
Consider the mapping cylinder
M(c_{0}|_{Y_{0}})
of
c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}\colon Y_{0}\to B
and the homotopy equivalence
B\to M(c_{0}|_{Y_{0}})
.
The composition
Y_{0}\xrightarrow{c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}}B\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(c_{0}|_{Y_{0}})
is homotopic to the inclusion
Y_{0}\subset M(c_{0}|_{Y_{0}})
.
Applying the homotopy extension property, we deduce that
the composition
X_{0}\xrightarrow{c_{0}}B\xrightarrow{\simeq}M(c_{0}|_{Y})
is homotopic to a map
\widehat{c}_{0}\colon X_{0}\to M(c_{0}|_{Y})
such that
\widehat{c}_{0}|_{Y_{0}}
is the inclusion
Y_{0}\subset M(c_{0}|_{Y_{0}})
.
Composing this homotopy with the homotopy equivalence
\operatorname{proj}\colon M(c_{0}|_{Y})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\penalty\ B
shows that
\operatorname{proj}\circ\widehat{c}_{0}\simeq c_{0}\colon X_{0}\xrightarrow{}B
rel.
Y_{0}
.

Apply Proposition
7.7
with
X_{0}=X_{1}
to get a
3
-connected
\widehat{c}_{0}^{\prime}\colon X_{0}\to M(c_{0}|_{Y})
with
(\widehat{c}_{0}^{\prime})^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=\widehat{c}_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}
and
\widehat{c}_{0}^{\prime}|_{Y_{0}}=\widehat{c}_{0}|_{Y_{0}}
and such that
(\widehat{c}_{0}^{\prime},\widehat{c}_{0})
realises the compatible triple

(\operatorname{id}_{H_{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}-\psi\circ j_{*},\operatorname{id}_{H_{2}(X_{0},Y_{0};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}+j_{*}\circ\phi\circ\partial,\operatorname{id}_{Y_{0}}).

Here, it is legitimate to apply Proposition
7.7
because
[
CK25
, Lemma 6.1]
shows that
j_{*}
maps the
k
-invariant to zero, thus ensuring that this compatible triple is
k
-invariant preserving.
Define

c_{0}^{\prime}:=\operatorname{proj}\circ\widehat{c}_{0}^{\prime}\colon X_{0}\to B.

We have
(\widehat{c}_{0}^{\prime})^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=\widehat{c}_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}
and
\widehat{c}_{0}|_{Y_{0}}=\widehat{c}_{0}^{\prime}|_{Y_{0}}
.
Applying
\operatorname{proj}
and the rel.
Y_{0}
homotopy
\operatorname{proj}\circ\widehat{c}_{0}\simeq c_{0}
, it follows that
(c_{0}^{\prime})^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}
and
c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}=c_{0}^{\prime}|_{Y_{0}}
.
From this and
c_{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}(c_{1})
,
it follows that
c_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}(c_{1})
.
In particular, the pairing
b(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1})
is defined.

It remains to show that
\widehat{b}(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1})=\widehat{b}(c_{0},c_{1})-\widehat{h^{*}q_{\phi}}.

Apply the transitivity of Proposition
5.5
to get a homotopy equivalence
\mathcal{\varphi}\in\mathcal{G}
with
\varphi\circ c_{0}=c_{0}^{\prime}
rel.
Y_{0}
.
Proposition
5.8
then implies that

b(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1})=b(\varphi\circ c_{0},c_{1})=b(c_{0},c_{1})-b(\varphi).

We claim that
b(\varphi)=h^{*}b(\widehat{c}_{0}^{\prime},\widehat{c}_{0}),

as the conclusion that
\widehat{b}(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1})=\widehat{b}(c_{0},c_{1})-h^{*}\widehat{q}_{\phi}
will then follow promptly.
By definition of
b(\varphi)
, for
x,y\in H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and
\widehat{x},\widehat{y}\in\penalty\ H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

with
c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}^{*}(\widehat{x})=x
and
c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}^{*}(\widehat{y})=y
,
we have
b(\varphi)(x,y)=\langle\widehat{y},\widehat{x}\cap\xi\rangle
, with
\xi=(c_{0}^{\prime}\cup c_{0})_{*}([X_{0}\cup_{\operatorname{id}}X_{0}])\in H_{4}(B)
.
We now calculate

\displaystyle b(\varphi)(x,y)

\displaystyle=\langle\widehat{y},\widehat{x}\cap\xi\rangle=\langle\widehat{y},\widehat{x}\cap(c_{0}^{\prime}\cup c_{0})_{*}([X_{0}\cup_{\operatorname{id}}X_{0}])\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle(c_{0}^{\prime}\cup c_{0})^{*}(\widehat{y}),(c_{0}^{\prime}\cup c_{0})^{*}(\widehat{x})\cap[X_{0}\cup_{\operatorname{id}}X_{0}]\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle(\widehat{c}_{0}^{\prime}\cup\widehat{c}_{0})^{*}\operatorname{proj}^{*}(\widehat{y}),(\widehat{c}_{0}^{\prime}\cup\widehat{c}_{0})^{*}\operatorname{proj}^{*}(\widehat{x})\cap[X_{0}\cup_{\operatorname{id}}X_{0}]\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\operatorname{proj}^{*}(\widehat{y}),\operatorname{proj}^{*}(\widehat{x})\cap(\widehat{c}_{0}^{\prime}\cup\widehat{c}_{0})_{*}([X_{0}\cup_{\operatorname{id}}X_{0}])\rangle

\displaystyle=b(\widehat{c}_{0}^{\prime},\widehat{c}_{0})(h^{*}(x),h^{*}(y))

\displaystyle=h^{*}b(\widehat{c}_{0}^{\prime},\widehat{c}_{0})(x,y).

For the penultimate equality, we used the definition of
b(\widehat{c}_{0}^{\prime},\widehat{c}_{0})
together with the fact that
\operatorname{proj}^{*}(\widehat{x})
is a preimage of
h^{*}(x)
by
\widehat{c}_{0}|_{Y_{0}}^{*}
(and similarly,
\operatorname{proj}^{*}(\widehat{y})
is a preimage of
h^{*}(y)
):

\widehat{c}_{0}|_{Y_{0}}^{*}\operatorname{proj}^{*}(\widehat{x})=c_{0}|_{Y_{0}}^{*}(\widehat{x})=h^{*}c_{1}|_{Y_{1}}^{*}(\widehat{x})=h^{*}(x).

Thus
b(\varphi)=h^{*}b(\widehat{c}_{0}^{\prime},\widehat{c}_{0}),
establishing the claim.

We can now conclude.
Indeed, using the claim, it follows that

\widehat{b}(c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1})=\widehat{b}(c_{0},c_{1})-\widehat{b}(\varphi)=\widehat{b}(c_{0},c_{1})-\widehat{h^{*}b}(\widehat{c}_{0}^{\prime},\widehat{c}_{0})=\widehat{b}(c_{0},c_{1})-\widehat{h^{*}q_{\phi}},

where in the last equality, we used Proposition
11.10
.
∎

12.
Groups to which the theorems apply

This section describes situations in which our results apply.
In what follows, we let
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
be
4
-dimensional Poincaré pairs with
\pi_{1}(X_{0})\cong\pi_{1}(X_{1}):=\pi
that satisfy the conditions listed in Notation
2.3
.

Section
12.1
discusses examples of groups with
\operatorname{cd}(\pi)\leq 3
as this condition appeared in Theorem
2.8
as well as in Proposition
3.4
(which characterised when
\pi_{2}(X)
is projective).
Section
12.2
recalls the definition of the Whitehead functor
\Gamma(-)
and lists examples of pairs
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
for which
\Gamma(\pi_{2})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}]}\mathbb{Z}
is torsion-free: this hypothesis was present in Theorem
2.8
.
Section
12.3
gives situations in which the map
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(Y_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is injective, as this condition appeared in Theorems
2.9
and
2.15
.
Finally, Section
12.4
considers examples when the dualised evaluation map
\operatorname{ev}^{*}\colon H^{2}(X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{**}\to H_{2}(X_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}
is an isomorphism, as this assumption also played an important role in Theorems
2.15
,
2.17
and
2.22
.

12.1.
Examples of groups of cohomological dimension
\leq 3
.

Examples of groups of cohomological dimension at most
3
include the trivial group (
\operatorname{cd}=0
), free groups (
\operatorname{cd}=1
), surfaces groups and (nonfree) Baumslag–Solitar groups
BS(m,n)
(both families have
\operatorname{cd}=2
), as well as fundamental groups of closed oriented aspherical
3
-manifolds (
\operatorname{cd}=3
).
Free products of the above yield new groups with
\operatorname{cd}\leq 3
.
It is also helpful to recall that finite groups have infinite cohomological dimension.

Note that finite groups are good, whereas among the aforementioned groups with
\operatorname{cd}\leq 3
,
examples of good groups include

\mathbb{Z}
, the solvable Baumslag–Solitar groups
BS(1,n)
(this includes
BS(0,1)=\mathbb{Z}
and
BS(1,1)=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
) and
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
.
We refer to
[
BKK+21
, Chapter 19]
for a discussion of good groups but note that elementary amenable groups are good.

12.2.
The
\Gamma
group condition

The
Whitehead group
of an abelian group
A
consists of the group
\Gamma(A)
generated by the elements of
A
(we write
v(a)\in\Gamma(A)
for the element corresponding to
a\in A
) and subject to the relations

\displaystyle v(-a)=v(a)\quad\text{ for all }a\in A,

\displaystyle v(a+b+c)=v(b+c)+v(a+c)+v(a+b)-v(a)-v(b)-v(c),\quad\text{ for all }a,b,c\in A.

If
A
is free abelian with basis
\mathcal{B}
then, by
[
Whi50
, page 62]
,
\Gamma(A)
is free abelian with basis

\{v(b),v(b+b^{\prime})-v(b)-v(b^{\prime})\}_{b\neq b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}}.

In this case, it is customary to consider
\Gamma(A)
as the subgroup of symmetric elements of
A\otimes A
given by sending
v(a)
to
a\otimes a
.
We refer to
[
Whi50
]
for more details on this construction but simply note that for
X
a
4
-dimensional Poincaré complex
\Gamma(\pi_{2}(X))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)]}\mathbb{Z}
is known to be torsion free for finite fundamental groups
\pi_{1}(X)
with 4-periodic cohomology
[
HK88
]
(e.g. finite cyclic groups) and more generally for finite groups where only the
2
-Sylow subgroup of the fundamental group has
4
-periodic cohomology
[
Bau88
]
.
This is also known to hold for finite groups whose
2
-Sylow subgroup is abelian with at most two generators
[
KPR24
]

or dihedral
[
KNR22
]
.

We note that the same is true in our setting.

Proposition 12.1
.

Assume that
\pi
is a finite group whose
2
-Sylow subgroup is dihedral, abelian with 2 generators or has 4-periodic cohomology.
If
(X,Y)
is a 4-dimensional Poincaré pair with
\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(X)=:\pi
surjective, then
\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)]}\Gamma(\pi_{2}(X))
is torsion-free.

Proof.

Lemma
3.3
gives finitely generated free
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-modules
C_{0}
,
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
and an exact sequence of
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-modules
0\to\pi_{2}(X)\to C_{2}\to C_{1}\to C_{0}\to\mathbb{Z}\to 0
.
Write
\ker(d_{2}):=\pi_{2}(X)
.
The fact that
\mathbb{Z}\otimes\Gamma(\ker(d_{2}))
is torsion-free now follows from (a simplification of) the argument in
[
KPR24
, Proof of Theorem 1.1 in Section 4]
,
[
KNR22
, Theorem 4.1]
and
[
HK88
, Theorem 2.1 and Remark 2.5]
.
We emphasise that in these articles
the passage from the
2
-Sylow subgroups relies on the work of Bauer
[
Bau88
]
.
We also note that for closed
4
-manifolds, the situation is actually more delicate since
\mathbb{Z}\otimes\Gamma(\operatorname{coker}(d_{2}))
must also be shown to be torsion-free.
∎

12.3.
Situations in which
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(Y_{i};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is injective.

Let
Y
be a closed oriented
3
-manifold and let
\pi_{1}(Y)\twoheadrightarrow\pi
be a surjection.

Proposition 12.2
.

If
H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}=0
, then
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is injective.

Proof.

The UCSS gives rise to an exact sequence
H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}\to H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.
Since
H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}=0
, it follows that
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is indeed injective.
∎

Proposition 12.3
.

The induced homomorphism
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is injective in the following circumstances:

(1)

\pi
is free, finite or a
\operatorname{PD}_{3}
group.

(2)

\pi
is a free product of a free group and
\operatorname{PD}_{3}
groups.

(3)

\pi=\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
is the fundamental group of an orientable surface,
Y=\Sigma\times S^{1}
, and
\pi_{1}(\Sigma\times S^{1})\to\pi
is the projection onto the first coordinate.

Proof.

The cases where
\pi
is free or a
\operatorname{PD}_{3}
group follow from the second case, but we treat them separately as a warm up.
For the groups in the first statement, we argue that
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
from which the conclusion is immediate.
For free and finite groups this is clear, whereas for a
\operatorname{PD}_{3}
group
G
, one notes that

H^{2}(G;\mathbb{Z}[G])\cong H_{1}(G;\mathbb{Z}[G])=0.

Next, we consider the case where
\pi=F**_{i=1}^{r}G_{i}
is a free product of a free group
F
and
\operatorname{PD}_{3}
-groups
G_{1},\ldots,G_{r}
.
Since
BH\vee BH^{\prime}
is a model for
B(H*H^{\prime})
, repeated applications of the Mayer-Vietoris sequence show that

H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong H^{2}(F;\Res_{F}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}H^{2}(G_{i};\Res_{G_{i}}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong 0\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}H_{1}(G_{i};\Res_{G_{i}}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),

where the last isomorphism follows from Poincaré duality.
Since
\Res_{G_{i}}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
is free as a
\mathbb{Z}[G_{i}]
-module (see e.g.
[
Bro94
, pages 13-14]
),
H_{1}(G_{i};\Res_{G_{i}}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
and the result follows.

The third statement follows by noting that
Y:=\Sigma\times S^{1}\to\Sigma\simeq B\pi
admits a section and, in particular, the induced map
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is injective.
∎

Note that the third case of Proposition
12.3
is relevant if one is attempting to decide whether a
4
-dimensional Poincaré pair is homotopy equivalent to
(\Sigma_{g}\times D^{2},\Sigma_{g}\times S^{1})
.

Remark 12.4
.

We conclude with an example where
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is not injective.
Consider the setting where
F_{2}\cong\pi_{1}(S^{1}\times S^{2}\#S^{1}\times S^{2})\to\pi:=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is abelianisation.
The induced map
\mathbb{Z}\cong H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
factors through
H^{2}(BF_{2};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=H^{2}(S^{1}\vee S^{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
, is therefore trivial, and thus non-injective.
This shows that Theorem
1.3
cannot be applied when trying to decide whether a given Poincaré pair is homotopy equivalent to a
4
-dimensional Poincaré pair of the form
(X,S^{1}\times S^{2}\#S^{1}\times S^{2})
with
\pi_{1}(X)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

12.4.
Examples for which
\operatorname{ev}^{*}\colon H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{**}\to H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}
is an isomorphism.

Let
(X,Y)
be a
4
-dimensional Poincaré pair with
\pi_{1}(Y)\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{1}(X):=\pi
a surjection.

Proposition 12.5
.

The dualised evaluation map

\operatorname{ev}_{X}^{*}\colon H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{**}\to H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}

is an isomorphism in the following circumstances:

(1)

\pi
is free or finite.

(2)

\pi=\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
is an orientable surface group or a solvable Baumslag–Solitar group
BS(1,n)
.

(3)

\pi
is a
\operatorname{PD}_{3}
group.

(4)

\pi
is a free product of free and
PD_{3}
groups.

Proof.

Most of the first and third cases follow from the fourth, but we treat them separately as a warm up.
We begin with some general considerations.
Looking at the second diagonal of the UCSS yields the exact sequence

0\to H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\xrightarrow{\operatorname{ev}_{X}}H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}\xrightarrow{d_{3}}H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).

Since
H_{1}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
and
H_{0}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{0}(X,\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is an isomorphism (because
Y\to X
is
\pi_{1}
-surjective), it follows that
H^{3}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong H_{1}(X,\partial X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
.
Looking at the third diagonal of the UCSS, this implies that
\operatorname{coker}(d_{3})=0
and so we obtain the short exact sequence

(12.6)

0\to H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\xrightarrow{\operatorname{ev}_{X}}H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}\to H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to 0.

It follows that for
\pi
a free or a finite group,
\operatorname{ev}_{X}
is an isomorphism and dualising therefore gives the first assertion.
We move on to the second assertion.
If
\pi
is the fundamental group of an orientable surface
\Sigma
, then
H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong H^{3}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
and
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}\cong H^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{*}=0.

By
[
HKT09
, Lemmas 6.2 and 6.4]
, the same is true if
\pi
is a solvable Baumslag–Solitar group. Hence, dualising (
12.6
), we see that
\operatorname{ev}_{X}^{*}
is an isomorphism as claimed.
Next we consider the third assertion, i.e. the case where
\pi
is a
\operatorname{PD}_{3}
group.
In this case
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
and
H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong\mathbb{Z}
.
Again, we deduce that
H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}=0
and the assertion follows by dualising (
12.6
) and using that
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong H^{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong H_{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi)=0
:

0\to\overbrace{H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}}^{=0}\to H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{**}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{ev}_{X}^{*}}H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}\to\overbrace{\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}^{=0}.

It remains to prove the fourth assertion, i.e. the case where
\pi\cong F**_{i=1}^{r}G_{i}
with
F
a free group and
G_{i}
a
PD_{3}
-group.
The argument is essentially the same as
[
HKPR25
, Lemmas 5.6 and 5.8]
, but we repeat it for the reader’s convenience.
Since
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
(by the proof of Proposition
12.3
), dualising (
12.6
) yields the exact sequence

(12.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{**}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ev}_{X}^{*}}

We assert that
H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}=0
.
For each
i
, we have
H^{3}(G_{i};\mathbb{Z}[G_{i}])\cong H_{0}(G_{i};\mathbb{Z}[G_{i}])\cong\mathbb{Z}
.
A Mayer–Vietoris argument as in the proof of Proposition
12.3

By
[
HKPR25
, Lemma 4.9]
, we have
H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong\oplus_{i=1}^{r}E_{i}
, where
E_{i}=\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[G_{i}]}\mathbb{Z}=\Ind_{G_{i}}^{\pi}\big(\mathbb{Z}\big)
.
Taking duals gives

E_{i}^{*}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(\Ind_{G_{i}}^{\pi}\big(\mathbb{Z}\big),\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[G_{i}]}(\mathbb{Z},\Res_{G_{i}}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).

Since
G_{i}
is infinite, the only
G_{i}
-fixed point in
\mathbb{Z}[G_{i}]
is the trivial element,
and thererefore
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[G_{i}]}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z}[G_{i}])=0
.
Since
\Res^{\pi}_{G_{j}}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
is free,
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[G_{j}]}(\mathbb{Z},\Res_{G_{j}}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
.
Thus
E_{i}^{*}=0
.
We conclude that
H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}E_{i}^{*}=0
as asserted.

Next, we assert that
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
.
Apply the adjunction from Lemma
12.8
, we obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(\oplus_{i=1}^{r}E_{i},\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong\prod_{i=1}^{r}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(E_{j},\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\cong\prod_{i=1}^{r}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\big(\Ind_{G_{i}}^{\pi}\big(\mathbb{Z}\big),\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\big)\cong\prod_{i=1}^{r}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}[G_{j}]}\big(\mathbb{Z},\Res_{G_{i}}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\big).

We consider each factor separately. By Poincaré duality, we have

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}[G_{i}]}\big(\mathbb{Z},\Res_{G_{i}}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\big)\cong H^{1}\big(G_{i};\Res_{G_{i}}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\big)\cong H_{2}(G_{i};\Res_{G_{i}}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).

Since
\Res_{G_{j}}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
is free, this is trivial.
Thus
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, as asserted.

Since the assertions show that
H^{*}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
and
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),\mathbb{Z}[\pi])=0
, it follows from (
12.7
) that
\operatorname{ev}_{X}^{*}
is an isomorphism, as required.
∎

It remains to establish a result that was used during the proof of Proposition
12.5
.

Lemma 12.8
.

Let
\pi
be a group.
If
G\leq\pi
is a subgroup,
M
is a
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-module and
N
is a
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module then, for every
k\geq 1
, there is an isomorphism

\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathbb{Z}[G]}^{k}(M,\Res^{\pi}_{G}N)\cong\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}^{k}(\Ind_{G}^{\pi}M,N).

Proof.

If
F_{*}
is a free
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-resolution of
M
, then
\Ind_{G}^{\pi}F_{*}\cong\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[G]}F_{*}
is a sequence of free
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-modules.
Since
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
is free as a
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-module
it is flat as a
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-module, so
\Ind_{G}^{\pi}(-)=\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[G]}-
preserves exactness and therefore
\Ind_{G}^{\pi}F_{*}
is a free
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-resolution of
\Ind_{G}^{\pi}M=\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[G]}F_{*}
.
Thus

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}^{k}(\Ind_{G}^{\pi}M,N)

\displaystyle\cong H^{k}(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(\Ind_{G}^{\pi}F_{*},N))\cong H^{k}(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[G]}(F_{*},\Res_{G}^{\pi}N))

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathbb{Z}[G]}^{k}(M,\Res^{\pi}_{G}N)

as claimed.
∎

Our final result does not directly address
\operatorname{ev}_{X}^{*}
being an isomorphism.
Instead it shows that this condition ensures that
\operatorname{Herm}(\operatorname{ev}_{X})\colon\operatorname{Herm}(H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*})\to\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]))
is also an isomorphism.
This was used during the proof of Theorem
5
.

Proposition 12.9
.

If the map

\operatorname{ev}_{X}^{*}\colon H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{**}\to H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}

is an isomorphism of abelian groups, then so is

\operatorname{Herm}(\operatorname{ev}_{X})\colon\operatorname{Herm}(H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*})\to\operatorname{Herm}(H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])).

In particular
\operatorname{Herm}(\operatorname{ev}_{X})
is injective if
\pi
is a Baumslag–Solitar group, an orientable surface group, a free group, a
\operatorname{PD}_{3}
or a free product of a free group and
\operatorname{PD}_{3}
-groups.

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as
[
HKPR25
, Proposition 5.9]
but we repeat it for the reader’s convenience.
Applying the covariant functor
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*},-)
to
\operatorname{ev}_{X}^{*}
, we see that

(\operatorname{ev}_{X}^{*})_{*}\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*},H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{**})\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*},H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*})

is an isomorphism.
Next, apply
Lemma

9.27
with
f=\operatorname{ev}_{X}
and
A=H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
to see that

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(\operatorname{ev}_{X},H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*})\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*},H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*})\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*})

is an isomorphism.
Consider the following diagram in which the top horizontal is the composition
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(\operatorname{ev}_{X},H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*})\circ(\operatorname{ev}_{X}^{*})_{*}
:

(12.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*},H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{**})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sesq(H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sesq(H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])).}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sesq(\operatorname{ev}_{X})}

Here
\Sesq(\operatorname{ev}_{X})(b)(\alpha,\beta)=b(\operatorname{ev}_{X}(\alpha),\operatorname{ev}_{X}(\beta)).

This diagram commutes: indeed, unravelling the definitions shows that for
\alpha,\beta\in H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and
\varphi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*},H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{**})
, one has
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(\operatorname{ev}_{X},H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*})\circ(\operatorname{ev}_{X}^{*})_{*}(\varphi)(\alpha)=\varphi(\operatorname{ev}_{X}(\alpha))(\operatorname{ev}_{X}(\beta))
.
In particular, it follows that
\Sesq(\operatorname{ev}_{X})
is an isomorphism.

The set of hermitian forms on
H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*}
arises as the fixed point set of the involution on
\Sesq(H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])^{*})
given by
b\mapsto((x,y)\mapsto\overline{b(y,x)}
).
Thus
\operatorname{Herm}(\operatorname{ev}_{X})
is obtained by restricting
\Sesq(\operatorname{ev}_{X})
to hermitian forms.
It follows that
\operatorname{Herm}(\operatorname{ev}_{X})
is an isomorphism, as required.
∎

Appendix A
Twisted homology

The aims of this section are to fix our conventions concerning twisted homology and cohomology as well as to collect several results that are used throughout this article.
Proofs that consist of direct verifications are omitted.

A.1.
The evaluation map

Fix a pair
(X,Y)
and a ring
R
that is endowed with an involution.
Let
M
and
M^{\prime}
be
(R,\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)])
-bimodules and let
S
be an
(R,R)
-bimodule.
Furthermore, let

\Theta\colon M^{\prime}\times M\to S

be a nonsingular

\pi
-invariant sesquilinear pairing, meaning that for every
m\in M,m^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}
and
r,s\in R,\gamma\in\pi_{1}(X)
, it satisfies
\langle m^{\prime}\gamma,m\gamma\rangle=\langle m^{\prime},m\rangle
and
\langle rm^{\prime}\gamma,sm\gamma\rangle=r\langle m^{\prime},m\rangle\overline{s}
.
Reformulating, if
\overline{M}
denotes the
(\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)],R)
-bimodule obtained by using the involutions on
R
and
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)]
, then
\Theta
is an
(R,R)
-linear map

M^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)]}\overline{M}\to S.

We proceed to the main construction of this section.

Construction A.1
.

(The evaluation
\operatorname{ev}\colon H^{i}(X,Y;M)\to\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{\text{left-}R}(H_{i}(X,Y;M^{\prime}),S)}
.)

This evaluation map is defined in three steps.
Consider the map

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\text{right-}\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)]}(\overline{C_{*}(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{Y})},M)

\displaystyle\to\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{\text{left-}R}(M^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)]}C_{*}(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{Y}),M^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)]}\overline{M})}

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto\left((m^{\prime}\otimes\sigma)\mapsto(m^{\prime}\otimes f(\sigma))\right).

Composing with the pairing
\Theta
gives rise to the chain map

\displaystyle\kappa\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{\text{right-}\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)]}(\overline{C_{*}(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{Y})},M)

\displaystyle\to\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{\text{left-}R}(M^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)]}C_{*}(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{Y}),S)},

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto\left((m^{\prime}\otimes\sigma)\mapsto\Theta(m^{\prime},f(\sigma))\right).

The nonsingularity of
\Theta
ensures this is an isomorphism of cochain complexes of left
R
-modules.

Evaluating cocycles on homology classes yields a homomorphism of left
R
-modules

E\colon H^{i}(\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{\text{left-}R}(M^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)]}C_{\ast}(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{Y}),S)})\xrightarrow{}\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{\text{left-}R}(H_{i}(X,Y;M^{\prime}),S)}.

Finally, the evaluation map
\operatorname{ev}
, which is also left
R
-linear, is obtained by composing
\kappa
with
E
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{ev}\colon H^{i}(X,Y;M)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\kappa,\cong}H^{i}(\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{\text{left-}R}(M^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)]}C_{\ast}(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{Y}),S)})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{E}\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{\text{left-}R}(H_{i}(X,Y;M^{\prime}),S)}.

In what follows we often write
\langle\varphi,x\rangle
instead of
\operatorname{ev}(\varphi)(x).

Example A.2
.

Let
\varphi\colon\pi_{1}(X)\twoheadrightarrow\pi
be an epimorphism.
Continuing with the notation above, the example that most relevant to this article is the case where
M=M^{\prime}=R=S=\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
(with the module structure
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)]
-module structure induced by
\varphi
) and the pairing is

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\overline{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\operatorname{mult}}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto x\overline{y}.

In this case, the evaluation map satisfies

\operatorname{ev}([f])([m^{\prime}\otimes\sigma])=m^{\prime}\overline{f(\sigma)}.

A.2.
Twisted cap and cup products

Let
X
be a space with fundamental group
\pi
and with universal cover
\widetilde{X}
. Let
R
be a ring with involution, and let
M,N
be
(R,\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
-bimodules.
Given an
(i+j)
-chain
\sigma\in C_{i+j}(\widetilde{X})
, we use
\lrcorner\sigma\in C_{i}(\widetilde{X})
and
\llcorner\sigma\in C_{j}(\widetilde{X})
to denote the front-face
i
-chain and back-face
j
-chain of
\sigma
.
They are defined on a singular simplex
s_{i+j}\colon\Delta^{i+j}\to\widetilde{X}
as follows and then extended linearly to chains: in barycentric coordinates, define projections
p_{\lrcorner}\colon\Delta^{i+j}\to\Delta^{i}
by
[v_{0},\ldots,v_{i+j}]\mapsto[v_{0},\ldots,v_{i}]
and
p_{\llcorner}\colon\Delta^{i+j}\to\Delta^{j}
by
[v_{0},\ldots,v_{i+j}]\mapsto[v_{i},\ldots,v_{i+j}]
. Now define
\lrcorner s_{i+j}=s_{i+j}\circ p_{\lrcorner}
and
\llcorner s_{i+j}=s_{i+j}\circ p_{\llcorner}
.
Given twisted cochains
f\in C^{i}(X;M)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\text{right-}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(\overline{C_{i}(\widetilde{X})},M)
and
g\in C^{j}(X;N)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\text{right-}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(\overline{C_{j}(\widetilde{X})},N)
, a chain
\sigma\in C_{i+j}(\widetilde{X})
, and a twisted chain
b\otimes\sigma^{\prime}\in C_{k}(X;N)
, one sets

\displaystyle\big(f\cup g\big)(\sigma)

\displaystyle:=f(\lrcorner\sigma)\boxtimes g(\llcorner\sigma)\in M\boxtimes N,

\displaystyle f\cap(b\otimes\sigma^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\big(f(\lrcorner\sigma^{\prime})\boxtimes b\big)\otimes\llcorner\sigma^{\prime}\in C_{k-i}(X;M\boxtimes N).

A verification shows that
\cup
and
\cap
define
(R,R)
-bilinear maps

\displaystyle\cup\colon C^{i}(X;M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\overline{C^{j}(X;N)}\to C^{i+j}(X;M\boxtimes N),

\displaystyle\cap\colon C^{i}(X;M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\overline{C_{k}(X;N)}\to C_{k-i}(X;M\boxtimes N).

Indeed we have

\displaystyle(rf\cup gs)(\sigma)=(rf)(\lrcorner\sigma)\boxtimes(\overline{s}g)(\llcorner\sigma)=rf(\lrcorner\sigma)\boxtimes\overline{s}g(\llcorner\sigma)=r(f(\lrcorner\sigma)\boxtimes g(\llcorner\sigma))s=(r(f\cup g)s)(\sigma),

\displaystyle(rf)\cap(b\otimes\sigma^{\prime})s=(rf(\lrcorner\sigma^{\prime})\boxtimes\overline{s}b)\otimes\llcorner\sigma^{\prime}=r\left(\big(f(\lrcorner\sigma^{\prime})\boxtimes b\big)\otimes\llcorner\sigma^{\prime}\right)s.

It can be checked that
\cup
and
\cap
are chain maps and descend to (co)homology, leading to the main definition of this subsection.

Definition A.3
.

Let
X
be a space with fundamental group
\pi
and, and let
M,N
be
(R,\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
-bimodules. The chain maps
\cup
and
\cap
defined above respectively induce the
(R,R)
-linear
twisted cup product
and
twisted cap product

\displaystyle\cup\colon H^{i}(X;M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\overline{H^{j}(X;N)}\to H^{i+j}(X;M\boxtimes N),

\displaystyle\cap\colon H^{i}(X;M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\overline{H_{k}(X;N)}\to H_{k-i}(X;M\boxtimes N).

We note that the relation
\big(\alpha\cup\beta\big)\cap\sigma=\alpha\cap\big(\beta\cap\sigma\big)
holds between the cap and the cup product, as in the untwisted case
[
Bre93
, Theorem 5.2 (3)]
.

Remark A.4
.

We recall briefly how cup and cap products extend to pairs, referring to
[
Fri23
, Part XXIV]
for more details.
Recall that a triple
(X,Y,Z)
of spaces is called an
excisive triad
if the inclusion
(Y,Y\cap Z)\to(Y\cup Z,Z)
induces isomorphisms on homology.
Similarly, following
[
Fri23
, Part XXIV]
, if
X
admits a universal cover
p\colon\widetilde{X}\to X
, we say a triple
(X,Y,Z)
is
an universally excisive triad
if
(\widetilde{X},p^{-1}(Y),p^{-1}(Z))
is an excisive triad.
Given such an universally excisive triad, Definition
A.3
extends to a relative cup and cap products

\displaystyle\cup\colon H^{i}(X,Y;M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\overline{H^{j}(X,Z;N)}\to H^{i+j}(X,Y\cup Z;M\boxtimes N),

\displaystyle\cap\colon H^{i}(X,Y;M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\overline{H_{k}(X,Y\cup Z;N)}\to H_{k-i}(X,Z;M\boxtimes N).

In the sequel, we will mostly be concerned with the case where
Y=\emptyset
or
Z=\emptyset.

Recalling the map isomorphism
\varpi_{0}\colon H_{0}\big(X;M\boxtimes N\big)\to M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\overline{N}
from Lemma
8.3
, we also note the graded symmetry of the twisted cup product.

Lemma A.5
.

Let
(X,Y)
be a pair with fundamental group
\pi
and, let
M
be an
(R,\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
-bimodule, let
\Psi\colon M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\overline{M}\to R
be a hermitian form.
Then for any
a\in H^{k}(X;M)
, any
b\in H^{l}(X;N)
and any
\sigma\in H_{k+l}(X,Y)
, the cup product satisfies the following graded symmetry:

\Psi\circ\varpi_{0}\circ((b\cup a)\cap\sigma)=(-1)^{kl}\overline{\Psi\circ\varpi_{0}((a\cup b)\cap\sigma)}\in R.

In particular, for
\Psi=\operatorname{mult}\colon\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\overline{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi],(x,y)\mapsto x\overline{y}
, and
\operatorname{mult}_{0}:=\operatorname{mult}\circ\varpi_{0}
as in Notation
9.10
, this yields

\operatorname{mult}_{0}((b\cup a)\cap\sigma)=(-1)^{kl}\overline{\operatorname{mult}_{0}(((a\cup b)\cap\sigma)}\in\mathbb{Z}[\pi].

Proof.

Consider the following diagram of abelian groups:

\textstyle{H^{k}(X,Y;M)\otimes H^{l}(X;M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-1)^{kl}\cup}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{swap}}
\textstyle{H^{k+l}(X,Y;M\boxtimes M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\cap\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{flip}}
\textstyle{H_{0}(X;M\boxtimes M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varpi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{flip}}
\textstyle{M\otimes\overline{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{flip}}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x\mapsto\overline{x}}
\textstyle{H^{l}(X;M)\otimes H^{k}(X,Y;M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\textstyle{H^{k+l}(X,Y;M\boxtimes M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\cap\sigma}
\textstyle{H_{0}(X;M\boxtimes M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varpi_{0}}
\textstyle{M\otimes\overline{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\textstyle{R.}

The statement amounts to proving that this diagram commutes.
The left square commutes by
[
Fri23
, Twisted Cup Product-Commutativity Proposition]
.
The second square from the left commutes by naturality of the cap product.
The commutativity of the third square is readily verified.
The rightmost square commutes because
\Psi
is hermitian.
∎

We conclude by noting the relation between cap products and evaluation.

Lemma A.6
.

Let
\pi
be a group, let
(X,Y)
be a pair,
and let
\varphi\colon\pi_{1}(X)\twoheadrightarrow\pi
be a surjection.
For
\varphi\in H^{k}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and
x\in H_{k}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, we have

\operatorname{mult}\circ\varpi_{0}(\varphi\cap x)=\overline{\langle\varphi,x\rangle}\in\mathbb{Z}[\pi].

Proof.

Without loss of generality we can assume that
x=[m^{\prime}\otimes\sigma]
.
Writing
f
for a cocycle representative of
\varphi
, the definition of the cap product and Example
A.2
give

\displaystyle\operatorname{mult}\circ\varpi_{0}(\varphi\cap x)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{mult}\circ\varpi_{0}(f(\sigma)\otimes m^{\prime}\otimes\operatorname{pt})=\operatorname{mult}(f(\sigma)\otimes m^{\prime})=f(\sigma)\overline{m^{\prime}}.=\overline{\operatorname{ev}([f])([m^{\prime}\otimes\sigma])}

\displaystyle=\overline{\langle\varphi,x\rangle}.

This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

A.3.
Twisted intersection forms

This section has two goals.
The first is to recall the definition of the equivariant intersection form.
The second, given a
space
X
, an epimorphism
\varphi\colon\pi_{1}(X)\twoheadrightarrow\pi
and
\sigma\in H_{4}(X)
,
is to collect some properties of the pairing

\displaystyle b_{\sigma}\colon H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle y,x\cap\sigma\rangle.

Namely we will reformulate this pairing using cup products, show that it is hermitian and relate it to the equivariant intersection form in the case where
X
is a Poincaré complex and
\sigma
is the fundamental class of
X
.

Definition A.7
.

Let
(X,Y)
be a
4
-dimensional Poincaré pair with
\pi:=\pi_{1}(X)
.

•

The
cohomological relative equivariant intersection form
is

\displaystyle b_{X}^{\partial}\colon H^{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle\beta,\operatorname{PD}_{X}(\alpha)\rangle=\langle\beta,\alpha\cap[X]\rangle.

•

The
relative equivariant intersection form
is

\displaystyle\lambda_{X}^{\partial}\colon H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H_{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle\operatorname{PD}_{X}^{-1}(y),x\rangle.

Remark A.8
.

Observe that Poincaré duality induces an isometry between these two pairings in the sense that for
\alpha\in H^{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and
\beta\in H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
one has

\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(\operatorname{PD}_{X}(\alpha),\operatorname{PD}_{X}(\beta))=\langle\operatorname{PD}^{-1}(\operatorname{PD}_{X}(\beta)),\operatorname{PD}_{X}(\alpha)\rangle=\langle\beta,\operatorname{PD}_{X}(\alpha)\rangle=b_{X}^{\partial}(\alpha,\beta).

In what follows, it is helpful to recall from Notation
9.10
that we write

\operatorname{mult}_{0}\colon H_{0}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\boxtimes\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\xrightarrow{\varpi_{0}}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\overline{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{mult}}\mathbb{Z}[\pi].

The next lemma expresses the pairing
b_{\sigma}
in terms of the cup product.

Lemma A.9
.

Let
\pi
be a group, let
X
be a
space, and let
\varphi\colon\pi_{1}(X)\twoheadrightarrow\pi
be a surjection.
The pairing
b_{\sigma}
agrees with the hermitian pairing

\displaystyle b_{\sigma}^{\cup}\colon H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\operatorname{mult}_{0}((x\cup y)\cap\sigma).

In particular the pairing
b_{\sigma}
is hermitian.

Proof.

The graded symmetry of the cup product (recall Lemma
A.5
) ensures that
b_{\sigma}^{\cup}
is hermitian.
The equality
b_{\sigma}=b_{\sigma}^{\cup}
now follows directly from the cup-cap-formula
and Lemma
A.6
:

\displaystyle b_{\sigma}^{\cup}(x,y)=\overline{b_{\sigma}^{\cup}(y,x)}=\overline{\operatorname{mult}_{0}((y\cup x)\cap\sigma)}=\overline{\operatorname{mult}_{0}((y\cap(x\cap\sigma)}=\langle y,x\cap\sigma\rangle=b_{\sigma}(x,y).

The fact that
b_{\sigma}
is hermitian follows from the fact that
b_{\sigma}^{\cup}
is hermitian.
∎

The same proof yields the following result for Poincaré pairs.

Lemma A.10
.

Let
(X,Y)
be a
4
-dimensional Poincaré pair with
\pi:=\pi_{1}(X)
.
The pairing
b_{X}^{\partial}
agrees with the pairing

\displaystyle b_{X}^{\partial,\cup}\colon H^{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\overline{\operatorname{mult}(\varpi_{0}((y\cup x)\cap[X]))}.

Proof.

This follows directly from the relation between the cap and cup products and Lemma
A.6

\displaystyle b_{X}^{\partial,\cup}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{mult}_{0}((y\cup x)\cap[X])=\operatorname{mult}_{0}((y\cap(x\cap[X]))=\overline{\langle y,x\cap[X]\rangle}

\displaystyle=\overline{b_{M}^{\partial}(x,y)}.

This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

A.4.
Twisted cross products

Let
X_{1},X_{2}
be spaces, let
R_{1},R_{2},
and
R
be rings.
Let
M
be a
(R_{1},\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{1})]\times R)
-bimodule, and let
N
be a
(R,\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{2})]\times R_{2})
-bimodule.
Identify the ring
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{1})\times\pi_{1}(X_{2})]
with
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{1})]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{2})]
and endow the
(R_{1},R_{2})
-bimodule
M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}N
with the right
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{1})\times\pi_{1}(X_{2})]
-diagonal action given by
(m_{1},m_{2})\cdot(\gamma_{1}\otimes\gamma_{2})=(m_{1}\gamma_{1},m_{2}\gamma_{2})
so that it becomes a
(R_{1},\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{1})\times\pi_{1}(X_{2})]\times R_{2})
-bimodule.
Recall that the Eilenberg-Zilber theorem ensures there are chain maps

\displaystyle\times^{\prime}\colon C_{*}(\widetilde{X}_{1})\otimes C_{*}(\widetilde{X}_{2})\to C_{*}(\widetilde{X}_{1}\times\widetilde{X}_{2})

\displaystyle\theta\colon C_{*}(\widetilde{X}_{1}\times\widetilde{X}_{2})\to C_{*}(\widetilde{X}_{1})\otimes C_{*}(\widetilde{X}_{2})

which are unique up to chain homotopy, are natural in
X
and
Y
, and that are chain homotopy inverses of one another.
We now begin the construction of the cross products.

Construction A.11

(Twisted cross products on chain complexes and homology)
.

A
cross product
on the twisted chain complexes is the
(R,R)
-linear chain homotopy equivalence with

\displaystyle\times\colon C_{k}(X_{1};M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\overline{C_{l}(X_{2};N)}

\displaystyle\to C_{k+l}(X_{1}\times X_{2};M\boxtimes N)

\displaystyle\Big(a\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{1})]}\widetilde{\sigma}_{X_{1}},b\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(Y)]}\widetilde{\sigma}_{X_{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(a\boxtimes b\big)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{1}\times X_{2})]}\widetilde{\sigma}_{X_{1}}\times^{\prime}\widetilde{\sigma}_{X_{2}}.

To see that
\times
is indeed a chain homotopy equivalence, express it as the composition

\displaystyle\times\colon C_{k}(X_{1};M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}C_{l}(X_{2};N)

\displaystyle=\left(M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}C_{*}(\widetilde{X}_{1})\right)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\left(N\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}C_{*}(\widetilde{X}_{2})\right)

\displaystyle\cong(M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}N)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{1})\times\pi_{1}(X_{2})]}C_{k}(\widetilde{X}_{1})\otimes C_{l}(\widetilde{X}_{2})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\operatorname{id}\otimes\times^{\prime}}(M\boxtimes_{R}N)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{1}\times X_{2})]}C_{k+l}(\widetilde{X}_{1}\times\widetilde{X}_{2})

\displaystyle=C_{k+l}(X_{1}\times X_{2};M\boxtimes_{R}B).

Since
\times^{\prime}
is unique up to chain homotopy, it then makes sense to define the
twisted cross product
to be the
(R,R)
-linear map induced by
\times
on homology:

\times\colon H_{k}(X_{1};M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\overline{H_{l}(X_{2};N)}\to H_{k+l}(X_{1}\times X_{2};M\boxtimes N).

Construction A.12

(Twisted cross products on cochain complexes and cohomology.)
.

A
cross product
on twisted cochain complexes is the
(R,R)
-linear chain homotopy equivalence

\displaystyle C^{k}(X_{1};M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}C^{l}(X_{2};N)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{1})]}\left(\overline{C_{k}(\widetilde{X}_{1})},M\right)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{2})]}\left(\overline{C_{l}(\widetilde{X}_{2})},N\right)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{h,\simeq}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{1})\times\pi_{1}(X_{2})]}\left(\overline{C_{k}(\widetilde{X})\otimes C_{l}(\widetilde{X}_{2})},M\boxtimes N\right)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\theta^{*},\simeq}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{1}\times X_{2})]}\left(\overline{C_{k+l}(\widetilde{X}_{1}\times\widetilde{X}_{2})},M\boxtimes N\right)

\displaystyle=C^{k+l}(X_{1}\times X_{2};M\boxtimes N),

where the map
h
is induced by mapping
f\otimes g
to
(\sigma_{X}\otimes\sigma_{X_{2}})\mapsto f(\sigma_{X})\otimes g(\sigma_{X_{2}}).

To see that
h
is indeed a chain homotopy equivalence,
consider the following commutative diagram in which the horizontal maps arise from the fact that
X_{1},X_{2}
have the homotopy type of finite CW complexes, and the map
h^{\operatorname{CW}}
is defined similarly to
h
but using the cellular chain complex:

\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}\left(\overline{C_{k}(\widetilde{X}_{1})},M\right)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\operatorname{Hom}\left(\overline{C_{l}(\widetilde{X}_{2})},N\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}\left(\overline{C_{k}^{\operatorname{CW}}(\widetilde{X}_{1})},M\right)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\overline{\operatorname{Hom}(C_{l}^{\operatorname{CW}}(\widetilde{X}_{2}),N)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{h^{\operatorname{CW}}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}\left(\overline{C_{k}(\widetilde{X}_{1})\otimes C_{l}(\widetilde{X}_{2})},M\boxtimes N\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}\left(\overline{C_{k}^{\operatorname{CW}}(\widetilde{X}_{1})\otimes C_{l}^{\operatorname{CW}}(\widetilde{X}_{2})},M\boxtimes N\right).}

Since
\theta
is unique up to chain homotopy, it then makes sense to define the
twisted cross product
to be
(R,R)
-linear map induced by
\times
on cohomology:

\times\colon H^{k}(X_{1};M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\overline{H^{l}(X_{2};N)}\to H^{k+l}(X_{1}\times X_{2};M\boxtimes N).

Next we recall the statement of the Künneth theorem, assuming for simplicity that
R=\mathbb{Z}
.

Proposition A.13
.

Assume that
X_{1},X_{2}
are finite CW complexes and that
M
and
N
are free as
\mathbb{Z}
-modules.
The cross product gives rise to the following split short exact sequences of
(R_{1},R_{2})
-bimodules:

\displaystyle 0\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}(X_{1};M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}H_{n-i}(X_{2};N)\xrightarrow{\times}H_{n}(X_{1}\times X_{2};M\boxtimes N)\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{Tor}_{1}^{R}(H_{i}(X_{1};M),H_{n-i-1}(X_{2};N))\to 0

\displaystyle 0\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}H^{i}(X_{1};M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}H^{n-i}(X_{2};N)\xrightarrow{\times}H^{n}(X_{1}\times X_{2};M\boxtimes N)\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{R}(H^{i}(X_{1};M),H^{n-i-1}(X_{2};N))\to 0.

Proof.

In homology this follows by combining the Künneth theorem for chain complexes (which applies since both
M
and
N
are free as
\mathbb{Z}
-modules) and the definition of the cross product from Construction
A.11
:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigoplus\limits_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}(X_{1};M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}H_{n-i}(X_{2};N)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\times}
\textstyle{H_{n}\left(C_{*}(X;M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\overline{C_{*}(X_{2};N)}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\times}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\bigoplus\limits_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{Tor}_{1}^{R}(H_{i}(X;M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}H_{n-i-1}(X_{2};N)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{\underbrace{H_{n}\left(C_{*}(X_{1}\times X_{2};M\boxtimes N)\right).}_{=H_{*}(X_{1}\times X_{2};M\boxtimes N)}}

In cohomology this follows by combining the Künneth theorem for cochain complexes (which applies since both
M
and
N
are free as
R
-modules) and the definition of the cross product from Construction
A.11
:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigoplus\limits_{i=1}^{n}H^{i}(X_{1};M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}H^{n-i}(X_{2};N)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\times}
\textstyle{H_{n}\left(C^{*}(X_{1};M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\overline{C^{*}(X_{2};N)}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\times}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\bigoplus\limits_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{R}(H_{i}(X_{1};M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}H_{n-i-1}(X_{2};N)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{\underbrace{H_{n}\left(C^{*}(X_{1}\times X_{2};M\boxtimes N)\right).}_{=H^{*}(X_{1}\times X_{2};M\boxtimes N)}}

This concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

We conclude with a remark that was used during the proofs of Lemma
9.18
and Proposition
9.29
.

Remark A.14
.

Let
Y
be a space, let
\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi
be an epimorphism, and endow
Y\times S^{1}
with coefficient system
\pi_{1}(Y\times S^{1})\to\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi.

Under the identification
