H_{0}(Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\boxtimes\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H_{0}(S^{1})\xrightarrow{\times}H_{0}(Y\times S^{1};(\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\boxtimes\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\boxtimes\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{0}(Y\times S^{1},\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\boxtimes\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),

the following equality holds:

\operatorname{mult}_{0}([(\gamma_{1}\otimes\gamma_{2})\otimes[\operatorname{pt}]]\times n1_{H_{0}(S^{1})})=n\gamma_{1}\overline{\gamma}_{2}

for any
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Appendix B
3
-connected maps and isometries of intersection forms

The goal of this section is to prove Proposition
B.3
that was used during the proofs of Propositions
7.7
and
7.12
.
This requires the following lemma which describes the effect of applying duality to the absolute class instead of the relative class.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
(X,Y)
be a
4
-dimensional Poincaré pair with
\pi:=\pi_{1}(X)
.
The pairing
b_{X}^{\partial}
agrees with the pairing

\displaystyle H^{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle\mapsto\overline{\langle\alpha,\operatorname{PD}_{X}(\beta)\rangle}.

while the pairing
\lambda_{X}^{\partial}
agrees with the pairing

\displaystyle H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H_{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\overline{\langle\operatorname{PD}_{X}^{-1}(x),y\rangle}.

Proof.

The proof follows promptly from Lemmas
A.10
and
A.5
and
A.6

Indeed, by
Lemma

A.10
and
Lemma

A.5
,

b_{X}^{\partial}(\alpha,\beta)=\overline{\operatorname{mult}_{0}((\beta\cup\alpha)\cap[X])}=\operatorname{mult}_{0}((\alpha\cup\beta)\cap[X])=\operatorname{mult}_{0}(\alpha\cap(\beta\cap[X])).

By
Lemma

A.6
,

\operatorname{mult}_{0}(\alpha\cap(\beta\cap[X]))=\overline{\langle\alpha,\beta\cap[X]\rangle}=\overline{\langle\alpha,\operatorname{PD}_{X}(\beta)\rangle}.

This completes the proof for
b_{X}^{\partial}
. Since
\operatorname{PD}_{X}
is an isomorphism, there exist
\alpha,\beta
with
x=\operatorname{PD}_{X}(\alpha)
and
y=\operatorname{PD}_{X}(\beta)
. We then compute using
Remark

A.8
,

\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(x,y)=\lambda_{X}^{\partial}(\operatorname{PD}_{X}(\alpha),\operatorname{PD}_{X}(\beta))=b_{X}^{\partial}(\alpha,\beta).

The first part of the lemma now gives

b_{X}^{\partial}(\alpha,\beta)=\overline{\langle\alpha,\operatorname{PD}_{X}(\beta)\rangle}=\overline{\langle\operatorname{PD}_{X}^{-1}(x),y\rangle}.

This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Notation B.2
.

Let
(X_{0},Y_{0})
and
(X_{1},Y_{1})
be
4
-dimensional Poincaré pairs with
\pi_{1}(X_{j})\cong\pi
,
Postnikov
2
-type
B:=P_{2}(X_{1})
and
\iota_{j}\colon\pi_{1}(Y_{j})\to\pi_{1}(X_{j})
surjective for
j=0,1
.
Fix a homotopy equivalence
h\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
and assume there is a group isomorphism
u\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\pi_{1}(X_{1})
that satisfies
u\circ\iota_{0}=\iota_{1}\circ h_{*}
.
Let
c_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B,c_{1}\colon X_{1}\to B
be

3
-connected maps with
c_{1}
the inclusion.
For elements
x\in H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and
y\in H_{2}(B,Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, we write

(c_{i})_{*}^{-1}\lambda_{i}(x,y):=\lambda_{X_{i}}^{\partial}((c_{i})_{*}^{-1}(x),(c_{i})_{*}^{-1}(y)).

Proposition B.3
.

The following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

The pairings
(c_{0})_{*}^{-1}\lambda_{X_{0}}^{\partial},(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}\lambda_{X_{1}}^{\partial}\colon H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H_{2}(B,Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
are equal,

(2)

The pairings
c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial},c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}\colon H^{2}(B,Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\times H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
are equal.

Proof.

Consider the maps
H^{2}(B,Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}\pi)\to H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)
and
H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\to H_{2}(B,Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}\pi)
obtained by capping with
(c_{i})_{*}([X_{i}])
for
i=0,1
.
Denote both of these maps by
\operatorname{PD}_{cX_{i}}
.
Observe that
\operatorname{PD}_{cX_{i}}=(c_{i})_{*}\circ\operatorname{PD}_{X_{i}}\circ c_{i}^{*}
implies both that
\operatorname{PD}_{cX_{i}}
is an isomorphism for
i=0,1
and that the equality
(c_{0})_{*}^{-1}\lambda_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}\lambda_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
is equivalent to the following equality holding for all
x\in H_{2}(B,Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}\pi)
and
y\in H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)
:

\langle\operatorname{PD}_{cX_{0}}^{-1}(x),y\rangle=\langle\operatorname{PD}_{cX_{1}}^{-1}(x),y\rangle.

Since
\operatorname{PD}_{cX_{i}}
is an isomorphism, this is equivalent to the following holding for all
x^{\prime}\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)
and
y^{\prime}\in H^{2}(B,Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}\pi)
:

\overbrace{\langle\operatorname{PD}_{cX_{0}}^{-1}\circ\operatorname{PD}_{cX_{0}}(x^{\prime}),\operatorname{PD}_{cX_{1}}(y^{\prime})\rangle}^{=\langle x^{\prime},\operatorname{PD}_{cX_{1}}(y^{\prime})\rangle}=\langle\operatorname{PD}_{cX_{1}}^{-1}\circ\operatorname{PD}_{cX_{0}}(x^{\prime}),\operatorname{PD}_{cX_{1}}(y^{\prime})\rangle.

Using Lemma
B.1
twice, first for
c_{1}^{*}b_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
, then for
c_{0}^{*}b_{X_{0}}^{\partial}
, the right hand side can be rewritten as

\langle\operatorname{PD}_{cX_{1}}^{-1}\circ\operatorname{PD}_{cX_{0}}(x^{\prime}),\operatorname{PD}_{cX_{1}}(y^{\prime})\rangle=\overline{\langle y^{\prime},\operatorname{PD}_{cX_{0}}(x^{\prime})\rangle}=\langle x^{\prime},\operatorname{PD}_{cX_{0}}(y^{\prime})\rangle.

Thus
(c_{0})_{*}^{-1}\lambda_{X_{0}}^{\partial}=(c_{1})_{*}^{-1}\lambda_{X_{1}}^{\partial}
is equivalent to the following equality holding for all
x^{\prime}\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)
and
y^{\prime}\in H^{2}(B,Y_{1};\mathbb{Z}\pi)
:

\langle x^{\prime},\operatorname{PD}_{cX_{1}}(y^{\prime})\rangle=\langle x^{\prime},\operatorname{PD}_{cX_{0}}(y^{\prime})\rangle.

By definition, this is equivalent to
c_{0}^{*}b^{\partial}_{X_{0}}=c_{1}^{*}b^{\partial}_{X_{1}}
.
∎

Appendix C
Obstruction theoretic results

The goal of this section is to prove results in obstruction theory that were used in Sections
7
and
9
.
Whereas these these results can probably be obtained using
[
Bau77
]
, we prefer to give hands on proofs.
In what follows, given a CW pair
(X,Y)
, we write
j\colon Y\to X
for the inclusion.
Also, we write
f_{0}^{*}\pi_{2}(B)
for the abelian group
\pi_{2}(B)
considered as a
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X_{0})]
-module via
\gamma\cdot x:=f_{*}(\gamma)x.

Proposition C.1
.

Let
(X,Y)
and
(B,B^{\prime})
be pairs of spaces that are homotopy equivalent to CW pairs, and let
f_{0}\colon(X,Y)\to(B,B^{\prime})
be a map.
Assume that
\pi_{i}(B)=0
for
i>2
and set
\pi:=\pi_{1}(B)
.
Suppose furthermore that
Y\to X
is a cofibration.
For every class
[d]\in H^{2}(X,Y;f_{0}^{*}\pi_{2}(B))
, there exists a map
f_{1}\colon X\to B
satisfying
(f_{0})_{*}=(f_{1})_{*}
on
\pi_{1}
and
f_{0}|_{Y}=f_{1}|_{Y}
,
and making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(B,B^{\prime};\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{0})_{*}-(f_{1})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{0})_{*}-(f_{1})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ev}([d])}

Proof.

During this proof, all chain complexes and homology groups of
B
and
(B,B^{\prime})
(resp.
X
and
(X,Y)
) are taken with
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
coefficients (resp.
f_{0}^{*}\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
coefficients).
Also, a short verification (which uses that
Y\to X
is a cofibration) shows that the general case follows from the case where
(X,Y)
and
(B,B^{\prime})
are CW pairs and
f_{0}
is cellular.
We now assume this is the case and work with cellular chain complexes and cellular homology.

Let
C_{2}(X,Y)\xrightarrow{d}\ker d_{2}^{B}/\operatorname{im}d_{3}^{B}\cong\pi_{2}(B)
be a cocycle representing
[d]
.
Since
C_{2}(X,Y)
is free, we can pick a lift
\widehat{d}\colon C_{2}(X,Y)\to\ker d_{2}^{B}
of
d
.
Since
d
is a cocycle,
d\circ d_{3}^{X,Y}=0
. Hence since
C_{3}(X,Y)
is free, there exists a map
e\colon C_{3}(X,Y)\to C_{3}(B)
such that
d_{3}^{B}\circ e=\widehat{d}\circ d_{3}^{X,Y}
.

Let
\operatorname{proj}_{i}\colon C_{i}(X)\to C_{i}(X)/C_{i}(Y)=C_{i}(X,Y)
be the projection and consider the chain map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{3}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{3}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}(B).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{3}^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{0})_{3}+e\circ\operatorname{proj}_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{0})_{2}+\widehat{d}\circ\operatorname{proj}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{0})_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{0})_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{3}^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{B}}

Here, commutativity of the left square follows from the definition of
e
, while commutativity of the middle square follows since
\widehat{d}
takes values in
\ker d_{2}^{B}
.
Note for later use that this map induces a chain map
C_{*}(X,Y)\to C_{*}(B,B^{\prime})
.

The chain map agrees with
(f_{0})_{*}
on
C_{*}(Y)
,
C_{1}(X)
and
C_{0}(X)
.
By
[
Whi49a
, Theorem 16]
, the partial realisation
f_{0}|_{Y^{(3)}\cup X^{(1)}}\colon Y^{(3)}\cup X^{(1)}\to B
extends to a realisation
f_{1}^{\prime}\colon X^{(3)}\to B
of the chain map.
Since
\pi_{i}(B)=0
for
i>2
, we can extend
f_{1}^{\prime}
to a map
f_{1}\colon X\to B
with
f_{1}|_{Y\cup X^{(1)}}=f_{0}|_{Y\cup X^{(1)}}
.
By construction
(f_{1})_{*}\colon C_{2}(X)\to C_{2}(B)
is given by
(f_{0})_{2}+\widehat{d}\circ\operatorname{proj}_{2}
so that the map induced on absolute homology is given by
(f_{0})_{2}+\operatorname{ev}([d])\circ j_{*}

while the map on relative cohomology is given by
(f_{0})_{2}+j_{*}\circ\operatorname{ev}([d])
as needed.
The condition
f_{1}^{\prime}|_{Y\cup X^{(1)}}=f_{0}|_{Y\cup X^{(1)}}
ensures that
(f_{1})_{*}=(f_{0})_{*}
on fundamental groups.
∎

The following result generalises the commutativity of the upper triangle in Proposition
C.1
.

Theorem C.2
.

Let
(X,Y)
be a pair of spaces that is homotopy equivalent to a CW pair, and let
f_{0}\colon X\to B
be a
\pi_{1}
-isomorphism where
\pi_{i}(B)=0
for
i>2
.
Set
\pi:=\pi_{1}(B)
.
Given

\psi\colon H_{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),\penalty\

there exists a map
f_{1}\colon X\to B
satisfying
(f_{1})_{*}=(f_{0})_{*}
on
\pi_{1}
,
f_{1}|_{Y}=f_{0}|_{Y}
, and such that

(f_{0})_{*}-(f_{1})_{*}=\psi\circ j\colon H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\to H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).

Proof.

Consider Theorem
6.2
with
(X_{0},Y_{0})=(X,Y),(X_{1},Y_{1})=(B,B)
as well as
u=(f_{0})_{*},h=f_{0}|_{Y}
and
F=(f_{0})_{*}
.
Since
B
is
3
-coconnected, applying this result with
c_{1}=\operatorname{id}
then implies that
(f_{0})_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}}^{\nu_{0}})=f_{0}|_{Y}^{*}(k_{B,B}^{\nu_{B}})
for any choice of maps
\nu_{0}\colon X_{0}\to B\pi
and
\nu_{B}\colon B\to B\pi
.
As
j_{*}(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}}^{\nu_{0}})=0
(see e.g.
[
CK25
, Lemma 6.1]
), we deduce
((f_{0})_{*}-\psi\circ j_{*})(k_{X_{0},Y_{0}}^{\nu_{0}})=f_{0}|_{Y}^{*}(k_{B,B}^{\nu_{B}})
.
A second application of
Theorem
6.2
(but this time with
F=(f_{0})_{*}-\psi\circ j_{*}
) therefore ensures that there exists a map
f_{1}\colon X\to B
(which is denoted by
c_{0}
in that theorem) with
(f_{1})_{*}=(f_{0})_{*}-\psi\circ j_{*}
and
f_{1}|_{Y}\simeq f_{0}|_{Y}
.
Applying the homotopy extension theorem, we can assume
f_{1}|_{Y}=f_{0}|_{Y}
.
∎
